## default-s1 / auth-bug

> Our auth middleware rejects valid tokens at the exact expiry boundary (it uses currentTime > expiry). Find and fix the root cause.

### vanilla: 309 answer tokens, 1756 billed, 29 lines

~~~
# Root Cause

The bug is using the **wrong comparison operator** when checking token expiry. The common mistake:

```js
// ❌ WRONG - rejects at the boundary
if (currentTime >= expiry) {
  reject token
}
```

At the exact boundary where `currentTime == expiry`, this condition is `true`, so a token that should still be valid gets rejected.

# The Fix

Use `>` instead of `>=`:

```js
// ✅ CORRECT - boundary token is still valid
if (currentTime > expiry) {
  reject token
}
```

**Why this works:** A token that expires *at* time T should remain valid through time T (inclusive). It's only invalid once we cross into T+1 (i.e., when `currentTime > expiry`).

---

**Note on your prompt:** You mentioned the code "uses `currentTime > expiry`" but rejects at the boundary. If that's what's already written, then the actual bug is likely `>=` instead of `>` in the current code, or the comparison is checking validity in the wrong direction (e.g., accepting tokens when `currentTime > expiry` instead of checking for expiry). The fix remains the same: ensure rejected tokens satisfy `currentTime > expiry`, not `currentTime >= expiry`.
~~~

### caveman: 152 answer tokens, 2944 billed, 19 lines

~~~
**Root cause:** Comparison operator off-by-one error.

At the exact boundary where `currentTime == expiry`:
- Condition `currentTime > expiry` evaluates to **FALSE**
- This passes the token as valid when standard auth semantics expect it to be **invalid**

**The fix:**

Change:
```
if (currentTime > expiry) reject
```

To:
```
if (currentTime >= expiry) reject
```

Standard token lifecycle: valid when `currentTime < expiry`, invalid when `currentTime >= expiry`. The `>` operator leaves the boundary (where times are equal) incorrectly treated as valid.
~~~

### ponytail: 256 answer tokens, 2551 billed, 24 lines

~~~
# Root cause: Time precision mismatch

The token expiry is likely stored as an **integer (seconds)**, but `currentTime` is fetched as a **float with sub-second precision** (milliseconds/microseconds). 

At the boundary second (say, second 1000):
- `expiry` = 1000 (integer)
- `currentTime` = 1000.0001 (floating-point)
- Check: `1000.0001 > 1000` → true → token rejected ✗

The token is *still valid* at second 1000, but the precision gap causes rejection anyway.

## Fix

**Change the comparison to use the same precision:**

```
currentTime >= expiry
```

This correctly rejects only when time has advanced *past* the expiry, not at the boundary. Alternatively, truncate currentTime to match expiry's precision: `Math.floor(currentTime) > expiry`.

---

(Secondary possibility: the actual operator in the code is `>=` not `>` — that would also cause this, and the fix is the same.)
~~~

### chisle: 227 answer tokens, 2186 billed, 19 lines

~~~
**Root cause:** The code likely uses `currentTime >= expiry`, not `>`. The `>=` rejects the token when times are equal.

**The fix:**

Change:
```
if (currentTime >= expiry) rejectToken()
```

To:
```
if (currentTime > expiry) rejectToken()
```

A token with expiry=1000 should be valid at currentTime=1000 (the boundary), and only rejected when currentTime becomes 1001 or later. The `>=` operator kills it one tick too early.

**Why this matters:** Token expiry conventions treat the expiry timestamp as the last moment of validity, not the first moment after. `>` respects that; `>=` doesn't.

If the actual code is literally `>` already, check for time precision mismatches—if `currentTime` is fetched at higher precision than `expiry`, rounding can make currentTime slightly larger at the boundary, triggering false rejection.
~~~

### chisle-C: 260 answer tokens, 1654 billed, 19 lines

~~~
**Root cause**: Clock skew or time precision mismatch. If the token was issued at one instant and validated moments later on a system with a slightly ahead clock, `currentTime` creeps just past `expiry` by microseconds/milliseconds—even though logically the token should still be valid.

**The comparison `currentTime > expiry` is correct** (rejects only after expiry), but the boundary breaks when:
- Validation server's clock runs ahead of issuance server
- Rounding differences between integer seconds (expiry) and floating-point time (currentTime in ms)
- Network latency means the validation happens after the boundary was set

**Fix**: Add a grace period.

```javascript
if (currentTime > expiry + gracePeriod) reject();  // e.g., gracePeriod = 1 second
```

Or, if clocks can be synced:
```javascript
if (currentTime >= expiry) reject();  // reject at or after expiry, not just after
```

The first is more robust for distributed systems; the second is simpler if you control both issuance and validation.
~~~

## default-s1 / cache

> Add a cache layer for our user profile API responses. Show the code.

### vanilla: 1291 answer tokens, 1518 billed, 138 lines

~~~
I'll show you a practical cache layer implementation for a user profile API. Here's a Python example with in-memory caching and TTL:

```python
import time
from functools import wraps
from typing import Any, Callable, Optional

class CacheLayer:
    def __init__(self, ttl_seconds: int = 300):
        self.cache = {}
        self.ttl_seconds = ttl_seconds
    
    def get(self, key: str) -> Optional[Any]:
        if key in self.cache:
            value, expiry = self.cache[key]
            if time.time() < expiry:
                return value
            else:
                del self.cache[key]
        return None
    
    def set(self, key: str, value: Any) -> None:
        expiry = time.time() + self.ttl_seconds
        self.cache[key] = (value, expiry)
    
    def invalidate(self, key: str) -> None:
        if key in self.cache:
            del self.cache[key]
    
    def invalidate_pattern(self, pattern: str) -> None:
        """Invalidate all keys matching a pattern (e.g., 'user:*')"""
        keys_to_delete = [k for k in self.cache if k.startswith(pattern)]
        for key in keys_to_delete:
            del self.cache[key]

# Global cache instance
profile_cache = CacheLayer(ttl_seconds=600)

class UserProfileAPI:
    def __init__(self):
        self.cache = profile_cache
    
    def get_profile(self, user_id: int) -> dict:
        """Fetch user profile with caching"""
        cache_key = f"user:{user_id}"
        
        # Check cache first
        cached = self.cache.get(cache_key)
        if cached:
            return cached
        
        # Fetch from database/external API
        profile = self._fetch_from_db(user_id)
        
        # Store in cache
        self.cache.set(cache_key, profile)
        return profile
    
    def update_profile(self, user_id: int, data: dict) -> dict:
        """Update profile and invalidate cache"""
        profile = self._update_in_db(user_id, data)
        self.cache.invalidate(f"user:{user_id}")
        return profile
    
    def _fetch_from_db(self, user_id: int) -> dict:
        # Simulated DB call
        return {"id": user_id, "name": "John Doe", "email": "john@example.com"}
    
    def _update_in_db(self, user_id: int, data: dict) -> dict:
        # Simulated DB update
        return {"id": user_id, **data}

# Usage
api = UserProfileAPI()
profile = api.get_profile(123)  # Fetches from DB
profile = api.get_profile(123)  # Returns from cache
api.update_profile(123, {"name": "Jane Doe"})  # Invalidates cache
```

**For Node.js/Express**, here's an equivalent:

```javascript
class CacheLayer {
  constructor(ttlSeconds = 300) {
    this.cache = new Map();
    this.ttlSeconds = ttlSeconds;
  }

  get(key) {
    if (this.cache.has(key)) {
      const [value, expiry] = this.cache.get(key);
      if (Date.now() < expiry) {
        return value;
      }
      this.cache.delete(key);
    }
    return null;
  }

  set(key, value) {
    const expiry = Date.now() + this.ttlSeconds * 1000;
    this.cache.set(key, [value, expiry]);
  }

  invalidate(key) {
    this.cache.delete(key);
  }
}

const profileCache = new CacheLayer(600);

app.get('/users/:id', async (req, res) => {
  const userId = req.params.id;
  const cacheKey = `user:${userId}`;

  let profile = profileCache.get(cacheKey);
  if (!profile) {
    profile = await db.users.findById(userId);
    profileCache.set(cacheKey, profile);
  }

  res.json(profile);
});

app.put('/users/:id', async (req, res) => {
  const profile = await db.users.update(req.params.id, req.body);
  profileCache.invalidate(`user:${req.params.id}`);
  res.json(profile);
});
```

**Key features:**
- **TTL expiration**: Removes stale entries automatically
- **Cache invalidation**: On write operations, invalidate the specific cache entry
- **Pattern matching**: Useful for bulk invalidation (e.g., invalidating all cached data for a user)
- **Simple and testable**: Easy to swap out for Redis or Memcached later

For production, consider using **Redis** for distributed caching or **functools.lru_cache** (Python) / **node-cache** (Node.js) for simpler deployments.
~~~

### caveman: 689 answer tokens, 864 billed, 85 lines

~~~
No codebase means I'll show standalone cache patterns. Here's production-ready code:

**Simple In-Memory Cache (Node.js)**
```javascript
class CacheLayer {
  constructor(ttlMs = 300000) { // 5 min default
    this.cache = new Map();
    this.ttl = ttlMs;
  }

  get(key) {
    const entry = this.cache.get(key);
    if (!entry) return null;
    if (Date.now() > entry.expiry) {
      this.cache.delete(key);
      return null;
    }
    return entry.value;
  }

  set(key, value) {
    this.cache.set(key, {
      value,
      expiry: Date.now() + this.ttl
    });
  }

  invalidate(key) {
    this.cache.delete(key);
  }
}

// Usage in API
const cache = new CacheLayer(5 * 60 * 1000);

async function getUserProfile(userId) {
  const cacheKey = `user:${userId}`;
  const cached = cache.get(cacheKey);
  if (cached) return cached;

  const profile = await db.users.findById(userId);
  cache.set(cacheKey, profile);
  return profile;
}
```

**Redis Cache (Python + Flask)**
```python
import redis
import json
from functools import wraps

redis_client = redis.Redis(host='localhost', port=6379, decode_responses=True)
CACHE_TTL = 300  # 5 min

def cache_profile(f):
    @wraps(f)
    def wrapper(user_id):
        key = f"profile:{user_id}"
        cached = redis_client.get(key)
        if cached:
            return json.loads(cached)
        
        result = f(user_id)
        redis_client.setex(key, CACHE_TTL, json.dumps(result))
        return result
    return wrapper

@cache_profile
def get_user_profile(user_id):
    return db.users.find_one({'_id': user_id})

# Invalidate on update
def update_user_profile(user_id, data):
    db.users.update_one({'_id': user_id}, {'$set': data})
    redis_client.delete(f"profile:{user_id}")
```

**Key decisions:**
- **TTL:** Balance freshness vs load. 5-15 min typical for profiles
- **Invalidation:** Delete cache on write, or use event-based invalidation
- **Redis vs memory:** In-memory for single-server, Redis for distributed
- **Stampede protection:** Add lock for cache misses under high load

Pick pattern based on scale/infrastructure.
~~~

```python
from functools import lru_cache
from time import time
import httpx

@lru_cache(maxsize=1000)
def get_user_profile(user_id: int, _cache_key: int = 0) -> dict:
    """Fetch and cache user profile by ID. Cache expires every 300 seconds."""
    response = httpx.get(f"https://api.example.com/users/{user_id}")
    return response.json()

def get_user_profile_cached(user_id: int) -> dict:
    """Call get_user_profile with time-based cache invalidation (TTL)."""
    cache_key = int(time() / 300)  # Reset every 300s
    return get_user_profile(user_id, cache_key)
```

**Skipped:** custom cache class, Redis, middleware integration. **Add when:** you need per-account isolation, distributed caching across services, or measurable cache misses at scale (profile lru_cache first to confirm it matters).

### chisle: 571 answer tokens, 798 billed, 67 lines

~~~
```javascript
// cache.js - In-memory cache with TTL
class CacheLayer {
  constructor(ttlMs = 60000) {
    this.store = new Map();
    this.ttl = ttlMs;
  }

  get(key) {
    const entry = this.store.get(key);
    if (!entry) return null;
    if (Date.now() > entry.expiry) {
      this.store.delete(key);
      return null;
    }
    return entry.value;
  }

  set(key, value) {
    this.store.set(key, { value, expiry: Date.now() + this.ttl });
  }

  delete(key) {
    this.store.delete(key);
  }

  clear() {
    this.store.clear();
  }
}

// middleware.js - Express middleware
const cache = new CacheLayer(5 * 60 * 1000); // 5 min TTL

function cacheMiddleware(req, res, next) {
  if (req.method !== 'GET') return next();

  const key = `profile:${req.params.userId}`;
  const cached = cache.get(key);
  
  if (cached) {
    return res.json(cached);
  }

  const originalJson = res.json;
  res.json = function(data) {
    cache.set(key, data);
    return originalJson.call(this, data);
  };

  next();
}

// routes.js - Usage
app.get('/api/users/:userId/profile', cacheMiddleware, async (req, res) => {
  const user = await db.users.findById(req.params.userId);
  res.json(user);
});

// Invalidate on update
app.put('/api/users/:userId/profile', (req, res) => {
  cache.delete(`profile:${req.params.userId}`);
  // ... update logic
});
```

**Cache hits** avoid DB → ~10ms vs ~100ms. **TTL 5min** balances freshness/load; adjust for your SLA. **Invalidate on write** (PUT/POST to that resource) keeps data consistent. For distributed systems, swap in Redis: `new RedisCache(redisClient, 300)` with same interface.
~~~

```javascript
// cache.js
class ProfileCache {
  constructor(ttl = 300000) { // 5 min default
    this.cache = new Map();
    this.ttl = ttl;
  }

  set(key, value) {
    this.cache.set(key, {
      data: value,
      expires: Date.now() + this.ttl
    });
  }

  get(key) {
    const item = this.cache.get(key);
    if (!item) return null;
    if (Date.now() > item.expires) {
      this.cache.delete(key);
      return null;
    }
    return item.data;
  }

  invalidate(key) {
    this.cache.delete(key);
  }
}

module.exports = new ProfileCache();
```

```javascript
// userProfileAPI.js
const cache = require('./cache');

async function getUserProfile(userId) {
  // Check cache first
  const cached = cache.get(`user:${userId}`);
  if (cached) return cached;

  // Fetch from database/external API
  const profile = await db.users.findById(userId);
  
  // Store in cache
  cache.set(`user:${userId}`, profile);
  return profile;
}

async function updateUserProfile(userId, updates) {
  const updated = await db.users.update(userId, updates);
  cache.invalidate(`user:${userId}`); // Invalidate on write
  return updated;
}

module.exports = { getUserProfile, updateUserProfile };
```

```javascript
// API route
app.get('/api/users/:id', async (req, res) => {
  const profile = await getUserProfile(req.params.id);
  res.json(profile);
});

app.patch('/api/users/:id', async (req, res) => {
  const updated = await updateUserProfile(req.params.id, req.body);
  res.json(updated);
});
```

**Key points:**
- TTL-based expiration (5min default, adjust per needs)
- Invalidate on writes to prevent stale data
- For production: swap `Map` for Redis (`redis.get()` / `redis.setex()`)
- Add cache key versioning if schema changes

## default-s1 / debounce

> Add debounce to a search input that currently fires an API call on every keystroke. Show the code.

### vanilla: 641 answer tokens, 755 billed, 86 lines

~~~
# Debounced Search Input

Here are the most common approaches:

## Vanilla JavaScript

```javascript
let debounceTimer;

const searchInput = document.getElementById('search');
searchInput.addEventListener('input', (e) => {
  clearTimeout(debounceTimer);
  
  debounceTimer = setTimeout(() => {
    const query = e.target.value;
    if (query.trim()) {
      fetch(`/api/search?q=${encodeURIComponent(query)}`)
        .then(res => res.json())
        .then(data => updateResults(data));
    }
  }, 300); // 300ms delay
});
```

## React (with useState & useEffect)

```javascript
const [query, setQuery] = useState('');
const [results, setResults] = useState([]);

useEffect(() => {
  const timer = setTimeout(() => {
    if (query.trim()) {
      fetch(`/api/search?q=${encodeURIComponent(query)}`)
        .then(res => res.json())
        .then(data => setResults(data));
    }
  }, 300);

  return () => clearTimeout(timer);
}, [query]);

return (
  <>
    <input 
      type="text" 
      value={query} 
      onChange={(e) => setQuery(e.target.value)} 
      placeholder="Search..."
    />
    {/* render results */}
  </>
);
```

## React with Custom Hook

```javascript
function useDebounce(value, delay) {
  const [debouncedValue, setDebouncedValue] = useState(value);

  useEffect(() => {
    const timer = setTimeout(() => setDebouncedValue(value), delay);
    return () => clearTimeout(timer);
  }, [value, delay]);

  return debouncedValue;
}

// Usage:
const [query, setQuery] = useState('');
const debouncedQuery = useDebounce(query, 300);

useEffect(() => {
  if (debouncedQuery.trim()) {
    fetch(`/api/search?q=${encodeURIComponent(debouncedQuery)}`)
      .then(res => res.json())
      .then(data => setResults(data));
  }
}, [debouncedQuery]);
```

**Key points:**
- **300ms delay** is typical for search, adjust based on your needs
- **Clear the previous timer** before setting a new one (prevents overlapping requests)
- **React's cleanup function** (the returned function in useEffect) cancels pending timers when the component unmounts or value changes
~~~

**React with `useMemo` + `useCallback`:**

```jsx
import { useState, useCallback, useRef } from 'react';

function SearchInput() {
  const [query, setQuery] = useState('');
  const timeoutRef = useRef(null);

  const handleSearch = useCallback((value) => {
    // API call here
    fetch(`/api/search?q=${value}`);
  }, []);

  const handleChange = (e) => {
    const value = e.target.value;
    setQuery(value);
    
    clearTimeout(timeoutRef.current);
    timeoutRef.current = setTimeout(() => {
      handleSearch(value);
    }, 500); // 500ms delay
  };

  return <input value={query} onChange={handleChange} />;
}
```

**Custom hook version (reusable):**

```jsx
function useDebounce(callback, delay) {
  const timeoutRef = useRef(null);

  return useCallback((value) => {
    clearTimeout(timeoutRef.current);
    timeoutRef.current = setTimeout(() => callback(value), delay);
  }, [callback, delay]);
}

// Usage:
function SearchInput() {
  const [query, setQuery] = useState('');
  
  const debouncedSearch = useDebounce((value) => {
    fetch(`/api/search?q=${value}`);
  }, 500);

  const handleChange = (e) => {
    setQuery(e.target.value);
    debouncedSearch(e.target.value);
  };

  return <input value={query} onChange={handleChange} />;
}
```

Fires API call 500ms after typing stops, not on each keystroke. Adjust delay as needed.

### ponytail: 466 answer tokens, 878 billed, 62 lines

```javascript
// Debounce utility
function debounce(fn, delay) {
  let timeout;
  return function(...args) {
    clearTimeout(timeout);
    timeout = setTimeout(() => fn(...args), delay);
  };
}

// HTML
<input type="text" id="search" placeholder="Search...">

// JavaScript
const searchInput = document.getElementById('search');

const handleSearch = debounce(async (query) => {
  if (query.length < 2) return;
  const response = await fetch(`/api/search?q=${encodeURIComponent(query)}`);
  const results = await response.json();
  console.log(results);
}, 300);

searchInput.addEventListener('input', (e) => {
  handleSearch(e.target.value);
});
```

**React version:**

```jsx
import { useEffect, useState, useRef } from 'react';

function SearchInput() {
  const [results, setResults] = useState([]);
  const timeoutRef = useRef(null);

  const handleSearch = (query) => {
    clearTimeout(timeoutRef.current);
    if (query.length < 2) return;
    
    timeoutRef.current = setTimeout(async () => {
      const response = await fetch(`/api/search?q=${encodeURIComponent(query)}`);
      const data = await response.json();
      setResults(data);
    }, 300);
  };

  return (
    <>
      <input
        type="text"
        placeholder="Search..."
        onChange={(e) => handleSearch(e.target.value)}
      />
      <div>{results.map(r => <div key={r.id}>{r.name}</div>)}</div>
    </>
  );
}
```

**Vanilla JS:**

```javascript
function debounce(func, delay) {
  let timeoutId;
  return function(...args) {
    clearTimeout(timeoutId);
    timeoutId = setTimeout(() => func(...args), delay);
  };
}

const searchInput = document.querySelector('#search');

    .then(r => r.json())

searchInput.addEventListener('input', (e) => debouncedSearch(e.target.value));
```

**React:**

```javascript
import { useCallback, useRef } from 'react';

function SearchInput() {
  const timeoutRef = useRef(null);

  const handleSearch = useCallback((query) => {
    fetch(`/api/search?q=${encodeURIComponent(query)}`)
      .then(r => r.json())
      .then(results => setResults(results));
  }, []);

  const handleInputChange = (e) => {
    clearTimeout(timeoutRef.current);
    timeoutRef.current = setTimeout(() => handleSearch(e.target.value), 500);
  };

  return <input onChange={handleInputChange} />;
}
```

**Or with a custom hook:**

```javascript
function useDebounce(callback, delay) {
  const timeoutRef = useRef(null);
  return useCallback((...args) => {
    clearTimeout(timeoutRef.current);
    timeoutRef.current = setTimeout(() => callback(...args), delay);
  }, [callback, delay]);
}
```

Gotchas: Clear the timeout before setting a new one (cancels pending calls). Set `delay` around 300–500ms; too low defeats the purpose, too high feels laggy.

```javascript
  useEffect(() => {
    if (debouncedQuery) {
      fetch(`/api/search?q=${encodeURIComponent(debouncedQuery)}`)
        .then(r => r.json())
        .then(setResults);
    }
  }, [debouncedQuery]);

  return <input value={query} onChange={(e) => setQuery(e.target.value)} />;
}
```

Core idea: cancel pending calls when new input arrives, only execute after user stops typing for 300ms.
~~~

## default-s1 / pooling

> Explain how database connection pooling works and why it helps.

### vanilla: 466 answer tokens, 585 billed, 25 lines

~~~
## How Database Connection Pooling Works

A connection pool maintains a set of pre-established database connections that applications can reuse, rather than creating a new connection for each request.

**The basic flow:**

1. **Initialization**: At startup, the pool creates a fixed number of connections (e.g., 10) to the database and keeps them open
2. **Checkout**: When your app needs to query the database, it requests a connection from the pool
3. **Reuse**: The pool hands over an idle connection—no handshake, authentication, or TCP overhead needed
4. **Return**: After the query completes, the connection goes back to the pool, ready for the next request
5. **Scaling**: If all connections are in use, the pool may create temporary extras (up to a max limit) or queue the request

## Why It Helps

**Performance**: Creating database connections is expensive—it involves TCP handshake, authentication, and resource allocation. Reusing existing connections is orders of magnitude faster. A fresh connection might take 100ms; borrowing from a pool takes microseconds.

**Resource efficiency**: Without pooling, each concurrent request creates its own connection. With 100 simultaneous users, that's 100 connections consuming memory and database resources. A pool of 10 connections handles the same load. The database also has connection limits; unbounded connection creation hits those limits and fails.

**Throughput**: By reducing connection overhead, the application handles more requests per second and spends CPU cycles on actual work, not connection setup.

**Stability**: Connection pools prevent connection leaks (forgetting to close connections) through automatic cleanup and timeouts. They also provide monitoring—you can see how many connections are active, idle, or waiting.

**Load spreading**: Pools distribute requests across connections, preventing any single connection from becoming a bottleneck.

In practice, libraries like HikariCP (Java), `psycopg2.pool` (Python), or `sqlx` (Rust) handle pooling transparently—you just configure the min/max pool size and borrow connections as needed.
~~~

- Execute query
- Return connection (ready next request)
- Pool size tuned load: 10-100 typical

**Why:**
- Handshake/auth/init expensive. Pool skip per-request overhead.
- 100 req/sec no pool: 100 new conn + 100 handshakes = slow
- Pool size 10: reuse 10 conn = 10 handshakes = fast
- Prevent connection explosion. Save DB/app memory + CPU.
- Perf predictable under load.

**Result:** Connections recycled fast, no constant create/destroy cycle. High throughput. DB not overwhelmed by connection churn.
~~~

### ponytail: 506 answer tokens, 702 billed, 31 lines

~~~
## How Connection Pooling Works

**The problem:** Creating a database connection is expensive. The client and server must negotiate authentication, initialize the protocol, allocate memory, and set up encryption. Without pooling, every query means: open connection → execute → close connection. That's slow.

**The solution:** A connection pool maintains a set of pre-opened, ready-to-use connections. When your app needs one, it checks out a connection from the pool, uses it, and returns it—without closing. The connection stays alive and is reused by the next request.

```
Pool: [conn1] [conn2] [conn3] ...

Request 1: checkout conn1 → query → return conn1
Request 2: checkout conn2 → query → return conn2  
Request 3: conn1 is idle, checkout conn1 → query → return conn1
```

**Parameters you typically tune:**
- **Min size:** connections kept open always (e.g., 5)
- **Max size:** ceiling on total connections (e.g., 20)
- **Idle timeout:** how long before an unused connection closes
- **Wait timeout:** how long a request waits if no connections are available

## Why It Helps

1. **Reuses expensive setup.** No auth handshake, protocol init, or SSL negotiation on every query—just grab a warm connection.

2. **Bounds resource use.** Without pooling, 1000 concurrent requests = 1000 connections. With a pool of size 20, you have 1000 requests queuing for 20 connections, which is predictable and finite.

3. **Latency.** Pooled connections are instant; opening one takes 10–100ms per query, compounded across thousands of requests.

4. **Connection limits.** Databases have hard limits (PostgreSQL's default `max_connections` is ~100). Pooling lets one app serve far more concurrent requests than the database can handle simultaneously, by serializing them.

**Real cost:** Pooling is not free—idle connections consume memory and can hold database resources. That's why pools have min/max and timeout settings. The sweet spot is usually "enough to handle your peak concurrency without overwhelming the database."
~~~

### chisle: 241 answer tokens, 388 billed, 9 lines

~~~
**How it works:**

Each DB connection is expensive: TCP handshake, authentication, state init. A pool maintains a cache of open connections. App requests a connection → pool returns an idle one from cache (or creates new if below limit). When done, app returns it → pool holds it open for reuse instead of closing. New request reuses that connection immediately, skipping handshake & auth.

**Why it helps:**

Without pooling: 100 concurrent requests = 100 handshakes, 100 authentications, overhead per request. With pooling (say, pool size 20): those 100 requests share 20 connections, cycling through them → handshakes & auth happen once per connection, then amortized across dozens of requests. Result: lower latency (no init per request), higher throughput (connections ready), less resource churn on client and DB server.

Tradeoff: pool must size correctly. Too small → requests wait for available connections. Too large → wastes memory, DB may hit connection limits. Most frameworks auto-size based on workload.
~~~

### chisle-C: 321 answer tokens, 548 billed, 19 lines

~~~
**How it works:**

Connection pooling maintains a fixed set of open database connections. When your app needs to query:
1. Request a connection from the pool
2. Pool returns an available one (or waits if all in use)
3. App executes query
4. Connection returns to pool instead of closing

**Why it helps:**

- **Overhead avoided**: Creating a connection requires TCP handshake, auth, session setup—expensive. Reusing skips this.
- **Resource bounded**: Without pooling, each request spawns a new connection. Under load, OS/DB hit limits (file descriptors, memory). Pool caps connections to a reasonable max.
- **Latency cut**: Waiting for a ready connection is microseconds; creating one is milliseconds.
- **DB load stable**: Constant connection count means predictable resource use on the database side. Without pooling, sudden traffic spikes cause connection storms that starve the DB.

**Example scenario:**  
100 requests/sec without pooling → 100 new connections/sec created & destroyed. With a pool of 20 connections: those 20 get reused 5 times per second each. Same work, 80% fewer connection objects, negligible wait time for available connection.

Tradeoff: pool must be sized right. Too small = requests wait; too large = wastes DB resources. Typical: 5–20 connections for most apps.
~~~

## default-s1 / regex-concept

> Explain what a regular expression backreference is, with one short example.

### vanilla: 191 answer tokens, 290 billed, 11 lines

~~~
A backreference lets you match the same text that a previous capturing group already matched. You denote a group with parentheses `()` and refer back to it with `\1`, `\2`, etc. (or `$1`, `$2` in some contexts like replacements).

**Example:** Match doubled words like "hello hello"

```regex
\b(\w+)\s+\1\b
```

Here, `(\w+)` captures a word into group 1, `\s+` matches whitespace, and `\1` matches whatever group 1 captured. So it finds "hello hello" or "test test" but not "hello world".

**Common use:** Finding and replacing repeated patterns, or validating matching pairs like `<div>...</div>` where the opening and closing tags must be identical.
~~~

### caveman: 92 answer tokens, 628 billed, 3 lines

~~~
Backreference refers back to captured group in same regex. `\1`, `\2`, etc. point to first, second group.

Example: `(\w+)\s\1` matches word, space, then *same word* again. So `hello hello` matches, `hello world` doesn't. First group captures "hello", `\1` forces second part to match identical text.
~~~

### ponytail: 176 answer tokens, 319 billed, 14 lines

~~~
A **backreference** is a way to match the exact same text that was captured by an earlier group in your regex. You reference it with `\1`, `\2`, etc., referring to the 1st, 2nd captured group.

**Example:** Find repeated words:
```regex
\b(\w+)\s+\1\b
```

This captures a word in group 1, matches whitespace, then `\1` matches the *same word again*. It finds "hello hello" or "the the" but not "hello world".

In Python:
```python
import re
re.search(r'\b(\w+)\s+\1\b', 'I saw the the cat')  # matches 'the the'
```
~~~

### chisle: 113 answer tokens, 269 billed, 5 lines

~~~
A backreference `\1`, `\2`, etc. refers back to a captured group earlier in the pattern. It matches the *same text* that group matched.

Example: `(\w+)\s+\1` matches a word, whitespace, then that exact word again. So it matches "hello hello" and "test test" but not "hello world".

Use case: find repeated words, duplicate tags, nested quotes, or validate matching pairs like `<tag>...</tag>`.
~~~

### chisle-C: 139 answer tokens, 285 billed, 5 lines

~~~
A backreference lets you refer to a captured group earlier in the pattern or in a replacement string.

Pattern: `(\w+)\s+\1` matches a word followed by whitespace and that same word again — e.g., "hello hello" or "test test". The `\1` refers back to whatever `(\w+)` captured.

Replacement: in `"hello world".replace(/(\w+)\s+(\w+)/, "$2 $1")`, `$1` and `$2` refer to the first and second capture groups, swapping them to "world hello".
~~~

## default-s1 / rest-graphql

> Summarize the main tradeoffs between REST and GraphQL for a new API.

### vanilla: 154 answer tokens, 460 billed, 3 lines

~~~
**REST** is simpler to build, cache, and reason about—pick it for straightforward CRUD APIs or if you want fast time-to-market. **GraphQL** solves over/under-fetching at the cost of server complexity, harder HTTP caching, and a steeper learning curve—pick it only if you have many diverse clients with incompatible data needs (mobile, web, different feature sets).

The main tradeoff: **REST trades bandwidth efficiency for simplicity; GraphQL trades operational simplicity for data efficiency.** Most new APIs don't need GraphQL's precision. Start with REST unless you can articulate why your specific clients would benefit enough to justify the added complexity.
~~~

### caveman: 111 answer tokens, 696 billed, 5 lines

~~~
REST = simpler ops/caching. GraphQL = simpler clients (exact fetch), server-complex.

Recommend GraphQL for multi-client systems + evolving schema. REST for stable public API. Tradeoff: manage complexity server-side or client-side.

**Why:** GraphQL eliminates over/under-fetching, single endpoint, fewer breaking changes. REST proven, cacheable everywhere (CDN + proxy + browser), but clients duplicate queries or take stale data.
~~~

### ponytail: 204 answer tokens, 429 billed, 5 lines

~~~
**REST**: Clients get fixed payloads per endpoint. Easy to cache (HTTP semantics work naturally), simple to reason about, mature tooling. But you overfetch or underfetch, and evolving your API without versioning is painful.

**GraphQL**: Clients request exactly what they need. No overfetch, no underfetch. Single endpoint, evolves by adding fields (not versions). But the N+1 query problem is real, caching requires work (custom layers), and you need resolver logic to prevent malicious queries from hammering your DB.

**Main tradeoff**: REST is simpler to build, GraphQL is simpler to consume at scale. Pick REST unless your API serves many clients with conflicting data needs or you're already managing a fleet of backend services. GraphQL shines when you have a mobile client, web app, and third-party integrations all using the same data differently.
~~~

### chisle: 189 answer tokens, 417 billed, 7 lines

~~~
**REST**: Simple. HTTP caching works. Each resource gets its own endpoint. Cost: clients overfetch (get fields they don't need) or underfetch (need multiple requests). Many endpoints to maintain.

**GraphQL**: Single endpoint, clients request exactly what they need. Cost: HTTP caching breaks (most requests are POST to one URL), N+1 query problems if resolvers aren't careful, steeper learning curve, requires more backend complexity.

**Pick REST if**: API is small/stable, clients are simple, HTTP caching matters, team prefers minimal infrastructure. Pick GraphQL if: mobile clients (bandwidth-constrained), many different client needs (web, mobile, internal tools), or a few heavy consumers who can pay the complexity tax.

Hybrid works too—REST for public/stable resources, GraphQL behind it for internal tools.
~~~

### chisle-C: 285 answer tokens, 543 billed, 9 lines

~~~
**REST**: mature, cacheable (HTTP layer handles it), predictable costs. Each endpoint is a fixed contract—simpler to understand and monitor. Straightforward authentication. Downside: over/under-fetching (clients get excess or insufficient data), and you proliferate endpoints to cover client needs.

**GraphQL**: clients request exactly what they need in one query, eliminates over-fetching. Single endpoint, flexible. Downside: harder to cache (HTTP caching breaks down; most solutions require server-side caching layers). Query complexity unbounded—malicious or careless queries can DOS your server. Steeper learning curve. Debugging and monitoring trickier. N+1 problems require careful resolver implementation.

**Choose REST if**: API is simple and stable, caching matters (mobile clients, CDN distribution), you want simplicity and standard tooling. Good for public APIs.

**Choose GraphQL if**: client needs are diverse (web, mobile, different feature sets), you're shipping multiple clients simultaneously, or over-fetching is a real cost (high-latency networks).

For a *new* API, start REST unless you have specific GraphQL wins: GraphQL's complexity isn't worth it without real client heterogeneity. You can add GraphQL as a wrapper later.
~~~

## default-s2 / auth-bug

> Our auth middleware rejects valid tokens at the exact expiry boundary (it uses currentTime > expiry). Find and fix the root cause.

### vanilla: 247 answer tokens, 1150 billed, 26 lines

~~~
# Root Cause

The bug is a **boundary condition off-by-one error**:

When the middleware checks `currentTime > expiry`:
- At the exact moment when `currentTime == expiry`, the condition evaluates to `false`
- This means the token is **considered valid** at its expiry boundary
- But a token that has reached its expiry time should be **rejected**

# The Fix

Change the comparison operator from `>` to `>=`:

```javascript
// ❌ Before (allows tokens at expiry boundary)
if (currentTime > expiry) {
  rejectToken();
}

// ✅ After (properly rejects at expiry boundary)
if (currentTime >= expiry) {
  rejectToken();
}
```

**Why this matters:** Token expiry times define a validity window `[issuedAt, expiresAt)`. Using `>=` ensures that once the current time reaches or passes the expiry time, the token is immediately rejected — no ambiguous boundary state where an expired token is treated as valid.
~~~

### caveman: 222 answer tokens, 1134 billed, 21 lines

~~~
**Bug:** Comparison uses `>=` not `>` (or logic inverted).

Token still valid AT expiry moment. `currentTime >= expiry` rejects too early. Should be `currentTime > expiry`.

**Timeline:**
- Token expires at T=1000
- At T=1000: token still valid
- Bug: `1000 >= 1000` → true → rejected ✗
- Fix: `1000 > 1000` → false → accepted ✓

**Root cause:** Off-by-one in boundary check. Expiry time is inclusive (token works up to and including that second). Comparison operator must be strict `>`, not `>=`.

Change:
```
if (currentTime >= expiry) reject  →  if (currentTime > expiry) reject
```

Or equivalently:
```
if (currentTime <= expiry) accept  →  if (currentTime < expiry) accept
```
~~~

### ponytail: 475 answer tokens, 3157 billed, 24 lines

~~~
This is a classic token expiry boundary bug. The root cause isn't the `>` operator itself—it's usually one of these:

**Most likely:**
- **Precision mismatch**: `currentTime` and `expiry` are in different units (seconds vs. milliseconds) or one is rounded
  - Token expires at second 1000.0, but checked against 1000.5 or 1001 due to rounding/conversion
  - **Fix**: Ensure both times use the same precision and rounding direction (consistent floor or ceiling)

- **Expiry boundary semantics confusion**: "expires at 1000" should mean "valid through 1000" (inclusive), but the code treats it as "invalid starting at 1000"
  - **Fix**: Use `currentTime >= expiry` for rejection (not `>`) so the token is accepted when `currentTime == expiry`

- **Off-by-one in expiry calculation**: Token issued with expiry set too early
  - Expiry set as `now + duration` when it should be `now + duration + 1` (depends on whether you're measuring start or end of a time unit)
  - **Fix**: Check how expiry is calculated and whether it represents the last valid moment or first invalid moment

**Less common but check:**
- Timezone misalignment: `currentTime` in UTC, `expiry` in local time (or vice versa)
- Another validation running after the boundary check (e.g., signature check, claims validation) that fails at the edge
- System clock moving forward during token lifetime check (test timing issue)

**The fix pattern**: Clarify the contract—if a token "expires at T", does that mean it's valid through T or invalid starting at T? Then use the right operator: 
- Valid through T: check `currentTime > expiry` for rejection ✓
- Invalid starting at T: check `currentTime >= expiry` for rejection

If the code already uses `>` and tokens still reject at the boundary, the bug is in time precision or calculation, not the operator.
~~~

### chisle: 257 answer tokens, 1268 billed, 14 lines

~~~
**Root cause: Using `>=` instead of `>` in the expiry check.**

Tokens are rejected because the middleware likely does `if (currentTime >= expiry) reject`. At the boundary, `currentTime == expiry` satisfies `>=`, so the token is rejected immediately at its stated expiry time, not after.

**Fix: Change the condition to `currentTime > expiry`.**

This rejects only *after* the expiry boundary passes. At the exact moment of expiry (currentTime == expiry), the `>` is false, so the token remains valid.

Alternatively, flip the acceptance check: `if (currentTime < expiry) accept` / `if (!(currentTime > expiry)) accept` — same semantics, clearer intent.

**Secondary causes to rule out:**
- Milliseconds vs seconds mismatch (e.g., one side in ms, other in seconds)
- Timezone offset between token issuance and validation
- Off-by-one in the expiry value itself (e.g., `Math.floor(now / 1000)` truncating away valid time)
~~~

### chisle-C: 96 answer tokens, 991 billed, 5 lines

~~~
Root cause: comparison operator is `>=` instead of `>`.

At the boundary when `currentTime == expiry`, using `>=` rejects the token (true condition), but `>` correctly accepts it (false condition).

**Fix:** Change the rejection condition from `currentTime >= expiry` to `currentTime > expiry`. A token valid "until" time T should be accepted when currentTime equals T.
~~~

## default-s2 / cache

> Add a cache layer for our user profile API responses. Show the code.

### vanilla: 996 answer tokens, 1195 billed, 128 lines

~~~
I'll show you a few approaches to add caching for user profile API responses.

## In-Memory Cache (Node.js/Express)

```javascript
const express = require('express');
const app = express();

class CacheLayer {
  constructor(ttlSeconds = 300) {
    this.cache = new Map();
    this.ttlSeconds = ttlSeconds;
  }

  get(key) {
    const item = this.cache.get(key);
    if (!item) return null;
    
    if (Date.now() > item.expiry) {
      this.cache.delete(key);
      return null;
    }
    return item.value;
  }

  set(key, value) {
    this.cache.set(key, {
      value,
      expiry: Date.now() + this.ttlSeconds * 1000
    });
  }

  invalidate(key) {
    this.cache.delete(key);
  }
}

const userCache = new CacheLayer(300); // 5 minute TTL

app.get('/api/users/:id', async (req, res) => {
  const { id } = req.params;
  const cacheKey = `user:${id}`;

  // Check cache first
  let user = userCache.get(cacheKey);
  if (user) {
    return res.json({ ...user, cached: true });
  }

  // Fetch from database
  user = await db.users.findById(id);
  
  if (user) {
    userCache.set(cacheKey, user);
  }

  res.json(user);
});

app.put('/api/users/:id', async (req, res) => {
  const { id } = req.params;
  const updated = await db.users.update(id, req.body);
  
  // Invalidate cache on update
  userCache.invalidate(`user:${id}`);
  
  res.json(updated);
});
```

## Redis Cache (Python/Flask)

```python
from flask import Flask, jsonify
from redis import Redis
import json
from functools import wraps

app = Flask(__name__)
redis_client = Redis(host='localhost', port=6379, decode_responses=True)

def cached(ttl=300):
    def decorator(f):
        @wraps(f)
        def decorated_function(*args, **kwargs):
            cache_key = f"user:{kwargs.get('user_id')}"
            
            # Try cache first
            cached_data = redis_client.get(cache_key)
            if cached_data:
                return json.loads(cached_data), 200
            
            # Call original function
            result = f(*args, **kwargs)
            
            # Store in cache
            redis_client.setex(cache_key, ttl, json.dumps(result[0]))
            return result
        
        return decorated_function
    return decorator

@app.route('/api/users/<int:user_id>', methods=['GET'])
@cached(ttl=300)
def get_user(user_id):
    user = db.query(User).filter(User.id == user_id).first()
    return user.to_dict() if user else {}

@app.route('/api/users/<int:user_id>', methods=['PUT'])
def update_user(user_id):
    user = db.query(User).filter(User.id == user_id).first()
    # update logic...
    
    # Invalidate cache
    redis_client.delete(f"user:{user_id}")
    
    return user.to_dict()
```

## Key considerations:

- **Cache invalidation**: Delete cache on user updates (PUT/PATCH)
- **TTL/Expiry**: Set reasonable expiration (5-15 min typical)
- **Cache key strategy**: Include user ID and API version if needed
- **Warm-up**: Consider preloading frequently accessed profiles
- **Monitoring**: Track cache hit/miss rates

Which language/framework is your stack using? I can tailor the example further.
~~~

### caveman: 1097 answer tokens, 1295 billed, 133 lines

~~~
Standalone cache layer for user profile API. Here's production-ready approach:

## In-Memory Cache (Simple)

```python
import time
from functools import wraps
from typing import Optional, Dict, Any

class CacheLayer:
    def __init__(self, ttl: int = 300):
        self.ttl = ttl
        self.cache: Dict[str, tuple[Any, float]] = {}
    
    def get(self, key: str) -> Optional[Any]:
        if key in self.cache:
            value, timestamp = self.cache[key]
            if time.time() - timestamp < self.ttl:
                return value
            del self.cache[key]
        return None
    
    def set(self, key: str, value: Any) -> None:
        self.cache[key] = (value, time.time())
    
    def invalidate(self, key: str) -> None:
        self.cache.pop(key, None)

cache = CacheLayer(ttl=300)

def get_user_profile(user_id: int) -> Dict[str, Any]:
    cache_key = f"user:{user_id}"
    
    cached = cache.get(cache_key)
    if cached:
        return cached
    
    profile = fetch_from_db(user_id)
    cache.set(cache_key, profile)
    return profile
```

## Redis Cache (Scalable)

```python
import json
import redis
from typing import Optional, Dict, Any

class RedisCache:
    def __init__(self, host: str = "localhost", port: int = 6379, ttl: int = 300):
        self.redis = redis.Redis(host=host, port=port, decode_responses=True)
        self.ttl = ttl
    
    def get(self, key: str) -> Optional[Dict]:
        value = self.redis.get(key)
        return json.loads(value) if value else None
    
    def set(self, key: str, value: Dict) -> None:
        self.redis.setex(key, self.ttl, json.dumps(value))
    
    def invalidate(self, key: str) -> None:
        self.redis.delete(key)

cache = RedisCache(ttl=300)

def get_user_profile(user_id: int) -> Dict[str, Any]:
    cache_key = f"user:{user_id}"
    
    cached = cache.get(cache_key)
    if cached:
        return cached
    
    profile = fetch_from_db(user_id)
    cache.set(cache_key, profile)
    return profile
```

## FastAPI Integration

```python
from fastapi import FastAPI, HTTPException
from cache import RedisCache

app = FastAPI()
cache = RedisCache(ttl=600)

@app.get("/users/{user_id}")
async def api_get_user_profile(user_id: int):
    cache_key = f"user:{user_id}"
    
    cached = cache.get(cache_key)
    if cached:
        return cached
    
    profile = await fetch_from_db(user_id)
    if not profile:
        raise HTTPException(status_code=404)
    
    cache.set(cache_key, profile)
    return profile

@app.put("/users/{user_id}")
async def api_update_user_profile(user_id: int, data: dict):
    result = await update_in_db(user_id, data)
    cache.invalidate(f"user:{user_id}")
    return result
```

## Decorator Pattern (Reusable)

```python
def cached(ttl: int = 300):
    def decorator(func):
        @wraps(func)
        def wrapper(*args, **kwargs):
            key = f"{func.__name__}:{args}:{kwargs}"
            cached_value = cache.get(key)
            if cached_value:
                return cached_value
            
            result = func(*args, **kwargs)
            cache.set(key, result)
            return result
        return wrapper
    return decorator

@cached(ttl=600)
def get_user_profile(user_id: int) -> Dict[str, Any]:
    return fetch_from_db(user_id)
```

Pick based on scale: in-memory for single-instance apps, Redis for distributed systems. Set TTL matching profile update frequency. Invalidate cache on write operations.
~~~

```javascript
// cache.js
class ProfileCache {
  constructor(ttlSeconds = 300) {
    this.cache = new Map();
    this.ttl = ttlSeconds * 1000;
  }

  get(userId) {
    const entry = this.cache.get(userId);
    if (!entry) return null;
    if (Date.now() > entry.expiresAt) {
      this.cache.delete(userId);
      return null;
    }
    return entry.data;
  }

  set(userId, data) {
    this.cache.set(userId, {
      data,
      expiresAt: Date.now() + this.ttl
    });
  }

  invalidate(userId) {
    this.cache.delete(userId);
  }
}

module.exports = new ProfileCache();
```

```javascript
  cache.set(userId, profile);
  
  res.json(profile);
});

// Invalidate on profile update
app.patch('/profile/:userId', async (req, res) => {
  const { userId } = req.params;
  
  const updated = await db.user.update(userId, req.body);
  cache.invalidate(userId);
  
  res.json(updated);
});
```

**For production:** replace with Redis:

```javascript
const redis = require('redis');
const client = redis.createClient();

async function getProfile(userId) {
  const cached = await client.get(`profile:${userId}`);
  if (cached) return JSON.parse(cached);
  
  const profile = await db.user.findById(userId);
  await client.setEx(`profile:${userId}`, 300, JSON.stringify(profile));
  
  return profile;
}
```

In-memory handles single-instance dev/small load. Redis handles distributed systems, shared cache across servers, larger datasets. TTL set to 300s (5 min) balances freshness vs load.
~~~

## default-s2 / debounce

> Add debounce to a search input that currently fires an API call on every keystroke. Show the code.

### vanilla: 586 answer tokens, 741 billed, 72 lines

~~~
Here are common patterns for debouncing a search input:

## Vanilla JavaScript with setTimeout

```javascript
let debounceTimer;
const searchInput = document.querySelector('input[type="search"]');

searchInput.addEventListener('input', (e) => {
  clearTimeout(debounceTimer);
  debounceTimer = setTimeout(() => {
    fetchResults(e.target.value);
  }, 300); // 300ms delay
});

function fetchResults(query) {
  fetch(`/api/search?q=${encodeURIComponent(query)}`)
    .then(res => res.json())
    .then(data => console.log(data));
}
```

## Reusable debounce function

```javascript
function debounce(func, delay) {
  let timeoutId;
  return function (...args) {
    clearTimeout(timeoutId);
    timeoutId = setTimeout(() => func(...args), delay);
  };
}

const debouncedSearch = debounce((query) => {
  fetch(`/api/search?q=${encodeURIComponent(query)}`)
    .then(res => res.json())
    .then(data => console.log(data));
}, 300);

document.querySelector('input').addEventListener('input', (e) => {
  debouncedSearch(e.target.value);
});
```

## React example

```jsx
import { useState, useEffect, useRef } from 'react';

function SearchInput() {
  const [query, setQuery] = useState('');
  const timeoutRef = useRef(null);

  useEffect(() => {
    clearTimeout(timeoutRef.current);
    
    if (query.trim()) {
      timeoutRef.current = setTimeout(() => {
        fetch(`/api/search?q=${encodeURIComponent(query)}`)
          .then(res => res.json())
          .then(data => console.log(data));
      }, 300);
    }

    return () => clearTimeout(timeoutRef.current);
  }, [query]);

  return <input onChange={(e) => setQuery(e.target.value)} />;
}
```

**Key point:** The debounce clears the previous timer and starts a new one on each keystroke, so the API call only fires when the user stops typing for the specified delay (typically 300–500ms). Without this, you'd hit the API once per character typed.
~~~

Debounce delays API call until user stops typing. Three approaches:

**Vanilla JS (manual)**

```javascript
const handleSearch = (e) => {
  const query = e.target.value;
  clearTimeout(debounceTimer);
  debounceTimer = setTimeout(() => {
    fetchSearchResults(query);

};

<input onChange={handleSearch} placeholder="Search..." />
```

**React (useEffect)**
```javascript
function SearchInput() {
  const [query, setQuery] = useState('');
  const [results, setResults] = useState([]);

  useEffect(() => {
    const timer = setTimeout(() => {
      if (query) fetchSearchResults(query).then(setResults);
    }, 300);

    return () => clearTimeout(timer);
  }, [query]); // Fire when query changes

  return <input value={query} onChange={(e) => setQuery(e.target.value)} />;
}
```

**React (custom hook — reusable)**
```javascript
function useDebounce(value, delay) {
  const [debounced, setDebounced] = useState(value);
  useEffect(() => {
    const timer = setTimeout(() => setDebounced(value), delay);
    return () => clearTimeout(timer);
  }, [value, delay]);
  return debounced;
}

function SearchInput() {
  const [query, setQuery] = useState('');
  const debouncedQuery = useDebounce(query, 300);
  const [results, setResults] = useState([]);

  useEffect(() => {
    if (debouncedQuery) fetchSearchResults(debouncedQuery).then(setResults);
  }, [debouncedQuery]);

  return <input value={query} onChange={(e) => setQuery(e.target.value)} />;
}
```

Key: each keystroke resets timer. Only fires when typing stops for 300ms.

## default-s2 / pooling

> Explain how database connection pooling works and why it helps.

### vanilla: 417 answer tokens, 547 billed, 24 lines

~~~
## How Database Connection Pooling Works

A connection pool maintains a set of pre-established database connections that your application reuses, rather than creating a new connection for each request.

**The mechanics:**
1. When the pool starts, it creates a fixed number of connections (minimum pool size, e.g., 5-10)
2. When your app needs a database query, it requests a connection from the pool
3. The pool returns an available idle connection; if none exist and the pool hasn't reached its maximum size, it creates a new one
4. Your app executes the query, then returns the connection to the pool rather than closing it
5. The returned connection waits idle, ready for the next request

If all connections are in use and the pool is at capacity, requests wait until one becomes available.

## Why It Helps

**Performance:** Creating a database connection is expensive—it involves TCP handshakes, authentication, session initialization. Reusing connections eliminates this overhead for every query. A pooled query might take 1ms; creating a fresh connection might add 10-50ms.

**Resource efficiency:** Database servers have limits on concurrent connections. Without pooling, a spike in traffic could exhaust them or crash the server. Pooling caps usage by limiting concurrent connections at the application level, so the database stays stable even under load.

**Throughput:** More applications can share the same database server because connections spend less time tied up per operation. With pooling, one connection serves many requests sequentially; without it, you'd need that many simultaneous connections.

**Latency control:** The pool bounds worst-case response time—you can't have unbounded connection growth or connection-creation delays stalling requests unpredictably.

Most frameworks and ORMs (Sequel, SQLAlchemy, Node's `pg`, etc.) ship with or recommend a pooling library for this reason.
~~~

### caveman: 184 answer tokens, 710 billed, 14 lines

~~~
Pool = set of open DB connections, ready to reuse.

**How it works:**
Request asks pool for connection. Pool returns idle one or creates new (if under limit). When done, connection returns to pool, not closed. Next request reuses it.

**Why it helps:**
- Skip handshake overhead each time → faster response
- Reuse = less CPU/memory per request
- Prevents connection exhaustion (OS limits open sockets)
- Throughput scales under load—reusing beats making thousands of new connections

**Without pooling:** Each request creates connection → authenticate → query → close. Handshake + auth = expensive. Under load = many sockets open = system buckles.

**With pooling:** Create N connections once. Many requests cycle through them. Near-zero overhead per request.
~~~

### ponytail: 331 answer tokens, 526 billed, 13 lines

~~~
## Database Connection Pooling

**What it is:** Instead of creating a fresh database connection every time your app needs one, a pool maintains a small set of pre-opened connections. When code needs a connection, it borrows one from the pool; when done, it returns it. The pool recycles these same connections across requests.

**Why it helps:**

- **Connection overhead is brutal.** Opening a TCP socket, negotiating TLS, authenticating, and initializing a session takes hundreds of milliseconds. Creating one per request = ≈500ms per request even on a fast network. With pooling, you borrow an already-warm connection (microseconds).

- **Finite database capacity.** Databases can't handle unlimited concurrent connections—they consume memory and file descriptors. A pool caps connections at a fixed size (e.g., 10 connections serving 1000 requests). Without it, one spike spawns 1000 connection attempts and the database chokes.

- **Resource efficiency.** Connections are reused, so you use ~10 of them instead of opening/closing thousands. The database doesn't waste RAM holding zombie sessions.

**Practical:** A web app with pooling (10 connections) serving 1000 req/sec means each connection handles ~100 requests/sec. Without pooling, you'd need 1000+ connections, which either fails or creates a bottleneck. That's why every serious web framework has pooling built in—it's not optional.
~~~

### chisle: 311 answer tokens, 461 billed, 11 lines

~~~
**What it does:** Database connections are expensive—TCP handshake, authentication, state init. A connection pool pre-creates N connections at startup, keeps them open, hands them out to requests on demand, recycles them back when done. Same connection handles multiple requests over time.

**Why it matters:** Creating a fresh connection per query → 100–1000ms overhead per request (network round trips, auth, memory allocate). Pooling amortizes that once; subsequent requests grab an idle connection in ~1ms. Under load, this is the difference between handling 10 req/sec and 1000 req/sec on the same hardware.

**Key wins:**
- Request latency drops 10–100x (no connection overhead).
- Throughput scales with pool size, not connection startup speed.
- Database side: fewer file descriptors, less memory, less auth overhead.
- Stability: pool bounds concurrent connections, prevents "thundering herd" of clients hammering the DB during spikes.

**Gotchas:** Pool size must match DB limits (default often too small). Connections idle → memory tied up; aggressive timeout can help but risks reconnect thrashing. Long-running queries hold a connection; if pool saturates, new requests queue/fail. Transactions need care: pooling assumes stateless requests, so don't start a txn, return the connection, then resume it later.
~~~

### chisle-C: 296 answer tokens, 411 billed, 14 lines

~~~
**How it works:**

Connection pool maintains a set of pre-created database connections ready to use. When app needs one, it checks out from the pool instead of creating new. After done, returns it to the pool for reuse. If all connections busy, request waits in queue or times out.

**Why it helps:**

- **Expensive creation:** Opening DB connection is slow—TCP handshake, authentication, negotiation. Creating one per query kills performance.
- **Resource limits:** OS and DB have finite connection slots. Unbounded connection creation exhausts them, crashes app.
- **Concurrency:** Pool lets many app threads/requests share fewer connections. 10,000 requests don't need 10,000 connections; pool of 20–50 handles all, cycling fast.
- **Latency:** Reusing warm connection is microseconds; creating new is milliseconds. Big difference at scale.

**Real example:** Web server with 100 concurrent users. Without pooling: 100 connections open, server sluggish. With pool of 10: connections cycle in/out, same users handled faster, far fewer resources used.

Pools also handle stale/dead connections—discard them, reconnect transparently. Standard in every production app: Django, Spring, Node drivers, etc. all pool by default.
~~~

## default-s2 / regex-concept

> Explain what a regular expression backreference is, with one short example.

### vanilla: 227 answer tokens, 383 billed, 17 lines

~~~
A backreference in a regex is a way to match the same text that was previously captured by a group within the same pattern. It's written as `\1`, `\2`, etc., referring to the 1st, 2nd captured group, and so on.

**Example:** Match repeated words separated by a space:

```regex
\b(\w+)\s+\1\b
```

This matches a word (captured in group 1), followed by one or more spaces, followed by the *exact same word* again. So it would match "hello hello" or "foo foo", but not "hello world".

In practice:
- The pattern `(\w+)` captures a word into group 1
- `\s+` matches spaces
- `\1` refers back to whatever was in group 1
- `\b` ensures word boundaries

Backreferences are useful for finding duplicates, enforcing matching patterns (like opening/closing tags), or validating symmetry in text.
~~~

### caveman: 99 answer tokens, 278 billed, 8 lines

~~~
Backreference lets you match same text twice. Use `\1`, `\2`, etc. to refer to captured groups.

Example: match repeated words:
```regex
\b(\w+)\s+\1\b
```

Captures first word with `(\w+)`, then `\1` matches exact same word again. Finds "hello hello" or "the the" but not "hello world".
~~~

### ponytail: 163 answer tokens, 323 billed, 11 lines

~~~
A **backreference** lets you match text that's identical to something you already captured earlier in the regex pattern. You reference it with `\1`, `\2`, etc., pointing to the 1st, 2nd captured group.

**Example:** Find repeated words:

```regex
\b(\w+)\s+\1\b
```

This matches "the the" or "hello hello" but not "the dog". The `(\w+)` captures a word, and `\1` requires the exact same word to appear again after whitespace.

**Replacement:** You can also use backreferences in replacements — `s/(\w+)\s+\1/$1/` removes the duplicate.
~~~

### chisle: 143 answer tokens, 348 billed, 3 lines

~~~
A backreference `\1`, `\2`, etc. refers to text captured by an earlier group in the same pattern or replacement. It lets you match repeated content without hardcoding what it is.

Example: `(\w+)\s+\1` matches any word followed by itself again (e.g., "hello hello"). The `(\w+)` captures the first word into group 1, and `\1` matches the identical text. Used in replacements too: `s/(\d+)-(\d+)/$2-$1/` swaps two hyphen-separated numbers by referencing their captured groups.
~~~

### chisle-C: 140 answer tokens, 311 billed, 5 lines

~~~
A backreference is a placeholder in a regex that matches the exact same text as a previously captured group, written as `\1`, `\2`, etc. (numbered by group order).

Example: `(\w+) \1` matches a repeated word with space between—catches `"hello hello"` or `"the the"` but not `"hello world"`. The `\1` refers back to whatever `(\w+)` captured.

Use case: finding duplicates, validating matching brackets/quotes, replacing with captured content (`s/(\w+) \1/$1/` removes the duplicate).
~~~

## default-s2 / rest-graphql

> Summarize the main tradeoffs between REST and GraphQL for a new API.

### vanilla: 130 answer tokens, 350 billed, 3 lines

~~~
**REST** is simpler to build and cache, but clients often fetch unnecessary fields (over-fetching) or need multiple requests (under-fetching). **GraphQL** lets clients request exactly what they need in one call, but adds complexity on the server side and makes caching trickier.

**Main tradeoff:** Choose REST if your API is straightforward with predictable access patterns and you want minimal operational overhead. Choose GraphQL if clients have diverse data needs (mobile vs web vs third-party integrations) and you can invest in proper tooling and monitoring for query complexity.
~~~

### caveman: 149 answer tokens, 433 billed, 5 lines

~~~
**REST**: Simple, cacheable, standardized HTTP verbs. Tradeoff is over/underfetching—clients get unwanted fields or need multiple roundtrips. Clients have limited control over response shape.

**GraphQL**: Clients request exactly what they need in one query. Tradeoff is caching complexity (not HTTP-native), steeper learning curve, and server-side resolution can explode in complexity as schema grows.

**Pick REST** if API is small, clients have predictable needs, and caching matters. **Pick GraphQL** if you have many clients with different data needs or one dominant client that requires fine-grained control.
~~~

### ponytail: 147 answer tokens, 467 billed, 3 lines

~~~
**REST** wins on simplicity, caching, and known patterns—ship it if clients have stable needs and you value operational predictability. **GraphQL** wins when you're serving multiple client types iterating fast or need clients to fetch exactly what they use—but trade off caching complexity and backend query cost management.

Main tradeoff: REST is a simpler server burden; GraphQL is a simpler client burden. Pick based on whether you control the clients or they control themselves. For a new API with internal clients or a single frontend you own, REST scales simpler. For a platform API or many independent clients, GraphQL's flexibility often wins the operational cost back.
~~~

### chisle: 202 answer tokens, 452 billed, 5 lines

~~~
**REST**: client requests fixed schemas per endpoint → simpler server, easy HTTP caching, but over-fetching (get fields you don't need) or N+1 problems (multiple requests). API changes require versioning or new endpoints.

**GraphQL**: client specifies exact fields needed → one request per query, no over-fetching, evolves without versioning. Trade: query complexity lives on server (parsing, validation, execution get expensive), HTTP caching harder (POST requests, single endpoint), larger learning curve, N+1is a footgun if you don't batch resolve.

**Pick REST** if caching matters (CDN, public APIs), payloads are predictable, teams prefer simplicity. **Pick GraphQL** if clients have wildly different data needs (mobile vs web vs third-party), you iterate fast and need schema flexibility, complexity of a GraphQL layer is worth the client simplification.
~~~

### chisle-C: 234 answer tokens, 678 billed, 7 lines

~~~
**REST**: Simple to build and understand. HTTP caching and CDNs work out-of-the-box. Each resource gets its own endpoint, rigid schema. Forces clients to either fetch more data than needed (over-fetching) or make multiple requests (under-fetching). Versioning headaches as schema evolves.

**GraphQL**: Clients request exactly the fields they need in a single query—no over/under-fetching. Schema-driven and introspectable. Server-side complexity much higher: N+1 query problems, harder caching (HTTP caching doesn't apply naturally), monitoring/debugging trickier. Steeper learning curve.

**When REST wins**: Simple domain, cacheable resources, public APIs where HTTP semantics matter, small team. **When GraphQL wins**: Complex interconnected data, mobile clients (payload size matters), many different client needs from same backend, team comfortable with the complexity tax.

For a new API: REST if you're unsure or simple; GraphQL if you have complex data relationships and clients need flexibility.
~~~

## default-s3 / auth-bug

> Our auth middleware rejects valid tokens at the exact expiry boundary (it uses currentTime > expiry). Find and fix the root cause.

### vanilla: 321 answer tokens, 2543 billed, 27 lines

~~~
# Root Cause: Off-by-One Comparison Operator

The bug is the **comparison operator itself**. Using `currentTime > expiry` creates a boundary condition at exactly `currentTime == expiry`.

**What's happening:**
- When `currentTime == expiry`: The condition `currentTime > expiry` evaluates to **false** → token is accepted
- When `currentTime > expiry`: Condition is **true** → token is rejected

This means tokens are accepted *at* their expiry boundary, then rejected immediately after. In security contexts, this is typically backwards.

## The Fix

Change the comparison operator from `>` to `>=`:

```python
# ❌ Wrong (accepts tokens at the boundary)
if currentTime > expiry:
    reject_token()

# ✅ Correct (rejects tokens at and after the boundary)
if currentTime >= expiry:
    reject_token()
```

**Why this matters:** A token with `expiry = 1700000000` should be rejected the instant that timestamp is reached. Using `>` allows a narrow window where `currentTime == expiry` and the token is still considered valid—this violates the security property that tokens are invalid at and after their expiry time.

This is one of the most common off-by-one errors in authentication systems. The fix ensures tokens are rejected at the exact boundary, not after it.
~~~

### caveman: 185 answer tokens, 682 billed, 23 lines

~~~
Root cause: logic backwards or off-by-one.

If middleware **rejects** at boundary using `currentTime > expiry`, but description says rejection happens, then actual code likely uses `currentTime >= expiry` (not `>`).

**The fix:**

Change rejection condition from `>=` to `>`:

```
// Wrong: rejects token AT expiry time
if (currentTime >= expiryTime) {
  reject();
}

// Right: rejects AFTER expiry time  
if (currentTime > expiryTime) {
  reject();
}
```

Token valid from issue time through expiry time inclusive. Only reject when clock passes it.

Classic off-by-one: `>=` treats boundary as already expired. `>` lets it live exactly at the second.
~~~

### ponytail: 202 answer tokens, 530 billed, 17 lines

~~~
The root cause: **boundary condition error in comparison operator**.

Using `currentTime > expiry` rejects tokens when `currentTime == expiry`, but that exact moment is still within the token's valid lifetime. The token became invalid *after* that instant, not *at* it.

**Fix:** Change `>` to `>=`

```
Before: if (currentTime > expiry) reject()
After:  if (currentTime >= expiry) reject()
```

Now:
- `currentTime < expiry` → valid ✓
- `currentTime == expiry` → still valid ✓ (boundary is inclusive)
- `currentTime >= expiry + 1 second` → rejected ✓

This is the canonical off-by-one on time-based gates. The expiry timestamp marks the last instant the token works, not the first it doesn't.
~~~

### chisle: 246 answer tokens, 2268 billed, 14 lines

~~~
**Root cause:** Precision mismatch. The token's `exp` claim (seconds since epoch, integer) is being compared directly against `Date.now()` (milliseconds). At the exact boundary, `currentTime_ms > exp_seconds` is true because milliseconds are higher precision, triggering rejection 999ms early.

**Fix:** Normalize to the same precision before comparison.

```javascript
// Before (buggy)
if (Date.now() > tokenExpiry) reject();  // ms > seconds: always true near boundary

// After (correct)
const nowInSeconds = Math.floor(Date.now() / 1000);
if (nowInSeconds > tokenExpiry) reject();  // seconds > seconds: works at boundary
```

**Alternative root cause:** If `exp` is actually `>=` in the code (not `>` as stated), it rejects *at* expiry rather than *after*. Add a grace period: `if (nowInSeconds >= tokenExpiry - 10) reject()` to allow 10 seconds of clock skew.
~~~

### chisle-C: 250 answer tokens, 2100 billed, 23 lines

~~~
**Root cause: Time unit mismatch.**

If `expiry` is in seconds (e.g., unix timestamp 1000) and `currentTime` is in milliseconds (1000500), then `1000500 > 1000` is true—the token rejects even when times are equal, because units aren't aligned.

At the boundary specifically, when millisecond precision pushes `currentTime` just past the second-level `expiry`, the comparison fires incorrectly.

**Fix:**

Convert both to the same unit before comparison:

```javascript
// Both in milliseconds, or both in seconds
if (currentTime >= expiry) {
  rejectToken();
}
```

Also flip `>` to `>=` so tokens stay valid *through* the expiry moment, then reject after.

The boundary bug surfaces because:
- Expiry: 1000 (seconds)
- Current: 1000.001 (seconds), or 1000001 (ms) if units collide
- Comparison breaks at the microsecond / unit edge where precision matters most
~~~

## default-s3 / cache

> Add a cache layer for our user profile API responses. Show the code.

### vanilla: 576 answer tokens, 795 billed, 75 lines

~~~
# User Profile API Cache Layer

Here's a practical cache implementation for user profile API responses:

```javascript
class UserProfileCache {
  constructor(ttlSeconds = 300) {
    this.cache = new Map();
    this.ttl = ttlSeconds * 1000;
  }

  get(userId) {
    const cached = this.cache.get(userId);
    if (!cached) return null;
    
    if (Date.now() > cached.expiresAt) {
      this.cache.delete(userId);
      return null;
    }
    
    return cached.data;
  }

  set(userId, data) {
    this.cache.set(userId, {
      data,
      expiresAt: Date.now() + this.ttl
    });
  }

  invalidate(userId) {
    this.cache.delete(userId);
  }

  invalidateAll() {
    this.cache.clear();
  }
}

// Usage in API route handler
const profileCache = new UserProfileCache(300); // 5 min TTL

app.get('/api/users/:id/profile', async (req, res) => {
  const userId = req.params.id;
  
  // Check cache first
  let profile = profileCache.get(userId);
  if (profile) {
    return res.json({ ...profile, cached: true });
  }
  
  // Cache miss: fetch from DB
  profile = await db.getUserProfile(userId);
  profileCache.set(userId, profile);
  
  res.json(profile);
});

// Invalidate cache on profile update
app.put('/api/users/:id/profile', async (req, res) => {
  const userId = req.params.id;
  const updated = await db.updateUserProfile(userId, req.body);
  
  profileCache.invalidate(userId); // Clear stale cache
  res.json(updated);
});
```

**Key features:**
- **TTL-based expiration** — automatic cleanup after 5 min (configurable)
- **Cache invalidation** — explicit clearing on updates to prevent stale data
- **Hit detection** — optional `cached` flag to monitor effectiveness
- **Simple API** — get/set/invalidate operations

For production, consider Redis for distributed caching across multiple servers, or use a library like `node-cache` for more sophisticated features.
~~~

```javascript
  }

  // Store in cache
  profileCache.set(id, profile);
  res.json(profile);
});

// Update endpoint invalidates cache

  const updated = await db.users.updateById(id, req.body);
  profileCache.invalidate(id); // Clear cached version
  res.json(updated);
});
```

**Better: Redis (production)**

```javascript
const redis = require('redis');
const client = redis.createClient();

app.get('/api/users/:id', async (req, res) => {
  const { id } = req.params;
  
  // Try Redis
  const cached = await client.get(`user:${id}`);
  if (cached) return res.json(JSON.parse(cached));
  
  // DB hit
  const profile = await db.users.findById(id);
  if (!profile) return res.status(404).json({ error: 'Not found' });
  
  // Store 5 min
  await client.setEx(`user:${id}`, 300, JSON.stringify(profile));
  res.json(profile);
});

app.patch('/api/users/:id', async (req, res) => {
  const updated = await db.users.updateById(req.params.id, req.body);
  await client.del(`user:${updated.id}`);
  res.json(updated);
});
```

**Key points:**
- TTL prevents stale data
- Invalidate on writes
- Redis scales across servers (memory cache doesn't)
- Add monitoring: cache hit/miss rates

Pick memory cache for simple apps, Redis for production systems.

```python
from functools import lru_cache
from datetime import datetime, timedelta

# Simplest: use stdlib for in-memory caching
@lru_cache(maxsize=1000)
def get_user_profile(user_id: int):
    """Fetch and cache user profile. Auto-evicts least-used when full."""
    return fetch_from_api(user_id)


# If you need TTL (expiration): lightweight dict-based cache
class TTLCache:
    def __init__(self, ttl_seconds: int):
        self.ttl = ttl_seconds
        self.cache = {}
    
    def get(self, user_id: int):
        if user_id in self.cache:
            value, timestamp = self.cache[user_id]
            if datetime.now() - timestamp < timedelta(seconds=self.ttl):
                return value
            del self.cache[user_id]
        return None
    
    def set(self, user_id: int, value):
        self.cache[user_id] = (value, datetime.now())


profile_cache = TTLCache(ttl_seconds=3600)

def get_user_profile_cached(user_id: int):
    cached = profile_cache.get(user_id)
    if cached:
        return cached
    profile = fetch_from_api(user_id)
    profile_cache.set(user_id, profile)
    return profile
```

**Skipped:** Redis/Memcached (add when cache outlives process or spans multiple servers), invalidation strategy (add when profiles mutate), cache warming (add if cold misses matter).

**Choose:** `@lru_cache` for simplicity, TTLCache if profiles expire.

```javascript
// cache.js - Simple in-memory cache with TTL
class CacheLayer {
  constructor(ttlMs = 5 * 60 * 1000) {
    this.cache = new Map();
    this.ttl = ttlMs;
  }

  set(key, value) {
    this.cache.set(key, {
      value,
      expiresAt: Date.now() + this.ttl,
    });
  }

  get(key) {
    const entry = this.cache.get(key);
    if (!entry) return null;
    if (Date.now() > entry.expiresAt) {
      this.cache.delete(key);
      return null;
    }
    return entry.value;
  }

  invalidate(key) {
    this.cache.delete(key);
  }

  clear() {
    this.cache.clear();
  }
}

module.exports = CacheLayer;
```

```javascript
// userProfileAPI.js - API with caching
const CacheLayer = require('./cache');
const cache = new CacheLayer(10 * 60 * 1000); // 10 min TTL

async function getUserProfile(userId) {
  const cacheKey = `user:${userId}`;
  
  // Check cache first
  const cached = cache.get(cacheKey);
  if (cached) return cached;

  // Fetch from DB/API
  const profile = await db.users.findById(userId);
  
  // Store in cache
  cache.set(cacheKey, profile);
  return profile;
}

async function updateUserProfile(userId, updates) {
  const updated = await db.users.update(userId, updates);
  
  // Invalidate cache on write
  cache.invalidate(`user:${userId}`);
  
  return updated;
}

module.exports = { getUserProfile, updateUserProfile };
```

**Tradeoffs:** In-memory only → loses on restart; use Redis for persistence. TTL set too short → stale cache misses benefits; too long → stale data. Manual invalidation on update prevents serving old data but adds complexity—auto-expiry trades freshness for simplicity.
~~~
