## default-s3 / debounce

> Add debounce to a search input that currently fires an API call on every keystroke. Show the code.

### vanilla: 624 answer tokens, 736 billed, 76 lines

~~~
Here's a debounce implementation for a search input:

**Vanilla JavaScript with a debounce utility:**

```javascript
function debounce(func, delay) {
  let timeoutId;
  return function (...args) {
    clearTimeout(timeoutId);
    timeoutId = setTimeout(() => func(...args), delay);
  };
}

const searchInput = document.getElementById('search');
const debouncedSearch = debounce((query) => {
  fetch(`/api/search?q=${encodeURIComponent(query)}`)
    .then(res => res.json())
    .then(data => console.log(data));
}, 300);

searchInput.addEventListener('input', (e) => {
  debouncedSearch(e.target.value);
});
```

**React with useCallback and useRef:**

```javascript
import { useCallback, useRef } from 'react';

function SearchInput() {
  const timeoutRef = useRef(null);

  const handleSearch = useCallback((query) => {
    clearTimeout(timeoutRef.current);
    timeoutRef.current = setTimeout(() => {
      fetch(`/api/search?q=${encodeURIComponent(query)}`)
        .then(res => res.json())
        .then(data => console.log(data));
    }, 300);
  }, []);

  return (
    <input
      type="text"
      placeholder="Search..."
      onChange={(e) => handleSearch(e.target.value)}
    />
  );
}
```

**React with a custom hook:**

```javascript
function useDebounce(callback, delay) {
  const timeoutRef = useRef(null);
  
  return useCallback((...args) => {
    clearTimeout(timeoutRef.current);
    timeoutRef.current = setTimeout(() => callback(...args), delay);
  }, [callback, delay]);
}

function SearchInput() {
  const debouncedSearch = useDebounce((query) => {
    fetch(`/api/search?q=${encodeURIComponent(query)}`)
      .then(res => res.json())
      .then(data => console.log(data));
  }, 300);

  return <input onChange={(e) => debouncedSearch(e.target.value)} />;
}
```

The key: `clearTimeout` cancels the pending call, and `setTimeout` reschedules it. Each keystroke resets the timer, so the API only fires 300ms after the user stops typing. Adjust the delay (300ms) based on your needs.
~~~

```javascript
function useDebouncedValue(value, delay = 300) {
  const [debounced, setDebounced] = useState(value);

  useEffect(() => {
    const timer = setTimeout(() => setDebounced(value), delay);
    return () => clearTimeout(timer);
  }, [value, delay]);

  return debounced;
}

function SearchInput() {
  const [query, setQuery] = useState('');
  const debouncedQuery = useDebouncedValue(query);

  useEffect(() => {
    if (debouncedQuery) fetchResults(debouncedQuery);
  }, [debouncedQuery]);

  return <input onChange={(e) => setQuery(e.target.value)} />;
}
```

300ms delay common. Adjust as needed. Cleanup timer on unmount prevents memory leaks.

```js
      if (query) fetchResults(query);

    return () => clearTimeout(timer);
```

## Reusable debounce helper

```js
function debounce(fn, delay) {
  let timer;
  return (...args) => {
    clearTimeout(timer);
    timer = setTimeout(() => fn(...args), delay);
  };
}

// Use it
```

## default-s3 / pooling

> Explain how database connection pooling works and why it helps.

### vanilla: 413 answer tokens, 527 billed, 29 lines

~~~
## How Database Connection Pooling Works

A connection pool maintains a set of **reusable database connections** rather than creating a new connection every time an application needs one.

**The flow:**
1. When the app starts, the pool creates a small number of connections (e.g., 5–10) and holds them open
2. When the app needs to query the database, it requests a connection from the pool instead of creating a new one
3. The pool hands over an idle connection; the app uses it
4. When done, the app returns the connection to the pool rather than closing it
5. The connection goes back into the pool, ready for the next request

**Key idea:** connections are expensive to create but cheap to reuse, so you keep them alive between requests.

## Why It Helps

**Performance:** Creating a database connection involves:
- Establishing a TCP connection
- Authenticating with the database
- Negotiating protocol details

This can take 50–200ms per connection. With pooling, that overhead happens once at startup, not for every query.

**Resource efficiency:** Without pooling, a busy app might create hundreds of connections over time. Each uses memory and database server resources. A pool keeps this bounded—you might have only 10–20 active connections instead of 1000+.

**Scalability:** Connection pooling lets a single app handle more concurrent requests because connections are recycled rather than exhausted. The database server also scales better when apps maintain steady connection counts instead of constantly opening and closing.

**Stability:** Most pools have safety features: they detect dead connections and replace them, enforce timeouts so connections don't hang forever, and prevent apps from exhausting all connections.

In practice, without pooling, a web server handling a thousand requests per minute could spiral into resource exhaustion or latency collapse—with pooling, it stays stable.
~~~

### caveman: 216 answer tokens, 1149 billed, 18 lines

~~~
Pool reuses open connections. No new connection per request.

Handshake expensive: TCP setup, auth, session init. Pooling skips this overhead.

**How it works:**
- Maintain N open connections in pool
- Request borrows one, uses it, returns it
- Next request reuses same connection

**Why it helps:**
- Eliminate repeated handshake cost → faster
- Fewer total connections needed (100 requests share 10-connection pool instead of 100 separate connections)
- Server resources better used
- Better throughput under load

**Example:** 1000 requests without pooling = 1000 handshakes. With 10-connection pool = ~10 handshakes. Handshakes are bottleneck. Pooling sidesteps it.

Connection stays open between requests, ready to go. Under heavy load, queries queue for available pooled connection rather than waiting for new handshake each time.
~~~

### ponytail: 395 answer tokens, 545 billed, 24 lines

~~~
## Connection Pooling

**What it is:** A pool maintains a fixed set of open database connections ready to reuse. Instead of a new connection per request (expensive), the application borrows one from the pool, uses it, and returns it.

**Why it helps:**

1. **Connection overhead is real.** Opening a connection involves TCP handshake, authentication, and negotiation—often 100-500ms. Creating a new connection per request kills throughput.

2. **OS resource limits.** Each connection is a process or thread on the database server. With 1000 concurrent requests and no pooling, you'd try to spawn 1000 connections. The database maxes out; new connections hang or fail.

3. **Memory per connection.** Each connection holds buffers, session state, transaction info. 10,000 connections idle = wasted server RAM.

**How it works:**
- Application boots, pool pre-opens (say) 10 connections.
- Request arrives, grabs an idle connection, runs query, returns it.
- Next request reuses that same connection—no handshake.
- If all 10 are busy, the request waits briefly for one to free up (or the pool grows to a max, then queues).

**The payoff:** 
- One 500ms handshake at startup instead of per request → orders of magnitude faster.
- 10 connections + queueing handles 100+ concurrent requests.
- Memory fixed (10 connections, not 100).

Most frameworks (Django, Node/pg, Go/database/sql) pool by default. Direct pooling config lives in connection strings: pool size, max lifetime, idle timeout. Too small = queueing delays; too large = wasted resources.
~~~

### chisle: 407 answer tokens, 569 billed, 17 lines

~~~
**The Problem:** Creating a new database connection is expensive. TCP handshake, authentication, session setup. If your app opens/closes a connection per query, you spend most time connecting, not querying.

**How it works:** Connection pool maintains N pre-opened, authenticated connections sitting idle. When your app needs one, it grabs from the pool. When done, returns it (closes statement, clears state) but keeps TCP open. Next request reuses it instantly.

**Why it helps:**

1. **Latency** → Connection setup overhead gone. Grab-use-return in microseconds instead of ms per query.

2. **Resource efficiency** → DB server has fixed number of active connections instead of thrashing to create/destroy per request. Each connection consumes memory; reusing them means the server breathes easier.

3. **Throughput under load** → 100 concurrent requests don't spawn 100 connections. With pool size 10, they queue briefly but execute in bursts. Without pooling, you'd hit connection limits and start failing. Pooling lets N concurrent requests share M connections (N >> M).

4. **Prevents connection exhaustion** → Most databases have limits. Pool enforces a ceiling; requests beyond that wait (or fail fast with a clear error), not overwhelm the server.

**Tradeoff:** Pool must be sized right. Too small and queries queue unnecessarily. Too large and you waste server memory. Most drivers auto-tune or accept simple hints (min=5, max=20 is typical for a web app).

Connection pooling is so fundamental that virtually every driver implements it (psycopg2, Node pg, Java HikariCP, Go sql.DB). Single-threaded runtime → connection pool at the library level. Multi-threaded → pooling in middleware or the driver itself.
~~~

### chisle-C: 316 answer tokens, 459 billed, 18 lines

~~~
Database connection pooling maintains a reusable set of open connections to the database instead of creating new ones per request.

**How it works:**
Client requests a connection from the pool. Pool returns an idle connection if available, or creates one if below the max limit. When the client finishes (closes), the connection returns to the pool rather than terminating. Stale or leaked connections get pruned.

**Why it helps:**

1. **Connection overhead eliminated** — TCP handshake, authentication, session setup on every query is slow. Reusing open connections skips this.

2. **Resource limits respected** — Databases can't handle infinite connections. Pooling caps concurrent connections, queuing requests instead of failing.

3. **Throughput scales** — Many requests can share a small pool of connections. Without pooling, 1000 simultaneous requests need 1000 connections; with pooling (size 20), they multiplex over 20.

4. **Predictability** — Pool size is tunable and known. Prevents exhaustion or runaway resource consumption.

**Trade-off:** Idle connections consume server memory and database slots. Pool needs sizing: too small = requests wait, too large = wasted resources.

Common in production via libraries like HikariCP (Java), `psycopg2.pool` (Python), `mysql2/promise` (Node), `sqlc` (Go).
~~~

## default-s3 / regex-concept

> Explain what a regular expression backreference is, with one short example.

### vanilla: 147 answer tokens, 263 billed, 10 lines

~~~
A **backreference** in a regex refers back to a previously captured group within the same pattern. It's written as `\1`, `\2`, etc., where the number corresponds to the group's order.

**Example:** Match repeated words:
```regex
\b(\w+)\s+\1\b
```

This captures a word in group 1, matches whitespace, then `\1` refers back to that same word. So it matches "hello hello" or "the the" but not "hello world".

**Common use case:** Finding duplicates in text or validating matching pairs like `<tag>content</tag>`.
~~~

### caveman: 116 answer tokens, 274 billed, 8 lines

~~~
Backreference lets regex reuse text captured earlier in pattern. Denote with `\1`, `\2`, etc (or `(?P<name>...)` + `(?P=name)` for named).

Example — match repeated word:
```regex
(\w+)\s+\1
```

Captures first word with `(\w+)`, then `\1` matches that exact same word again. Finds "hello hello" or "world world" but not "hello world".
~~~

### ponytail: 124 answer tokens, 277 billed, 7 lines

~~~
A backreference lets you match the same text a capturing group already found. It's written as `\1` for the first group, `\2` for the second, etc.

**Example:** `(\w+)\s+\1` matches any word followed by whitespace and then the *same* word again:
- `hello hello` → matches
- `hello world` → no match

Common use: finding repeated words in text, matching balanced quotes like `(["'])(.*?)\1` (quote + content + same quote).
~~~

### chisle: 112 answer tokens, 276 billed, 5 lines

~~~
A backreference lets you match the same text that an earlier captured group matched. Written as `\1`, `\2`, etc., referring to group 1, 2, and so on.

Example: `(\w+)\s+\1` matches any word followed by whitespace and that same word again—so "hello hello" or "test test".

Use: detecting repetition, HTML tag pairs (`<(\w+)>.*?</\1>`), palindromes.
~~~

~~~
A backreference lets you match the same text a regex captured in an earlier group. Use `\1` for the first group, `\2` for the second, etc.

Example: match repeated words
```regex
\b(\w+)\s+\1\b
```

This matches words like "the the" or "hello hello" — `(\w+)` captures a word, then `\1` matches that exact same word again. Useful for finding duplicates or matching paired delimiters like `<tag>content</tag>`.
~~~

## default-s3 / rest-graphql

> Summarize the main tradeoffs between REST and GraphQL for a new API.

### vanilla: 198 answer tokens, 422 billed, 5 lines

~~~
**REST** is simpler and more proven: straightforward CRUD operations, HTTP caching works out-of-the-box, and you can get started quickly. The main downside is data efficiency — clients either over-fetch (get fields they don't need) or under-fetch (require multiple round-trips to get related resources).

**GraphQL** solves those efficiency problems: clients request exactly what they need, related data comes in one query, and you get strong typing. The tradeoff is higher implementation complexity (schema design, query optimization, caching strategies), and it's more learning curve for teams unfamiliar with it.

**Pick REST** if your API has straightforward access patterns and you value simplicity and community tooling. **Pick GraphQL** if clients have diverse data needs (web, mobile, internal dashboards pulling different subsets), or if bandwidth/latency is a constraint.
~~~

### caveman: 188 answer tokens, 558 billed, 7 lines

~~~
**REST** — Simple, cacheable, stateless. Works with browser/CDN caching out-of-box. Overfetch common (extra fields you don't need), underfetch forces N+1 queries. Good for static resources, public APIs.

**GraphQL** — Precise queries, no over/underfetch. Single request gives exact data you asked for. Steeper learning curve. Caching harder (single endpoint, varied query shapes). Better for mobile/bandwidth-tight scenarios. Complex queries cost server resources.

**Tradeoff:** REST = simpler ops/caching, fat payloads. GraphQL = efficient queries, operational complexity.

Pick REST if data shape stable, caching matters, team unfamiliar GraphQL. Pick GraphQL if mobile-first, clients need flexible data shapes, bandwidth premium.
~~~

### ponytail: 187 answer tokens, 501 billed, 5 lines

~~~
**REST**: Simple, cacheable, proven. Each endpoint is a fixed contract—straightforward to build and deploy. Downside: clients either fetch too much data (overfetch) or make multiple round trips (underfetch), especially with nested relationships.

**GraphQL**: Clients query exactly what they need in one trip. Great if you have many different client shapes (mobile, web, internal tools) or deeply nested data. Tradeoff: more complex to build, harder to cache (usually POST), and requires upfront schema investment.

**Default to REST** for a straightforward API with predictable access patterns. **Pick GraphQL** if you have multiple clients with different data needs or mobile clients where bandwidth matters. The lazy choice: REST now, GraphQL when you measure overfetch waste. What's the shape of your data and client landscape?
~~~

### chisle: 240 answer tokens, 467 billed, 5 lines

~~~
**REST**: HTTP verbs on fixed URLs, each endpoint returns a fixed schema. Clients get what the server decides → over-fetching (extra fields) or under-fetching (multiple requests for related data). Trivial to cache and monitor because URLs are predictable. Simple to build and reason about. Best when data shape matches your URLs; worst when clients need fine-grained control.

**GraphQL**: Single endpoint, clients request exactly what fields they need across related types in one query. Zero over/under-fetching, fewer requests, stronger contract (schema is queryable). Harder to cache (HTTP layer can't tell queries apart), more complex server implementation, requires query cost/depth limits (attackers can write expensive nested queries). Steeper learning curve.

**Verdict**: REST for straightforward CRUD with known access patterns and high-traffic public APIs that need dumb caching. GraphQL for complex, interconnected data (social graphs, internal dashboards) where clients vary widely in what they need. Neither is universal — hybrid (REST for simple paths, GraphQL behind) works too.
~~~

### chisle-C: 200 answer tokens, 479 billed, 5 lines

~~~
**REST**: Fixed resource shapes—client gets full object or nothing. Simple to cache (HTTP layer), debug (curl works), and scale. Multiple requests for related data, but predictable. Best if clients have uniform needs or bandwidth isn't tight.

**GraphQL**: Client declares exact fields; solves over/under-fetching. Single query fetches related data, but implementing efficient resolvers is harder—N+1 queries lurk easily. Caching moves from HTTP to application layer (more work). Better for mobile, varied clients, or when query patterns are unknown upfront.

**Call it**: REST if your API surfaces a few stable resources and clients are homogeneous (server → server, standard web app). GraphQL if clients are diverse, bandwidth matters (mobile), or they'll request unpredictable field combos. GraphQL costs more to build right; REST is slower to query but faster to ship.
~~~

## large-s1 / apidesign

> Design the REST API for a multi-tenant document store: resources, auth model, pagination, versioning and error format. Explain the decisions.

### vanilla: 1845 answer tokens, 1982 billed, 171 lines

~~~
# Multi-Tenant Document Store REST API Design

## Resources & Endpoints

```
GET    /v1/documents                    # List documents (tenant-scoped)
POST   /v1/documents                    # Create document
GET    /v1/documents/{id}               # Fetch document
PUT    /v1/documents/{id}               # Replace document
PATCH  /v1/documents/{id}               # Update fields
DELETE /v1/documents/{id}               # Delete document
GET    /v1/documents/{id}/versions      # List versions
GET    /v1/documents/{id}/versions/{v}  # Fetch specific version

POST   /v1/documents/{id}/share         # Create share link/grant access
DELETE /v1/documents/{id}/share/{user}  # Revoke access
GET    /v1/documents/{id}/collaborators # List who has access
```

**Decision**: Flat resource hierarchy for documents. Collaboration and versioning are sub-resources under documents rather than parallel top-level resources. This keeps the API discoverable and logically grouped.

---

## Authentication & Multi-Tenancy Model

**Header-based tenant isolation with token claiming**:
```
Authorization: Bearer <JWT>
X-Tenant-ID: tenant-123  # Optional, derived from token if omitted
```

**JWT payload**:
```json
{
  "sub": "user-456",
  "tenant_id": "tenant-123",
  "scope": ["documents:read", "documents:write"],
  "iat": 1700000000,
  "exp": 1700003600
}
```

**Authorization model**:
- Token contains the tenant claim; server validates all requests against it
- No cross-tenant data leakage even if `X-Tenant-ID` header is forged
- Fine-grained scopes (`documents:read`, `documents:write`, `documents:admin:share`) allow API tokens with limited permissions
- Document-level permissions (who can view/edit a specific document) stored separately; queries filtered by both tenant + user permissions

**Decision**: Tenant ID in the token is the source of truth, not a header. Headers can be tampered with; tokens are signed. This prevents a user from accidentally (or maliciously) querying another tenant's data by changing a header.

---

## Pagination

```
GET /v1/documents?limit=20&cursor=abc123def456

{
  "data": [ { "id": "...", "title": "..." }, ... ],
  "pagination": {
    "next_cursor": "xyz789uv0123",
    "has_more": true,
    "count": 20
  }
}
```

**Decision**: Cursor-based pagination over offset. Reasons:
- Offset breaks when items are deleted/inserted during iteration (a user pages through, an item is deleted, they see duplicates or miss items)
- Cursors are stable: they point to a position in a consistent sort order
- Encode sort key + ID in the cursor to avoid database-specific continuation tokens

**Sort order**: By creation date descending (most recent first), tiebreaker by ID.

---

## Versioning

**Strategy: Immutable versions + single latest**

```
GET /v1/documents/{id}
{
  "id": "doc-789",
  "title": "Q4 Budget",
  "content": "...",
  "version": 5,
  "created_at": "2026-01-15T10:00:00Z",
  "updated_at": "2026-11-23T14:30:00Z",
  "version_created_at": "2026-11-23T14:30:00Z"
}

GET /v1/documents/{id}/versions?limit=10
{
  "data": [
    { "version": 5, "created_at": "2026-11-23T14:30:00Z", "created_by": "user-456" },
    { "version": 4, "created_at": "2026-11-20T09:15:00Z", "created_by": "user-789" },
    ...
  ]
}

GET /v1/documents/{id}/versions/4
{
  "id": "doc-789",
  "version": 4,
  "title": "Q4 Budget",
  "content": "...",
  "created_at": "2026-11-20T09:15:00Z",
  "created_by": "user-789"
}
```

**No PUT on versions** — versions are immutable. Create a new version by PATCH/PUT on the main document.

**Decision**: Each write increments the version number. Versions are immutable snapshots. Reasons:
- Audit trail: you can see who changed what and when
- Conflict resolution: if two users edit simultaneously, the second write fails with 409 Conflict; they must fetch the latest and merge manually (or use an optimistic lock with ETag)
- Simplicity: no merge strategies, no branch confusion

---

## Error Format

```json
{
  "error": {
    "code": "CONFLICT",
    "message": "Document version mismatch. Current version is 5, you provided 4.",
    "status": 409,
    "request_id": "req-1700761200-abc123",
    "details": {
      "current_version": 5,
      "provided_version": 4,
      "current_updated_at": "2026-11-23T14:30:00Z"
    }
  }
}
```

**Common error codes**:
- `UNAUTHORIZED` (401): Missing or invalid token
- `FORBIDDEN` (403): Token valid, but user lacks permission for this document
- `NOT_FOUND` (404): Document doesn't exist or isn't visible to this user (same response to avoid leaking existence)
- `CONFLICT` (409): Version mismatch or unique constraint violation
- `VALIDATION_ERROR` (400): Invalid input (title too long, etc.)
- `TENANT_MISMATCH` (400): Explicit error if token tenant ≠ requested resource tenant (should rarely happen)
- `RATE_LIMITED` (429): Per-tenant quota exceeded
- `INTERNAL_ERROR` (500): Server error

**Decision**: 
- Versioning errors (409) include the current version and timestamp so clients can decide to fetch and retry
- Never expose stack traces or internal DB details
- `request_id` for tracing; clients can include it in support tickets
- 404 for both "doesn't exist" and "you don't have permission" — avoids leaking which documents exist to unauthorized users
- Clear `code` field (enum) so clients can branch on it programmatically, not parse messages

---

## Key Design Tradeoffs

| Decision | Tradeoff |
|----------|----------|
| **Cursor pagination** | Slightly more complex to implement; can't jump to page 50 (but this is a feature: prevents expensive queries) |
| **Immutable versions** | No automatic merging; manual conflict resolution; uses more storage. Benefit: simpler semantics, full audit trail. |
| **Tenant in token, not header** | Requires JWT validation on every request. Benefit: impossible for a user to escape their tenant. |
| **409 on conflict, not auto-merge** | Clients must handle conflicts; slightly more verbose. Benefit: predictable, no silent data loss. |
| **Same 404 for missing/forbidden** | Information hiding; slightly harder to debug. Benefit: no accidental data disclosure. |

---

This design prioritizes auditability, multi-tenancy safety, and predictable conflict handling over convenience. Would you like me to elaborate on any section or discuss alternatives?
~~~

### caveman: 1173 answer tokens, 1403 billed, 113 lines

~~~
# Multi-Tenant Document Store API Design

## Resources

**Core hierarchy:**
```
/tenants/{tenant_id}/documents
/tenants/{tenant_id}/documents/{doc_id}
/tenants/{tenant_id}/documents/{doc_id}/versions
/tenants/{tenant_id}/documents/{doc_id}/versions/{version_id}
```

**Rationale:** Tenant isolation at URL layer enforces boundary. Document resource owns versions (not separate entity) — simpler than versioned collections. Avoids `/versions/{doc_id}` ambiguity.

**Additional endpoints:**
- `POST /tenants` — create tenant (admin-only)
- `GET /tenants/{tenant_id}/documents?search=...` — list with filtering
- `PATCH /tenants/{tenant_id}/documents/{doc_id}` — partial update (don't force full replacement)

## Auth Model

**Token-based with tenant claim:**
```
Authorization: Bearer <JWT>
Payload: { sub: user_id, tenant_id, scopes: ["docs:read", "docs:write"] }
```

**Rationale:** JWT + tenant_id = stateless, horizontal scale. Tenant claim in token prevents user requesting wrong tenant (verify on every request). Scopes over roles — finer control, easier audit.

**Enforcement:** Middleware checks `{tenant_id}` in URL matches token claim. API key alternative for service-to-service (tied to tenant, single scope).

## Pagination

**Cursor-based, not offset:**
```
GET /tenants/{tenant_id}/documents?limit=50&cursor=abc123def456
Response:
{
  "data": [...],
  "cursor": "next_xyz789",
  "has_more": true
}
```

**Rationale:** Offset breaks under concurrent inserts/deletes (skips/dupes). Cursor stable — points past last item, works at scale. Limit not `page_size` — user controls batch size.

**Why not keyset?** Works but cursor is opaque, easier to rotate encoding. Clients don't construct them.

## Versioning

**Immutable versions, mutable HEAD:**
```
POST /tenants/{tenant_id}/documents
  { "title": "...", "content": "..." }
  → { "id": "doc1", "version_id": "v1", "version": 1 }

PATCH /tenants/{tenant_id}/documents/{doc_id}
  { "content": "updated" }
  → { "id": "doc1", "version_id": "v2", "version": 2 }

GET /tenants/{tenant_id}/documents/{doc_id}/versions/{version_id}
  → { immutable snapshot }
```

**Rationale:** Versioning automatic (no client action). Query HEAD for latest. Versions immutable = safe audit trail, no "which version changed" ambiguity. Version ID (v1, v2) + number for sorting.

**Strategy:** New version on any PATCH. Tombstone (soft delete) keeps version history. Hard delete requires explicit endpoint + audit.

## Error Format

```json
{
  "error": {
    "code": "DOCUMENT_NOT_FOUND",
    "message": "Document doc123 does not exist in tenant acme.",
    "status": 404,
    "details": {
      "tenant_id": "acme",
      "doc_id": "doc123",
      "timestamp": "2026-11-23T14:30:00Z"
    },
    "request_id": "req_abc123xyz789"
  }
}
```

**Rationale:** 
- `code` = machine-readable, client switch on it (not message text)
- `message` = human context
- `status` = echo HTTP status (redundant but client-friendly)
- `details` = request context (tenant, resource) + reproducible timestamp
- `request_id` = trace logs, support reference

**HTTP status:**
- `401` — auth missing/invalid
- `403` — auth valid, tenant mismatch or permission denied
- `404` — resource not found
- `409` — conflict (concurrent patch, stale version)
- `422` — validation (bad content format)

---

## Key Decisions Explained

| Decision | Why |
|----------|-----|
| Tenant in URL path | Explicit, auditable. Prevents accidental cross-tenant leaks. |
| Cursor pagination | Stable under mutations. Scales to millions. |
| Automatic versioning | No client coordination. Full history preserved. |
| Immutable versions | Audit log by design. Conflict-free reads. |
| JWT + tenant claim | Stateless. Tenant boundary enforced at middleware. |
| Opaque cursor | No client parsing. Can rotate encoding/strategy without breaking clients. |
| Request ID in errors | Support + debugging. Correlates errors to logs. |
~~~

### ponytail: 1603 answer tokens, 1844 billed, 111 lines

~~~
# REST API for Multi-Tenant Document Store

## Resources & Routes

```
POST   /v1/documents              # Create document
GET    /v1/documents              # List documents (paginated, filtered)
GET    /v1/documents/{id}         # Retrieve document
PUT    /v1/documents/{id}         # Replace document
PATCH  /v1/documents/{id}         # Partial update
DELETE /v1/documents/{id}         # Delete document

GET    /v1/documents/{id}/versions    # List versions
GET    /v1/documents/{id}/versions/{version}  # Get specific version
```

Multi-tenancy is implicit: auth token determines tenant; queries always scoped to that tenant (enforced server-side). No `tenant_id` in URLs — simpler for clients, enforces isolation at the boundary.

## Authentication Model

**Bearer token (JWT or opaque session)** in `Authorization: Bearer <token>`

The token carries:
- `tenant_id` (which tenant owns this token)
- `user_id` (audit trail)
- `permissions` (empty list = read-only by default, explicit grant for write/delete)

No endpoint to list tenants or users — those live outside the API (admin panel, separate service). Keeps the document API focused.

**Why:** JWTs are stateless and scale; opaque tokens let you revoke instantly. Store one or the other based on your revocation needs. Multi-tenant isolation happens in middleware, not in every handler — guard rails, not spray-and-pray ACLs.

## Pagination

```json
GET /v1/documents?limit=20&cursor=abc123&sort=created_at:desc

{
  "data": [ /* 20 documents */ ],
  "pagination": {
    "cursor": "xyz789",  // next page's cursor
    "has_more": true
  }
}
```

**Why cursor over offset:** Offset breaks when documents are added/deleted between requests. Cursor (a bookmark into the sorted result set) is stable and cheap — one indexed lookup. Clients don't think about page numbers; they just follow `pagination.cursor` until `has_more` is false.

## Versioning Strategy

**Immutable history, mutable head.** 

Each document stores:
- `id`: unique ID
- `version`: incrementing int (1, 2, 3…)
- `content`: the document body
- `created_at`, `updated_at`

On update: increment `version`, keep old version in history (separate table or archive column). Never delete, only mark soft-deleted with a `deleted_at` timestamp.

**API versioning:** `/v1/` in the URL path. When you need a breaking change (new required field, renamed endpoint), ship `/v2/`. Both live side-by-side; deprecate `/v1/` over 2–3 months. Clients pin versions explicitly, so they don't break on your release.

**Why:** Document versioning is for audit + restore; API versioning is for safe evolution. Conflate them and you end up with a mess. Soft deletes make restore trivial. Incrementing `version` is simpler than timestamps for optimistic concurrency: `PUT /v1/documents/{id}?expected_version=5` fails if someone else wrote version 6 first.

## Error Format

```json
{
  "error": {
    "code": "CONFLICT",
    "message": "Document was modified. Current version is 7, expected 5.",
    "status": 409,
    "request_id": "req_abc123xyz",
    "details": {
      "field": "version",
      "current_version": 7
    }
  }
}
```

| Code | HTTP | Meaning |
|------|------|---------|
| `NOT_FOUND` | 404 | Document or version doesn't exist |
| `CONFLICT` | 409 | Version mismatch or already deleted |
| `FORBIDDEN` | 403 | No permission (write/delete) |
| `INVALID_REQUEST` | 400 | Malformed query or bad pagination cursor |
| `UNAUTHORIZED` | 401 | Missing or invalid token |
| `INTERNAL_ERROR` | 500 | Server error (log the request_id) |

**Why:** `code` is machine-readable (parse once, handle predictably). `request_id` ties the error back to logs for debugging. `details` varies by error (version mismatch needs `current_version`, validation errors need `field`). HTTP status tells proxies + clients the category instantly. No magic status codes like 418; stick to the standard 50 that everyone knows.

## Key Decisions

| Decision | Why |
|----------|-----|
| **Tenant in token, not URL** | Prevents cross-tenant leaks via typos. Simpler URLs. |
| **Cursor pagination** | Stable under concurrent writes. No offset math. |
| **Versioning in-DB** | Restore and audit queries hit one table. Soft deletes prevent orphans. |
| **Bearer auth (stateless)** | Scales to multiple servers. Revocation varies (JWT = check revocation list; opaque = cache misses = revoke instantly). |
| **Immutable history** | Append-only is auditable, debuggable, cheap. Mutations happen to the latest version only. |
| **No role-based endpoints** | No `/v1/documents/admin/bulk-delete`. Auth is in middleware; permission is in the token. Same endpoints for all users, different responses based on `permissions`. |
| **Versioning in URL path** | Clients don't break on deploy. Ops can run v1 and v2 handlers in parallel. |

## Minimal Example Flow

1. **Create:** `POST /v1/documents` with `{"title": "...", "content": "..."}` → returns `{"id": "doc_123", "version": 1}`.
2. **Update:** `PUT /v1/documents/doc_123?expected_version=1` with new content → returns `{"id": "doc_123", "version": 2}`. If version doesn't match, returns 409 CONFLICT.
3. **List:** `GET /v1/documents?limit=10` → returns 10 docs + cursor for next page.
4. **Restore:** `GET /v1/documents/doc_123/versions/1` → retrieve old version. If the user has permission, POST a copy or PUT the content back.

Constraints: Don't add per-user folders, permission levels, comment threads, or "share with other tenants" until you have a real request. Each feature is a new table, a new migration, a new test, a new place to leak data. Ship the core, expand when you know the shape.
~~~

### chisle: 1690 answer tokens, 2398 billed, 164 lines

~~~
## Multi-Tenant Document Store REST API

**Resources & Routing**

Path-scoped tenancy: `/api/tenants/{tenantId}/documents`. Tenant ID in route makes access control explicit, works with logs/monitoring, sidesteps subdomain complexity. Alternative (header or subdomain) obscures boundaries and complicates CDN/routing.

Documents as primary resource:
- `GET /api/tenants/{tenantId}/documents` — list
- `POST /api/tenants/{tenantId}/documents` — create
- `GET /api/tenants/{tenantId}/documents/{docId}` — read
- `PUT /api/tenants/{tenantId}/documents/{docId}` — full replace (idempotent)
- `PATCH /api/tenants/{tenantId}/documents/{docId}` — partial update
- `DELETE /api/tenants/{tenantId}/documents/{docId}` — soft delete (tombstone)
- `GET /api/tenants/{tenantId}/documents/{docId}/versions` — history
- `GET /api/tenants/{tenantId}/documents/{docId}/versions/{versionId}` — point-in-time
- `POST /api/tenants/{tenantId}/documents/{docId}/restore` — recover deleted/old version

Don't add `/api/tenants/{tenantId}/folders` unless documents *must* nest. Otherwise pagination and permission logic double.

---

**Auth Model**

Bearer token (JWT or opaque session, your choice):
```
Authorization: Bearer {token}
```

Token claims include:
- `sub` — user ID
- `tenant_id` — tenant (verify on every request against path param)
- `scopes` — `["documents:read", "documents:write", "documents:delete", "admin"]`
- `exp`, `iat`, `iss` — standard

Tenant verification is not just routing; it's enforced at token level. Token without matching `tenant_id` → 403 (Forbidden) even if route is syntactically valid. Catch layer violation early.

API key for service-to-service: separate token type with same structure, issued with explicit scopes, revokable per key.

---

**Pagination**

Offset/limit over cursor: simpler API, sufficient for documents (not feeds). Max limit 100, default 20.

```
GET /api/tenants/{tenantId}/documents?offset=0&limit=20
```

Response:
```json
{
  "data": [...],
  "pagination": {
    "offset": 0,
    "limit": 20,
    "total": 150,
    "has_next": true,
    "next_url": "/api/tenants/{tenantId}/documents?offset=20&limit=20"
  }
}
```

Include `total` for clients to know scope. Include `next_url` to avoid client math. If you need immutability (cursor), use: `?cursor=<opaque-string>&limit=20`, clients don't construct it.

---

**Versioning**

Implicit versioning: every write auto-increments `version` field. Clients don't manage it.

```json
{
  "id": "doc-123",
  "title": "...",
  "content": "...",
  "version": 5,
  "created_at": "2026-11-23T10:00:00Z",
  "updated_at": "2026-11-23T10:30:00Z",
  "updated_by": "user-456"
}
```

On update, client can include `version` in body to enforce optimistic locking:
```json
{
  "title": "New title",
  "content": "...",
  "expected_version": 5
}
```

If `expected_version` doesn't match current, return 409 Conflict with new version → client re-fetches and retries.

Versioning endpoint returns shallow list:
```
GET /api/tenants/{tenantId}/documents/{docId}/versions?limit=50
```
```json
{
  "data": [
    {"version": 5, "updated_at": "...", "updated_by": "user-456", "summary": "Changed title"}
  ],
  "pagination": {...}
}
```

Point-in-time:
```
GET /api/tenants/{tenantId}/documents/{docId}/versions/{versionId}
```

Returns full document body at that version. Avoid keeping full history blobs in main response (reduces list size).

Delete: soft delete (set `deleted_at` timestamp, increment version). Restore within 30 days or configurable TTL, then hard-delete in background. Deleted docs excluded from default list (`?include_deleted=false`).

---

**Error Format**

Consistent JSON error envelope:
```json
{
  "error": {
    "code": "CONFLICT",
    "message": "Document version changed; expected 5, found 6",
    "request_id": "req-abc123",
    "details": {
      "current_version": 6,
      "expected_version": 5
    }
  }
}
```

Errors always use this structure. HTTP status code + error code together (redundant on purpose—survives proxies, logs). Common codes:

| HTTP | Code | Scenario |
|------|------|----------|
| 400 | INVALID_REQUEST | Malformed JSON, missing fields |
| 401 | UNAUTHORIZED | Missing/expired token |
| 403 | FORBIDDEN | Token lacks scope or tenant mismatch |
| 404 | NOT_FOUND | Document/version doesn't exist |
| 409 | CONFLICT | Version mismatch or unique constraint |
| 422 | VALIDATION_ERROR | Semantic validation (title too long) |
| 429 | RATE_LIMITED | Quota exceeded (per-tenant rate limit) |
| 500 | INTERNAL_ERROR | Server error (always include request_id for support) |

Include `request_id` in every response header (`X-Request-ID: ...`) and in 5xx errors. Ties logs to customer reports.

---

**Decision Rationale**

**Route over header for tenancy:** Path is discoverable, cacheable, works with webhooks/links. Header hides scope.

**Soft delete:** Documents often need recovery. TTL-based cleanup avoids immediate irrecoverability. Tombstones let audit trails stay intact.

**Offset/limit:** Offset is easier for pagination UIs (page numbers). Cursor is better for consistency during large data shifts, but document stores are usually stable in read-heavy patterns.

**Optimistic locking via version:** Prevents silent data loss on concurrent edits. Alternative (pessimistic locks) adds state complexity; versioning is cheaper.

**Token in auth header:** Standard, works with CORS, CDN-friendly. API keys separate to avoid key rotation on users.

**Error envelope:** Clients parse `code` (machine actionable), `message` (user-facing), `details` (context). Single format reduces handler count.
~~~

### chisle-C: 901 answer tokens, 3044 billed, 67 lines

~~~
## REST API Design: Multi-Tenant Document Store

**Resources structure:**
```
/tenants/{tenantId}/documents
/tenants/{tenantId}/documents/{docId}
/tenants/{tenantId}/documents/{docId}/versions
/tenants/{tenantId}/documents/{docId}/versions/{versionId}
/tenants/{tenantId}/members
```

Tenant-first path enforces isolation at the routing layer—impossible to accidentally leak cross-tenant data. Flat documents (no folders) keeps the API simple; metadata + search replaces hierarchy. Versions are immutable sub-resources, not mutable history.

**Auth model: JWT + tenant ID in header**
```
Authorization: Bearer {jwt_token}
X-Tenant-ID: {tenantId}
```
Token payload: `{userId, tenantId, role, exp}`. Verification asserts `token.tenantId == header tenantId`—catches token replays across tenants. Role (admin/editor/viewer) embedded in token avoids DB lookups for every request. Separating tenant ID from token means a compromised token can't silently hit a different tenant.

**Pagination: cursor-based with sorting**
```
GET /tenants/{tenantId}/documents?limit=50&sort=created_desc&cursor=abc123
```
Cursors survive concurrent inserts/deletes without gaps; offset-based pagination fails when rows are added mid-pagination. Store `{sort_key, sort_direction, last_id}` encoded in cursor. Query as `WHERE (sort_key, id) > (decoded_key, id)` to fetch next batch. Response includes `has_more` and next `cursor`.

**Versioning: Accept header + document restore**

API versioning via Accept header:
```
Accept: application/vnd.company.v2+json
```
Fallback to `/v2/documents/{docId}` for curl. Major version only (v1 → v2 → v3) on breaking changes; deprecate old versions after 6 months.

Document versions are immutable. `PUT /documents/{docId}/versions/{versionId}/restore` creates a *new* version rather than mutating history. Store full snapshots or deltas every N revisions to control storage.

**Error format: machine-readable + debugging context**
```json
{
  "error": {
    "code": "DOCUMENT_NOT_FOUND",
    "message": "Document abc123 not found in tenant xyz",
    "status": 404,
    "details": {"doc_id": "abc123", "tenant_id": "xyz"},
    "request_id": "req_12345"
  }
}
```

Standard codes: `INVALID_PAYLOAD` (400), `UNAUTHORIZED` (401, token missing), `FORBIDDEN` (403, insufficient role), `DOCUMENT_NOT_FOUND` (404), `VERSION_CONFLICT` (409, optimistic lock failed), `RATE_LIMITED` (429), `INTERNAL_ERROR` (500). Request ID ties errors to server logs.

**Example: optimistic locking on update**
```
PUT /tenants/{tenantId}/documents/{docId}
{
  "content": "...",
  "if_version": 3
}
```
Return 409 if current version ≠ 3, with actual version in response. Clients retry with correct version or handle the conflict.

**Key decisions:**
- Tenant in path + header token prevents both routing bugs and token-replay attacks.
- Cursor pagination handles high-churn workloads.
- Immutable versions trade storage for audit safety and data recovery.
- Accept-header versioning avoids URL sprawl; URL fallback for tooling.
- Distinct 401/403 codes let clients distinguish "fix your auth" from "ask for permission."
~~~

## large-s1 / architecture

> Explain how you would migrate a monolith to services without a big-bang rewrite, covering ordering, data ownership, and how to keep it shippable throughout.

### vanilla: 1507 answer tokens, 1712 billed, 99 lines

~~~
# Migrating a Monolith to Services Incrementally

## High-Level Philosophy

The key is the **strangler fig pattern**: instead of replacing the monolith, you wrap it with new service boundaries and gradually route traffic away from it. The monolith stays running and deployable throughout. You're not rewriting—you're extracting.

## Ordering: What to Extract First

**Start with these characteristics:**

1. **Low-coupling, high-cohesion modules** — Look for feature areas with few dependencies into the monolith's core. Auth sidecars, background jobs, or reporting engines are often good first targets. Avoid extracting the transaction-heavy business logic center.

2. **Clear data seam** — Can you identify a table or data set that *only this module* really owns? Extract that first. Modules that touch many shared tables are harder because you have to solve the data-ownership problem before you ship.

3. **No backward compatibility pressure** — Early services should have fewer downstream consumers. If 50 internal services depend on something, the extraction cost is higher.

4. **Encapsulated already** — If the monolith has good internal module boundaries, extract those boundaries as services. If everything is spaghetti, extract smaller things first (3-5 person-week chunks), or refactor internally before splitting.

**Example sequencing:**
- Notifications/email service (read-only externally, owns nothing but its own queue)
- Search indexing (extract from main request path)
- Payment processing (owns its own domain, isolated)
- Then account/user logic (higher coupling, comes later)

## Data Ownership: The Hard Problem

**Three patterns:**

1. **Shared database (temporary)** — Early services share the monolith's database. This is not microservices yet, but it unblocks extraction. Use this as a bridge: the service owns certain *tables*, not rows. The monolith reads through APIs for those tables, not directly.
   - Pro: Deploy quickly, no data migration yet.
   - Con: Binds services; scaling becomes hard; schema changes require coordination.

2. **Data duplication via change log** — The monolith publishes events (via database polling, Kafka, or a WAL stream) of changes. Services subscribe and maintain their own read copies. The service-of-truth writes to a dedicated table in its own schema; the monolith reads the published data.
   - Pro: Loose coupling, services can scale independently.
   - Con: Consistency is eventual; debugging is harder.

3. **Strangler queries** — The monolith routes queries to the new service through an API instead of local database reads. The new service owns the data. Requires a temporary API layer in the monolith.
   - Pro: Single source of truth quickly.
   - Con: Latency across service boundary; requires the new service to be stable.

**How to execute:**
- Start with shared DB + ownership boundaries (table or schema ownership).
- As services stabilize, migrate to change events if they need independent scaling.
- Accept that early services will have cross-service consistency delays; that's okay if you've chosen features where it matters less.

## Keeping It Shippable: The Architecture That Matters

**1. API Gateway / Strangler Layer**
- Route requests: if the path maps to a new service, go there; else hit the monolith.
- Monolith doesn't know about services; services don't hardcode monolith endpoints.
- This is your "seam"—it stays shallow so you can change routing without touching core logic.

**2. Canary/Feature Flags, Not Big Cutoffs**
- New service handles 5% of traffic first. Monitor for 48 hours.
- Ramp to 20%, then 50%, then 100%.
- Fallback is a flag flip, not a rollback (though you can do both).
- Keeps deployments small and reversible.

**3. Monolith Stays Deployable**
- Extracting a service should not require a monolith deployment.
- Changes to the monolith to support a new service (e.g., event publishing) ship before the service does.
- Use feature flags *inside* the monolith if needed: "publish events for order service" can be off until the service is ready.

**4. Dual Writes During Transition**
- When migrating a feature: monolith writes to both old location and new service API.
- New service is read-only initially. Monolith reads old location, ignores new service.
- Once new service is validated, flip the flag: monolith reads from new service.
- If new service fails, monolith falls back to old reads without code change.

**5. Database Migration Happens Separately**
- Extract the *logic* first, leave data where it is (shared DB or replicated).
- Schema migrations in the monolith are not coupled to service deployments.
- Once services are stable (weeks or months in), migrate data as a separate project.

## Practical Sequencing Example

**Week 1–2:** Extract notifications service. Monolith publishes events to it; notifications service writes to its own database. Monolith still sends emails directly (as fallback).

**Week 3–4:** Route 10% of email sends to new service via API gateway. Monitor.

**Week 5–8:** Scale to 100%. Monolith stops sending directly; all goes through service. Old email code stays (turned off).

**Month 2:** Extract reporting. Same pattern. By now, you have the rhythm.

**Month 3–6:** Extract higher-risk domains (payments, accounts). You have practice and patterns.

**Month 6+:** Database migrations, cleanup, decommission monolith pieces.

## Key Trade-Offs

| Approach | Speed | Complexity | Risk |
|----------|-------|-----------|------|
| Shared DB + API strangler | Fast (weeks) | Low | Low—revert by routing |
| Change events + duplication | Medium (months) | Medium | Medium—eventual consistency bugs |
| Immediate data migration | Slow (months) | High | High—data loss possible |

**Principle:** Ship the service before you migrate its data. Keep the monolith as the fallback for 2–3 deployments of the new service. Only after it's proven do you decouple data.

The win is that the system is always in a runnable state, every deployment unblocks the next one, and you can pause or slow down without everything breaking.
~~~

### caveman: 992 answer tokens, 1249 billed, 84 lines

~~~
## Strangler Pattern: Gradual Replacement

**Core idea**: Wrap monolith with facade layer. New requests route to services; old requests hit monolith. Gradually move endpoints, keeping system live.

---

## Ordering (What to Extract First)

**Start with low-coupling, high-pain boundaries:**

1. **Independent domains first** — services with minimal cross-monolith calls. Example: auth, logging, file storage, notifications.
2. **High-change areas next** — parts teams touch frequently. Extracting them unblocks parallel work faster than waiting.
3. **Data-heavy reads last** — big tables need careful migration; extract compute-heavy services first.

**Avoid**: Extracting tightly-coupled core business logic early. You'll spend months on internal refactoring.

---

## Data Ownership (Avoid Shared DB Horror)

**Rule: Each service owns its tables. No direct cross-service queries.**

Setup:
- Service A gets `users`, `profiles` tables → only A queries them
- Service B needs user data → calls A's API
- Duplication OK early. Denormalize when needed (cache via event log)

**How to split existing schema:**

1. **Identify entity ownership** — which tables logically belong to which service?
   - Users service: `users`, `auth_tokens`, `sessions`
   - Orders service: `orders`, `order_items`, `payments`

2. **Write data via service API only** — monolith still exists; both monolith and service write to service's DB (dual-write phase). Backfill service DB from monolith.

3. **Event log for reads** — if service B needs to know "order created", service A publishes to event stream. Service B consumes, caches locally if needed. This beats RPC for every read.

4. **Eventually retire dual-write** — once service handles all writes, monolith becomes read-only for that domain.

---

## Keeping It Shippable

**Ship every few days:**

1. **Feature flag facade routes** — Add routing logic behind flags. Roll out 1% → 10% → 100% traffic to new service. Flip back if broken.

2. **Bidirectional sync during transition** — write to both monolith + new service until cutover. Read from new service (via flag), fallback to monolith. Catches mismatches early.

3. **Contract tests over end-to-end** — service's API has contract tests (what monolith expects). Catch breaking changes before they ship.

4. **Canary deploys** — new service handles 5% of traffic first. Monitor latency/errors vs. monolith. Ship to 100% only once stable.

5. **Rollback automation** — if error rate spikes, traffic routes back to monolith automatically.

---

## Example Flow

**Week 1–2**: Extract auth service
- Create `auth-service` repo. Move login/token endpoints.
- Monolith still owns `users` table (read-only after auth-service writes).
- Route: `POST /login` → flag `use_auth_service` → 10% hit auth-service, 90% hit monolith.
- Both write to users DB (dual-write).

**Week 3**: Ship to 100%. Auth service now owns auth.

**Week 4–6**: Extract orders service
- Orders service gets `orders`, `order_items` tables.
- Monolith continues writing to them (dual-write). Orders service reads from monolith's events.
- Route orders endpoints, canary to 20%.

**Week 7+**: Roll out more services in parallel. Teams work independently.

---

## Why This Works

- **Low risk per step** — one service extracted, easy to rollback.
- **Data consistency** — dual-write + event log keep monolith and service in sync.
- **Teams unblocked early** — after 2–3 services extracted, teams can own services independently.
- **No big-bang cutover** — no "migration weekend" where everything breaks. Incremental, observable.

**Main cost**: temporary complexity (dual-write, event logs, feature flags). But it's worth avoiding a 6-month rewrite where nothing ships.
~~~

### ponytail: 925 answer tokens, 1153 billed, 65 lines

~~~
# Incremental Monolith Migration

## Ordering: Extract the Easiest First

Start with services that have **fewest dependencies**:
1. **Leaves** (called but don't call back): logging, auth, payment processors. Zero risk of circular dependencies.
2. **Low-fan-in** (few callers): move things few parts of the monolith touch. Risk of missing a caller is lower.
3. **Avoid** high-fan-in core logic (e.g. user model, permissions) until you have the pattern locked down.

Extract the boring, isolated thing first. You'll ship a real service and learn the deployment, monitoring, and rollback machinery without betting the product on it.

## The Strangler Pattern

Don't delete the monolith. Wrap it:

```
Clients → Router (new)
            ├─→ Service A (extracted)
            └─→ Monolith (remaining)
```

Write a router (nginx, your app's entry point, API gateway—pick one) that:
- Routes specific paths/calls to the new service
- Routes everything else to the monolith
- Logs misroutes so you catch forgotten callers

Each service becomes a new route. The monolith shrinks as services grow. If a service breaks, re-route to monolith in 30 seconds—no revert needed.

## Data: Dual-Write, Then Migrate

**Never** fork the database on day one:

1. **Dual-write**: service writes its own tables *and* updates monolith tables (or message queue → monolith writes). Both stay in sync.
2. **Backfill**: batch-migrate existing data, verify checksums.
3. **Monitor**: run both reads in parallel, log mismatches.
4. **Switch**: cut reads to the service DB, keep dual-write for a grace period.
5. **Decommission**: remove dual-write, retire monolith's copy of that data.

This is tedious. That's the point—it's safe. The monolith stays the source of truth until you're certain the service is authoritative.

Skipped: distributed transactions—they're a trap. Each service owns its data. Cross-service calls are async or eventually consistent (read from service A, it tells you to read from service B). The monolith's transactional DB already broke that rule; services stop breaking it further.

## Keeping It Shippable

**Branch per service**: each extraction is a branch. At every commit:
- Monolith + new service both deploy
- Router knows about both
- Tests run against both (dual-write correctness matters)
- Rollback is re-route

**Feature-flag the extraction**: behind a flag, route a small % of traffic to the new service. 1% → 10% → 50% → 100%. Real clients, real load. Roll back if latency ticks up.

**Shared database initially is fine**: the first service often shares the monolith's DB. The extraction is *logical*, not physical. Query cost stays the same. Cross-service data ownership happens later—once you know who queries what.

**Ship every sprint**: each service extraction is 1–2 weeks of work (extract, dual-write, backfill, test, canary, monitor). Every 2 weeks the prod system is different and working.

## The One Hard Part

**Ordering dependencies**: if service A talks to service B talks to C, extract C first. If you extract A first, it has an RPC dependency on a service that doesn't exist yet—you have to change A's code later anyway (or add a synchronous RPC call, which couples them, which defeats the point).

Reverse-dependency order: leaves first, then work inward. Tool: draw a dependency graph, find the leaf nodes, extract those.

---

Skipped: event sourcing, saga orchestration, distributed tracing—add when the monolith's data consistency or observability actually breaks. YAGNI applies to the architecture too.
~~~

### chisle: 495 answer tokens, 762 billed, 9 lines

~~~
**Core pattern: strangler fig.** New services intercept requests bound for the monolith, handling some cases, forwarding others back. Monolith keeps shrinking, never dies abruptly. This keeps the system shippable at every step—no "migration branch that breaks for 3 months."

**Ordering by dependency, not business logic.** Extract leaf services first (no other services depend on them): logging, auth, payments, search. These have clear contracts and low fanout. Extract services that *call* many things last—they need stable APIs below them. Within a layer, start with high-churn domains (ones you're rewriting anyway) or high-value ones (they justify the extraction cost). Don't extract because it's "clean architecture" if nothing's driving it; extraction is a tax.

**Data ownership is the trap.** Monolith has one database; services need their own. Strangler pattern buys you time: the service can read *from* the monolith's DB initially (denormalized copies via event streams or sync reads), but *writes* go through the service only. This breaks the coupling gradually. Eventually move to event sourcing or dual writes (monolith writes to its DB *and* publishes events; service consumes). Dual writes are risky (distributed transaction failure) but simpler than rebuilding the data model. Pick the path based on how mission-critical the domain is.

**Keeping it shippable:** Feature flags on service calls. If the new service fails, flag it off and fall through to monolith—takes seconds, no rollback needed. Add observability *first* (tracing, latency metrics, error rates)—you're flying blind otherwise. Ship the extraction *before* optimization; a slow new service beating a buggy monolith is progress. Use the strangler to run A/B tests: route 10% of traffic to the new service, measure, grow.

**The gotcha:** migrations take 3–5× longer than greenfield builds because you're carrying legacy state, schema, and traffic simultaneously. Plan for that. The win: you ship continuously, so stakeholders see progress, and you can pivot if a service turns out badly—just don't extract it.
~~~

### chisle-C: 446 answer tokens, 950 billed, 11 lines

~~~
**Start with depended-on services, not dependents.** Extract things that many parts call but don't call much themselves (payment processor, auth, cache layer). Leaves monolith mostly intact; existing code keeps working. Reverse the dependency arrow gradually—don't pull out the core business logic first.

**Data: dual-write, then read-shift, then write-shift.** New service gets its own database. Monolith writes to both old and new. Gradually shift reads to the new DB (with feature flag per request or gradual traffic percentage). Once reads prove stable, migrate writes—monolith still writes, but to new service, not old table. Then kill the old schema. This avoids consistency nightmares and gives rollback windows at each step.

**Use a strangler pattern at the API boundary.** Router or proxy intercepts requests before they hit monolith. Routes known operations to extracted service; unknown ones still go to monolith. Monolith shrinks as services take over, but it never crashes—just handles fewer things. Each service ships independently; routing decisions are config, not code.

**Ordering within services:** Break cycles first. Find the dependency graph, pull out the leaves. Usually: data/cache services → utility services → domain services → UI/API orchestration last. Dependency inversion helps: monolith calls new service; new service doesn't call monolith (except maybe for auth or config initially).

**Consistency and rollback:** Each extraction is behind a flag. Traffic can shift back to monolith instantly if new service errors. Tests run both paths in parallel during transition. Database migrations are forward/backward compatible—new code reads old schema, old code tolerates new columns.

**Shippable = small, bounded, reversible.** Each PR extracts one seam, moves it to its own process, and ships. Monolith is still main app. Takes months or years; that's the point—no heroic rewrites, no "we're dark for three weeks."
~~~

## large-s1 / authflow

> Implement email-and-password signup and login with password hashing, session issuing, and rate limiting on failed attempts. Show the code.

### vanilla: 2315 answer tokens, 2631 billed, 274 lines

~~~
I'll create a complete, production-ready authentication system with signup, login, password hashing, session management, and rate limiting.

```javascript
// server.js
const express = require('express');
const bcrypt = require('bcrypt');
const jwt = require('jsonwebtoken');
const sqlite3 = require('sqlite3').verbose();
const path = require('path');

const app = express();
app.use(express.json());

// Configuration
const JWT_SECRET = process.env.JWT_SECRET || 'your-secret-key-change-in-production';
const JWT_EXPIRY = '24h';
const SALT_ROUNDS = 10;
const RATE_LIMIT_MAX_ATTEMPTS = 5;
const RATE_LIMIT_WINDOW_MS = 15 * 60 * 1000; // 15 minutes

// Database setup
const db = new sqlite3.Database(':memory:');

db.serialize(() => {
  db.run(`
    CREATE TABLE users (
      id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY AUTOINCREMENT,
      email TEXT UNIQUE NOT NULL,
      password_hash TEXT NOT NULL,
      created_at DATETIME DEFAULT CURRENT_TIMESTAMP
    )
  `);

  db.run(`
    CREATE TABLE failed_logins (
      id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY AUTOINCREMENT,
      email TEXT NOT NULL,
      attempt_time INTEGER NOT NULL
    )
  `);
});

// Rate limiting: track failed login attempts in memory
const failedAttempts = new Map(); // email -> [timestamps]

function recordFailedAttempt(email) {
  const now = Date.now();
  if (!failedAttempts.has(email)) {
    failedAttempts.set(email, []);
  }
  
  const attempts = failedAttempts.get(email);
  // Remove attempts outside the window
  const recentAttempts = attempts.filter(t => now - t < RATE_LIMIT_WINDOW_MS);
  recentAttempts.push(now);
  failedAttempts.set(email, recentAttempts);
  
  return recentAttempts.length;
}

function checkRateLimit(email) {
  const now = Date.now();
  const attempts = failedAttempts.get(email) || [];
  const recentAttempts = attempts.filter(t => now - t < RATE_LIMIT_WINDOW_MS);
  
  return {
    isLimited: recentAttempts.length >= RATE_LIMIT_MAX_ATTEMPTS,
    attempts: recentAttempts.length,
    remainingTime: recentAttempts.length > 0 
      ? Math.ceil((recentAttempts[0] + RATE_LIMIT_WINDOW_MS - now) / 1000)
      : 0
  };
}

function clearFailedAttempts(email) {
  failedAttempts.delete(email);
}

// Middleware to verify JWT token
function verifyToken(req, res, next) {
  const token = req.headers.authorization?.split(' ')[1];
  
  if (!token) {
    return res.status(401).json({ error: 'No token provided' });
  }
  
  try {
    const decoded = jwt.verify(token, JWT_SECRET);
    req.user = decoded;
    next();
  } catch (error) {
    res.status(401).json({ error: 'Invalid or expired token' });
  }
}

// SIGNUP endpoint
app.post('/signup', async (req, res) => {
  const { email, password } = req.body;
  
  // Validation
  if (!email || !password) {
    return res.status(400).json({ error: 'Email and password are required' });
  }
  
  if (password.length < 8) {
    return res.status(400).json({ error: 'Password must be at least 8 characters' });
  }
  
  try {
    // Hash password
    const passwordHash = await bcrypt.hash(password, SALT_ROUNDS);
    
    // Insert user
    db.run(
      'INSERT INTO users (email, password_hash) VALUES (?, ?)',
      [email, passwordHash],
      function(err) {
        if (err) {
          if (err.message.includes('UNIQUE constraint failed')) {
            return res.status(409).json({ error: 'Email already exists' });
          }
          return res.status(500).json({ error: 'Database error' });
        }
        
        res.status(201).json({
          message: 'User created successfully',
          userId: this.lastID,
          email: email
        });
      }
    );
  } catch (error) {
    res.status(500).json({ error: 'Server error' });
  }
});

// LOGIN endpoint
app.post('/login', async (req, res) => {
  const { email, password } = req.body;
  
  if (!email || !password) {
    return res.status(400).json({ error: 'Email and password are required' });
  }
  
  // Check rate limit
  const rateLimitStatus = checkRateLimit(email);
  if (rateLimitStatus.isLimited) {
    return res.status(429).json({
      error: `Too many failed attempts. Try again in ${rateLimitStatus.remainingTime} seconds`
    });
  }
  
  try {
    // Find user
    db.get(
      'SELECT id, email, password_hash FROM users WHERE email = ?',
      [email],
      async (err, user) => {
        if (err) {
          return res.status(500).json({ error: 'Database error' });
        }
        
        // User not found or password incorrect
        if (!user) {
          recordFailedAttempt(email);
          return res.status(401).json({ error: 'Invalid email or password' });
        }
        
        // Verify password
        const isValidPassword = await bcrypt.compare(password, user.password_hash);
        
        if (!isValidPassword) {
          const attempts = recordFailedAttempt(email);
          return res.status(401).json({ 
            error: 'Invalid email or password',
            attemptsRemaining: RATE_LIMIT_MAX_ATTEMPTS - attempts
          });
        }
        
        // Clear failed attempts on successful login
        clearFailedAttempts(email);
        
        // Generate JWT token
        const token = jwt.sign(
          { userId: user.id, email: user.email },
          JWT_SECRET,
          { expiresIn: JWT_EXPIRY }
        );
        
        res.json({
          message: 'Login successful',
          token: token,
          expiresIn: JWT_EXPIRY
        });
      }
    );
  } catch (error) {
    res.status(500).json({ error: 'Server error' });
  }
});

// Protected endpoint (example)
app.get('/profile', verifyToken, (req, res) => {
  res.json({
    message: 'User profile',
    user: req.user
  });
});

// Error handling middleware
app.use((err, req, res, next) => {
  console.error(err);
  res.status(500).json({ error: 'Internal server error' });
});

const PORT = process.env.PORT || 3000;
app.listen(PORT, () => {
  console.log(`Server running on port ${PORT}`);
});
```

```json
// package.json
{
  "name": "auth-system",
  "version": "1.0.0",
  "type": "module",
  "dependencies": {
    "express": "^4.18.2",
    "bcrypt": "^5.1.1",
    "jsonwebtoken": "^9.1.2",
    "sqlite3": "^5.1.6"
  },
  "scripts": {
    "start": "node server.js",
    "dev": "nodemon server.js"
  }
}
```

**Key features:**

1. **Password Hashing**: Uses bcrypt with 10 salt rounds (industry standard)
2. **Session Management**: JWT tokens with 24-hour expiry
3. **Rate Limiting**: 5 failed attempts per 15 minutes per email
4. **Input Validation**: Email/password requirements, minimum password length
5. **Error Handling**: Distinct error messages (without leaking whether email exists)
6. **Protected Routes**: Example `/profile` endpoint requires valid token

**Usage:**

```bash
# Install
npm install

# Run
node server.js

# Signup
curl -X POST http://localhost:3000/signup \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d '{"email":"user@example.com","password":"securepass123"}'

# Login
curl -X POST http://localhost:3000/login \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d '{"email":"user@example.com","password":"securepass123"}'

# Access protected route
curl http://localhost:3000/profile \
  -H "Authorization: Bearer <token>"
```

For production, add: environment variables for `JWT_SECRET`, persistent database (PostgreSQL), HTTPS enforcement, CORS configuration, and password reset flow.

# Email-Password Auth Implementation

Complete Node.js/Express auth system with hashing, sessions, rate limiting:

```javascript
// auth-system.js
const express = require('express');
const bcrypt = require('bcrypt');
const jwt = require('jsonwebtoken');
const app = express();

app.use(express.json());

const SECRET = process.env.JWT_SECRET || 'dev-secret-key-change-in-prod';
const SALT_ROUNDS = 10;

// In-memory user store (use real DB in production)
const users = new Map();

// Rate limiting: IP → { attempts, resetTime }
const failedAttempts = new Map();
const MAX_ATTEMPTS = 5;
const LOCKOUT_DURATION_MS = 15 * 60 * 1000; // 15 min

// Session store: token → { userId, expiresAt }
const sessions = new Map();
const SESSION_DURATION_MS = 7 * 24 * 60 * 60 * 1000; // 7 days

// --- Rate Limiting ---
function checkRateLimit(ip) {
  const record = failedAttempts.get(ip);
  if (!record) return true;
  
  const now = Date.now();
  if (now > record.resetTime) {
    failedAttempts.delete(ip);
    return true;
  }
  
  return record.attempts < MAX_ATTEMPTS;
}

function recordFailedAttempt(ip) {
  const record = failedAttempts.get(ip) || { attempts: 0, resetTime: Date.now() + LOCKOUT_DURATION_MS };
  record.attempts++;
  failedAttempts.set(ip, record);
}

function clearFailedAttempts(ip) {
  failedAttempts.delete(ip);
}

// --- Session Management ---
function issueSession(userId) {
  const expiresAt = Date.now() + SESSION_DURATION_MS;
  const token = jwt.sign({ userId, expiresAt }, SECRET, { expiresIn: '7d' });
  sessions.set(token, { userId, expiresAt });
  return token;
}

function verifySession(token) {
  try {
    const decoded = jwt.verify(token, SECRET);
    const session = sessions.get(token);
    if (!session || session.expiresAt < Date.now()) {
      sessions.delete(token);
      return null;
    }
    return decoded.userId;
  } catch (err) {
    return null;
  }
}

function revokeSession(token) {
  sessions.delete(token);
}

// --- Middleware ---
function authRequired(req, res, next) {
  const authHeader = req.headers.authorization;
  if (!authHeader?.startsWith('Bearer ')) {
    return res.status(401).json({ error: 'Missing token' });
  }
  
  const token = authHeader.slice(7);
  const userId = verifySession(token);
  if (!userId) {
    return res.status(401).json({ error: 'Invalid or expired token' });
  }
  
  req.userId = userId;
  req.token = token;
  next();
}

// --- Signup ---
app.post('/signup', async (req, res) => {
  try {
    const { email, password } = req.body;
    
    if (!email || !password) {
      return res.status(400).json({ error: 'Email and password required' });
    }
    
    if (users.has(email)) {
      return res.status(409).json({ error: 'Email already registered' });
    }
    
    const hashedPassword = await bcrypt.hash(password, SALT_ROUNDS);
    const userId = Date.now().toString(); // Simple ID generation
    
    users.set(email, { userId, hashedPassword });
    
    const token = issueSession(userId);
    res.status(201).json({ userId, token, expiresIn: SESSION_DURATION_MS });
  } catch (err) {
    res.status(500).json({ error: 'Signup failed' });
  }
});

// --- Login ---
app.post('/login', async (req, res) => {
  try {
    const ip = req.ip;
    const { email, password } = req.body;
    
    if (!email || !password) {
      return res.status(400).json({ error: 'Email and password required' });
    }
    
    // Check rate limit
    if (!checkRateLimit(ip)) {
      return res.status(429).json({ error: 'Too many failed attempts. Try again later.' });
    }
    
    const user = users.get(email);
    if (!user) {
      recordFailedAttempt(ip);
      return res.status(401).json({ error: 'Invalid email or password' });
    }
    
    const passwordMatch = await bcrypt.compare(password, user.hashedPassword);
    if (!passwordMatch) {
      recordFailedAttempt(ip);
      return res.status(401).json({ error: 'Invalid email or password' });
    }
    
    // Success: clear rate limit and issue session
    clearFailedAttempts(ip);
    const token = issueSession(user.userId);
    res.json({ userId: user.userId, token, expiresIn: SESSION_DURATION_MS });
  } catch (err) {
    res.status(500).json({ error: 'Login failed' });
  }
});

// --- Logout ---
app.post('/logout', authRequired, (req, res) => {
  revokeSession(req.token);
  res.json({ message: 'Logged out' });
});

// --- Protected Route Example ---
app.get('/profile', authRequired, (req, res) => {
  res.json({ userId: req.userId, message: 'This is your profile' });
});

app.listen(3000, () => console.log('Server running on port 3000'));
```

## Key features:

| Feature | Implementation |
|---------|---|
| **Password hashing** | bcrypt with 10 salt rounds |
| **Session tokens** | JWT signed, stored with expiry (7 days) |
| **Rate limiting** | 5 failed attempts → 15 min lockout per IP |
| **Signup** | Auto-login, deduplicate email |
| **Login** | Constant-time comparison (bcrypt), clear rate limit on success |
| **Logout** | Token revocation |
| **Protected routes** | `authRequired` middleware checks token validity |

## Production notes:

- Replace in-memory stores with database (PostgreSQL, MongoDB)
- Use Redis for distributed rate limiting + session store
- Add password validation (min length, complexity)
- Hash email to avoid leaking in logs
- Set `Secure` and `HttpOnly` flags if using cookies instead of Bearer tokens
- Monitor session cleanup (expired tokens eventually bloat memory)

# Email-Password Auth with Rate Limiting

Here's a minimal, working implementation in Flask:

```python
from flask import Flask, request, jsonify
import hashlib
import secrets
import time
from functools import wraps

app = Flask(__name__)

# In-memory storage (use DB in production)
users = {}  # {email: {'password_hash': str, 'password_salt': str}}
sessions = {}  # {token: {'email': str, 'created_at': float}}
failed_attempts = {}  # {email: [timestamp, ...]}

RATE_LIMIT_ATTEMPTS = 5
RATE_LIMIT_WINDOW = 15 * 60  # 15 minutes
SESSION_EXPIRY = 24 * 60 * 60  # 24 hours

def hash_password(password, salt=None):
    """PBKDF2-SHA256 with 100k iterations (stdlib only)."""
    if salt is None:
        salt = secrets.token_hex(32)
    hash_obj = hashlib.pbkdf2_hmac(
        'sha256',
        password.encode(),
        bytes.fromhex(salt),
        100000
    )
    return hash_obj.hex(), salt

def verify_password(password, password_hash, salt):
    computed_hash, _ = hash_password(password, salt)
    return computed_hash == password_hash

def is_rate_limited(email):
    """Check if email is rate limited; purge stale attempts."""
    now = time.time()
    if email not in failed_attempts:
        return False
    
    # Keep only recent attempts
    failed_attempts[email] = [t for t in failed_attempts[email] 
                               if now - t < RATE_LIMIT_WINDOW]
    return len(failed_attempts[email]) >= RATE_LIMIT_ATTEMPTS

def record_failed_attempt(email):
    if email not in failed_attempts:
        failed_attempts[email] = []
    failed_attempts[email].append(time.time())

def clear_failed_attempts(email):
    failed_attempts[email] = []

def require_auth(f):
    @wraps(f)
    def decorated(*args, **kwargs):
        token = request.headers.get('Authorization', '').replace('Bearer ', '')
        
        if not token or token not in sessions:
            return jsonify({'error': 'Unauthorized'}), 401
        
        session = sessions[token]
        if time.time() - session['created_at'] > SESSION_EXPIRY:
            del sessions[token]
            return jsonify({'error': 'Session expired'}), 401
        
        request.email = session['email']
        return f(*args, **kwargs)
    return decorated

@app.route('/signup', methods=['POST'])
def signup():
    data = request.json or {}
    email = data.get('email', '').strip().lower()
    password = data.get('password', '')
    
    if not email or '@' not in email:
        return jsonify({'error': 'Valid email required'}), 400
    if len(password) < 8:
        return jsonify({'error': 'Password must be 8+ characters'}), 400
    if email in users:
        return jsonify({'error': 'Email already exists'}), 400
    
    password_hash, salt = hash_password(password)
    users[email] = {'password_hash': password_hash, 'password_salt': salt}
    
    return jsonify({'message': 'Signup successful'}), 201

@app.route('/login', methods=['POST'])
def login():
    data = request.json or {}
    email = data.get('email', '').strip().lower()
    password = data.get('password', '')
    
    if not email or not password:
        return jsonify({'error': 'Email and password required'}), 400
    
    # Rate limit check before lookup (avoid timing leak)
    if is_rate_limited(email):
        return jsonify({'error': 'Too many failed attempts. Try again later.'}), 429
    
    if email not in users or not verify_password(password, users[email]['password_hash'], users[email]['password_salt']):
        record_failed_attempt(email)
        return jsonify({'error': 'Invalid credentials'}), 401
    
    clear_failed_attempts(email)
    token = secrets.token_urlsafe(32)
    sessions[token] = {'email': email, 'created_at': time.time()}
    
    return jsonify({'token': token}), 200

@app.route('/profile', methods=['GET'])
@require_auth
def profile():
    return jsonify({'email': request.email}), 200

@app.route('/logout', methods=['POST'])
@require_auth
def logout():
    token = request.headers.get('Authorization', '').replace('Bearer ', '')
    sessions.pop(token, None)
    return jsonify({'message': 'Logged out'}), 200

if __name__ == '__main__':
    app.run(debug=False)
```

**What's here:**
- **Password hashing:** PBKDF2-SHA256 (stdlib), 100k iterations, random salt per user
- **Sessions:** Opaque tokens via `secrets`, stored with creation time, 24h expiry
- **Rate limiting:** 5 failed attempts per email in 15-min window, purges old attempts on check
- **Routes:** /signup, /login (returns token), /profile (auth required), /logout

**Usage:**
```bash
curl -X POST http://localhost:5000/signup \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d '{"email":"user@example.com","password":"securepass123"}'

curl -X POST http://localhost:5000/login \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d '{"email":"user@example.com","password":"securepass123"}'

curl http://localhost:5000/profile \
  -H "Authorization: Bearer <token>"
```

**Skipped:** Database (use SQLAlchemy + PostgreSQL), email verification, password reset, CSRF tokens (add if serving HTML), HTTPS enforcement (add in production), token refresh. Add when users are real.
