# Multi-Tenant Document Store REST API

## Resources & Endpoints

```
POST   /v1/tenants/{tenantId}/documents          Create
GET    /v1/tenants/{tenantId}/documents          List (paginated)
GET    /v1/tenants/{tenantId}/documents/{docId}  Fetch
PATCH  /v1/tenants/{tenantId}/documents/{docId}  Update
DELETE /v1/tenants/{tenantId}/documents/{docId}  Delete
GET    /v1/tenants/{tenantId}/documents/{docId}/versions  Version history
GET    /v1/tenants/{tenantId}/documents/{docId}/versions/{versionId}  Fetch specific version
```

**Decision:** Tenant ID in URL path, not subdomain or header. Keeps logging/routing explicit, avoids subdomain DNS ops, works for all clients. Alternative (header-only) risks tenant leakage if routing fails silently.

---

## Authentication Model

**Scheme:** Bearer token (JWT or opaque), validated per request.

```
Authorization: Bearer <token>
```

**Token contains or resolves to:**
- `tenantId` (embedded in JWT) or lookup via token store
- `userId` / `actor` (who is making the request)
- `permissions` scope (e.g., `documents:read`, `documents:write`)
- `exp` timestamp

**Multi-tenant isolation check:** Every endpoint verifies the token's `tenantId` matches the URL's `{tenantId}`. Prevents one tenant from accessing another's data even with valid auth.

**Decision:** JWT (stateless) over opaque tokens for scaling; decode server-side to extract tenantId and validate it matches the route. If using opaque tokens, store in Redis keyed by token hash → tenantId for O(1) lookup.

---

## Pagination

**Query params:**
```
GET /v1/tenants/{tenantId}/documents?limit=20&cursor=abc123&sortBy=createdAt&sortOrder=desc
```

**Response:**
```json
{
  "data": [ /* documents */ ],
  "pagination": {
    "cursor": "next_cursor_xyz",
    "hasMore": true,
    "count": 20
  }
}
```

**Decision:** Cursor-based (opaque string), not offset. Offset breaks when documents are inserted/deleted mid-pagination (user sees duplicates or misses items). Cursor is stable and O(1) to encode/decode. Store `{tenantId, lastDocId, sortFields}` in cursor to reconstruct query without server state.

---

## Versioning (API)

**Strategy:** URL path versioning (`/v1`, `/v2`).

**Decision:** Path is explicit in logs, URLs, and client code. Breaking changes → new major version; clients can migrate gradually. Avoid header versioning (`Accept: application/vnd.api+json;version=1`) — less visible, harder to debug.

**Deprecation:** Add `Deprecation: true` and `Sunset: <date>` headers to v1 responses 6 months before EOL.

---

## Document Versioning (Stored Versions)

**On PATCH, store immutable version:**

```json
{
  "id": "doc123",
  "tenantId": "tenant456",
  "content": { /* current state */ },
  "contentType": "application/json",
  "size": 1024,
  "metadata": {
    "createdAt": "2026-11-23T10:00:00Z",
    "updatedAt": "2026-11-23T10:05:00Z",
    "updatedBy": "user789",
    "version": 3
  },
  "versions": [ /* array of {id, createdAt, updatedBy, size, checksum} */ ]
}
```

**Decision:** Increment `version` counter on each update. Store lightweight version records (timestamp, actor, checksum) to avoid storing full content copies. Full historical content lives in blob storage, keyed by `docId:versionId`.

---

## Error Format

**Standard error response (all 4xx/5xx):**

```json
{
  "error": {
    "code": "INVALID_INPUT",
    "message": "Document size exceeds 10MB limit",
    "details": {
      "field": "content",
      "maxSize": 10485760,
      "providedSize": 12000000
    },
    "requestId": "req_abc123def",
    "timestamp": "2026-11-23T10:05:30Z"
  }
}
```

**Common codes:**
- `UNAUTHORIZED` (401): Missing/invalid token
- `FORBIDDEN` (403): Valid token, wrong tenant or insufficient permissions
- `NOT_FOUND` (404): Document/tenant doesn't exist
- `CONFLICT` (409): Version mismatch (optimistic locking)
- `INVALID_INPUT` (400): Validation failure (bad schema, too large)
- `INTERNAL_ERROR` (500): Unhandled exception
- `RATE_LIMIT_EXCEEDED` (429): Too many requests

**Decision:** `requestId` ties error to logs for debugging. `details` object lets clients extract machine-readable context (don't parse message text). Consistent structure across all endpoints.

---

## Optimistic Locking (Conflict Avoidance)

**On document fetch, include `ETag`:**
```
GET /v1/tenants/tenant1/documents/doc123
→ ETag: "v3-abc123" (hash of content + version)
```

**On update, require it:**
```
PATCH /v1/tenants/tenant1/documents/doc123
If-Match: "v3-abc123"
Content: { ... new content ... }
```

If ETag mismatch (someone updated in parallel):
```
409 Conflict
{
  "error": { "code": "CONFLICT", ... },
  "current": { "version": 4, "eTag": "v4-xyz789", "content": { ... } }
}
```

**Decision:** Prevents silent data loss. Client retries with latest version. Alternative (server-side merging) is complex for arbitrary JSON; let client decide if they want to merge or discard.

---

## Key Decisions Recap

| Decision | Why |
|----------|-----|
| **Tenant in URL path** | Explicit, auditable, clear tenant boundary |
| **JWT + tenantId validation** | Stateless scale; defense-in-depth (token + route match) |
| **Cursor pagination** | Stable across inserts/deletes; no offset drift |
| **URL versioning** | Visible, explicit, easy to deprecate |
| **Immutable versions + blob storage** | Audit trail without 10x storage bloat |
| **Standard error codes + requestId** | Debuggable, machine-parseable, ties to logs |
| **ETag + If-Match** | Avoids accidental overwrites in concurrent edits |

---

**Left for implementation:** Rate limiting strategy (per-tenant quota?), search/filtering (Elasticsearch?), soft deletes or purge, encryption at rest, backup/restore, audit logging.
~~~

### chisle-C: 1852 answer tokens, 2029 billed, 155 lines

~~~
## Resources

**Document-centric model:**
- `GET/POST /documents` — tenant's documents
- `GET/PUT/DELETE /documents/{id}` — single document
- `GET /documents/{id}/versions` — version history (store snapshots on write)
- `GET /documents/{id}/versions/{version}` — specific version

**Why this shape:** documents are the core thing. Versions as a sub-resource keeps the API flat. No `/tenants/{id}/documents`—auth embeds the tenant, reduces URL nesting.

**Collections** (optional grouping):
- `POST /documents` with `collectionId` field (or omit for default)
- Filter: `GET /documents?collectionId=X`

Skip separate collection endpoints unless you need collection-level metadata (permissions, settings). Treat them as a document property, not a resource tier.

---

## Auth Model

**Bearer token + tenant routing:**
```
Authorization: Bearer {tenant_id}:{user_token}
```

Tenant ID in token avoids leaking it in URLs. Single endpoint per domain (no subdomain per tenant). Token validation: unpack, validate signature+claims+tenant, embed tenant context in request.

**Why:** Prevents URL-based tenant confusion. Tenant ID cryptographically bound to auth token. Token can embed user role (viewer/editor/admin) to gate operations server-side.

**Tenant isolation:** Query filters (always `WHERE tenant_id = X`) + database row-level security (if supported). Never trust URL tenant hints; always use auth token.

---

## Pagination

**Cursor-based, not offset:**
```json
GET /documents?limit=20&cursor=eyJpZCI6IjEyMyJ9

{
  "data": [{...}, {...}],
  "cursor": "eyJpZCI6IjE1MCJ9",
  "has_more": true
}
```

Cursor = base64-encoded last-document ID (or timestamp+ID for stability with deletes). Include `has_more` boolean to avoid off-by-one fetches.

**Why:** Offset doesn't scale; it must count from row 0 every time. Cursors work at any scale and are immune to inserts/deletes mid-pagination. With multi-tenant data, offset queries across millions of rows per tenant become expensive. Cursor + indexed sort key is O(1) seek.

**Sorting:** default by `-created_at` (newest first). Allow `?sort=created_at,name` (ascending/descending by prefix). Keep sortable fields indexed.

---

## Versioning

**Version via Accept header, not URL:**
```
GET /documents/{id}
Accept: application/vnd.app.v1+json
```

vs. `/v1/documents/{id}` (avoid; couples URL to lifecycle).

**Why:** API version is about contract, not URL structure. Query param `?version=1` works too but headers are cleaner. Document *data* versions stay independent—don't confuse them. If you must, use a data field: `{"version": 2, "data": {...}}`.

**Document versioning (separate):**
- Document has `version_id` (UUID) and `updated_at`.
- Write always increments `version_id` (clients can detect mid-flight conflicts).
- `GET /documents/{id}/versions/{version_id}` returns that snapshot.
- Soft-deletes: include `deleted_at` timestamp; return 410 Gone if deleted, include `Sunset` header with deletion date.

**Why:** Data versioning is content, API versioning is contract. Keep them apart. `version_id` lets optimistic concurrency control work. 410 Gone signals "this was deleted, not 404 (never existed)."

---

## Error Format

**Consistent structure:**
```json
{
  "error": {
    "code": "DOCUMENT_NOT_FOUND",
    "message": "Document abc123 does not exist or access denied.",
    "details": {
      "document_id": "abc123",
      "tenant_id": "tenant_456"
    },
    "request_id": "req_xyz789"
  }
}
```

- `code`: machine-readable, uppercase+snake_case. Same code across all formats (JSON, XML, etc.).
- `message`: human-readable, don't leak internals. "access denied" ≠ "document not found" unless you can't tell them apart safely—then pick one, be consistent.
- `details`: structured extra context (IDs, constraints, field names for 400 Bad Request).
- `request_id`: trace it; include in logs.

**HTTP status mapping:**
- 400 Bad Request: validation failure (bad JSON, missing field, constraint violation).
- 401 Unauthorized: missing/invalid token.
- 403 Forbidden: valid token, wrong tenant or no permission.
- 404 Not Found: document doesn't exist in this tenant.
- 409 Conflict: version mismatch (optimistic lock). Include `current_version_id` in `details`.
- 410 Gone: document was deleted. Include `deleted_at`.
- 429 Too Many Requests: rate limit hit. Include `Retry-After` header.
- 500 Internal Server Error: server fault, never expose error details (request_id only).

**Why:** Distinct 401/403 lets clients handle auth flow correctly. 409 Conflict signals retry-with-refresh pattern. 410 Gone is semantically correct and cacheable. Machine-readable codes decouple client logic from message text.

---

## Payload Example

```json
POST /documents
{
  "name": "Q4 Budget",
  "content": "{...}",
  "collectionId": "coll_789",
  "metadata": {
    "tags": ["budget", "finance"],
    "owner_email": "user@example.com"
  }
}

201 Created
{
  "data": {
    "id": "doc_abc123",
    "tenant_id": "tenant_456",
    "name": "Q4 Budget",
    "content": "{...}",
    "version_id": "v_1",
    "created_at": "2026-11-23T10:30:00Z",
    "updated_at": "2026-11-23T10:30:00Z",
    "created_by": "user_id",
    "metadata": {...}
  }
}
```

Include `tenant_id` in responses (confirms scope). `version_id` enables concurrency control. Timestamps in ISO 8601 UTC. Omit null fields or use explicit `null` consistently.

---

## Key Trade-offs

**Cursor vs. offset:** Cursors scale but require client state. If your tenant datasets are small (<50k docs), offset is simpler; at scale, cursor wins.

**Bearer token + tenant in token vs. tenant in URL:** Token approach is safer (can't forge URLs), but requires base64 parsing. URL approach (subdomain or path) is simpler for some CDN configs. Both work; choose based on domain model.

**Version in header vs. URL:** Headers are REST-pure but less obvious in browser. URLs are bookmarkable. If clients need stable links to specific API contracts, URL (`/v1/`, `/v2/`) is clearer. Use headers if contract rarely changes and you want flexibility.

**Soft-deletes vs. hard-deletes:** Soft-deletes with `deleted_at` are safer (audit trail, undo) but require `WHERE deleted_at IS NULL` on all queries. Hard-deletes are simpler but irreversible. Multi-tenant apps usually soft-delete to satisfy compliance (data retention). Include `deleted_at` as nullable field, filter by default, allow `?include_deleted=true` for admins.
~~~

## large-s3 / architecture

> Explain how you would migrate a monolith to services without a big-bang rewrite, covering ordering, data ownership, and how to keep it shippable throughout.

### vanilla: 917 answer tokens, 1063 billed, 45 lines

~~~
# Incremental Monolith-to-Microservices Migration

## Sequencing the extractions

**Extract by dependency flow, not arbitrary domains.** Start with the services that nothing else depends on (leaves of the dependency graph), then work backward to high-dependency services. This minimizes coupling during the transition and lets you deploy extracted services independently early.

1. Identify a service with few inbound dependencies and clear boundaries (logging, auth, a specific entity domain with limited cross-cutting calls).
2. Extract it. Leave a stub in the monolith that calls the extracted service over the network.
3. Run both in production simultaneously for a window (canary, A/B, or dual-write pattern).
4. Once confident, retire the monolith's code path and promote the network call to the only path.
5. Repeat for the next leaf.

## Data ownership and consistency

**Move data with the service.** Each service owns its own schema. The harder part is the cutover:

- **During extraction:** Use dual-write to both old and new schemas. The new service writes to its own database and also back to the monolith's schema for reads that still happen in the monolith.
- **After extraction:** Gradually shift reads to the extracted service's database. Monolith queries the extracted service's API instead of the shared schema.
- **Foreign key boundaries:** When service A needs data from service B, A calls B's API; it doesn't reach into B's database. This is slower but enforces the boundary and makes B replaceable.
- **Shared reference data:** Keep small, immutable lookup tables (country codes, status enums) in both places initially, or create a separate micro-service for them if they change frequently.

## Maintaining shippability

**Never branch for months.** Instead:

1. **Keep the monolith's main branch releasable.** New extracted services live in feature branches or behind feature flags until they're ready, so the monolith can ship unrelated bugfixes and features.
2. **Use feature flags for the cutover.** When the extracted service is ready, flag which percentage of traffic routes to it. Roll out incrementally (5% → 25% → 50% → 100%). If something breaks, flip the flag back; no revert needed.
3. **Run both paths in parallel temporarily.** The monolith calls the extracted service, but also keeps its old code path behind a flag for quick rollback. This costs some resources but is cheap insurance.
4. **Ship continuously.** Every 1–2 weeks, the monolith (and each extracted service) gets a release with either a cutover progress (flag bump to 10% more traffic) or unrelated improvements. This keeps momentum visible and limits batch risk.

## Practical order of operations

1. **Pick an easy win first** — something with few callers, isolated data (invoice PDF generation, notification dispatch). Success builds confidence.
2. **Extract to a sidecar process, not a separate VM/container.** Deploy it with the monolith initially. This reduces operational friction; you can move to separate deployment later.
3. **Build observability early.** Instrument both paths (old and new) so you can compare latency, error rates, and correctness in real time. If the new service is 10% slower, you'll see it immediately.
4. **Have a rollback plan.** Feature flags are your friend; also script the dual-write state so you can quickly revert to the old path if needed.

## Why this avoids the big-bang trap

- No massive rewrite on a hidden branch—everyone's work lands continuously.
- No "day zero" where the entire system flips over—you migrate users incrementally.
- If an extracted service has a bug, you roll it back without touching other services.
- You learn what "good boundaries" feel like by doing it incrementally, so later services go faster.

The hardest part is usually data consistency and the first few extractions (you're learning the pattern). After 2–3, teams get fast at it.
~~~

### caveman: 579 answer tokens, 749 billed, 39 lines

~~~
## Strangler Pattern: Core Move

Wrap monolith with facade (router). Route new/modified features through microservices, old code stays. Monolith shrinks over time, never dies abruptly.

## Ordering

Start with **high-change, low-fanout** services:
1. **Peripheral features** (logging, analytics, batch jobs) — few callers, isolated data
2. **New domains** — build as services from start, don't extract from monolith yet
3. **High-change APIs** — services absorb churn, monolith stabilizes
4. **Then core domains** — payment, auth, user model (last, most dependencies)

Avoid extracting hot-path dependencies first. That's backwards — creates circular calls, bloats network overhead.

## Data Ownership

**Dual writes + event sourcing:**
- Service owns its tables. Monolith writes to both (dual-write pattern or event log)
- Service consumes events from monolith (async). No distributed transactions, no two-phase commit
- Migrate data in background. Old code reads from monolith, new code from service. Reconcile on error
- Once verified, monolith stops writing its copy. Service source-of-truth

**Avoid:** Shared schema, distributed joins, monolith as integration point. Those lock you in.

## Shipping Throughout

- **Feature flags:** Route requests to service or monolith mid-request. No deploy sync needed
- **Canary deploys:** Route 5% → new service, 95% → monolith. Monitor. Increase %
- **Rollback:** Flag flip reverts traffic instantly. No data migration rollback needed if dual-write running
- **Decouple deploys:** Service deploys independent of monolith. Monolith shrinks without coordinating full app redeploy

## Keys

1. **Synchronization debt is real** — dual-write overhead increases till you delete monolith code. Plan an end-date, don't drift indefinitely
2. **Data migration last, not first** — migrate behavior/APIs first, data follows when safe
3. **Keep monolith in production** — never go dark. Every step is incremental, shippable, rollbackable
4. **Watch fanout** — if 50+ services call service X, you've built a new monolith. Extract again or accept it's platform now

Ordering matters most: low-fanout first = smaller blast radius per service, faster learning cycle, less coordination hell.
~~~

~~~
# Monolith to Services: The Shipping Path

**The core principle:** extract, don't replace. Use the strangler fig pattern — build the new service next to the old one, gradually route traffic, never flip a switch.

## Ordering: Extract the Right Thing First

Start with **leaf services**: pieces that few other parts depend on. Not the central auth system or the database layer — those are load-bearing walls.

Good targets:
- **Reporting/analytics jobs** that run offline. No tight latency coupling, easy to tolerate a service being slow or down.
- **A single domain with clear boundaries** — if you have a "payments" or "notifications" subdomain the main app calls through a few entry points, extract that.
- **Internal tools** (admin dashboards, ops scripts) — lower traffic, easier to break and fix.

**Why this order:** You learn the operational cost (new failure modes, monitoring, deployment) on low-risk work. By the time you're extracting the critical path, your team knows how to operate distributed systems without breaking prod.

Avoid extracting the central database layer first. That's the last piece, not the first.

## Data Ownership: The Hard Part

The monolith shares a database. Services can't — that's coupling that defeats the point.

**Strategy:**
1. **Identify the boundary.** A service owns its data. If service A and service B both read/write the same tables, you haven't actually extracted anything, you've just added latency and a network call.

2. **Duplicate the data, not the table.** When you extract a service, copy its rows into a new table in a new database (or schema). The monolith keeps its copy. They're decoupled now.

3. **Who's the source of truth?** For now, the monolith. The extracted service reads from its own copy; the monolith writes both. This is a temporary sync, not forever — but it ships.

4. **Sync mechanism:** 
   - If the monolith writes to a table that's duplicated in the new service, add a trigger or an event (message queue, webhook) that keeps the copy in sync. Keep it synchronous for now — if the sync fails, the monolith transaction fails. This is boring and reliable.
   - Or: the monolith does a dual-write (writes to both databases). Messy, but sometimes the only option if you can't add triggers.

5. **Phase it out.** Once the new service is stable and routes enough traffic, flip ownership: the service owns its data, the monolith reads a copy (or calls the service API). Finally, the monolith stops writing, and you can delete the old table.

**Example:** Extract "orders" into a service.
- Monolith has an `orders` table. Create `orders_service_db.orders`.
- Monolith writes to both on every order creation/update (or via trigger/event).
- Orders service reads from and writes to `orders_service_db.orders`. Monolith calls the service API for order operations.
- Once stable, deprecate the monolith's direct access to `orders` — it goes through the service API or reads a cache.
- Delete `monolith_db.orders` months later.

This is slow, but it ships. The alternative (migrate all data, flip a switch, pray) is faster until it isn't.

## Keeping It Shippable

**Network calls are slower and fail.** The monolith and new service communicate over HTTP or a message queue. This is slower than a local function call and can break. Ship it anyway, but mitigate:

- **Timeouts.** The monolith has a timeout calling the service; if it exceeds, fail fast. No infinite hangs.
- **Fallback.** For reads, if the new service is down, fall back to the old code path (read from the monolith's copy of the data). For writes, fail and retry.
- **Circuit breaker.** If the service is consistently failing, stop calling it and use the fallback for a while. Prevents cascading failures.

These aren't exotic — they're the floor, not the ceiling.

**Deploy independently, but carefully.**
- The monolith and service can deploy on different schedules.
- But the monolith's caller code (the code that calls the new service) and the service must stay compatible. If the monolith calls `POST /order/create` and you change the response schema, the monolith can break.
- Version the API or test both versions. Ship the monolith changes first (that call the new endpoints), then the service. Or use a feature flag.

**Ship one feature at a time.**
- Extract one domain/service → run it in parallel → route a subset of traffic to it → verify it works → route more traffic → turn off the old code path.
- Each step is a separate deploy. Each step is shippable, testable, reversible.

## The Rough Timeline

1. **Pick a leaf service** (2–4 weeks).
2. **Extract the code, build the API** (2–4 weeks).
3. **Set up sync, dual-write, fallback** (1–2 weeks).
4. **Deploy and monitor** (ongoing).
5. **Route 5% traffic, then 50%, then 100%** (1–2 weeks per step, watch metrics).
6. **Turn off the monolith's old code path** (1 week, leaves the data copy around).
7. **Delete the monolith's data copy** (1 month later, only if nothing broke).

Then repeat with the next service.

**What breaks it:**
- Extracting a service with hard coupling (service A calls B calls A).
- Flipping traffic in one deploy instead of gradual ramp.
- Shared schema between services.
- No fallback when the service is down.
- Extracting the core database layer first instead of a leaf.

The key is: **every step is reversible**. If the new service breaks, traffic reverts to the monolith. If sync stops, you stop calling the service. The monolith keeps running and shipping features the whole time.
~~~

### chisle: 531 answer tokens, 1056 billed, 9 lines

~~~
**Extract by seams, not big-bang.** Strangler pattern: new service lives alongside monolith, intercepts calls via adapter layer (reverse proxy, library wrapper, middleware). Monolith calls new service → if it fails, falls back to old code. Feature flags control the routing. This keeps production shippable: each extraction is independent, rollback is a flag flip, not a deploy.

**Ordering: backends first, UI last.** Extract high-ROI, loosely-coupled services (auth, payments, notifications). Avoid anything that depended-on heavily. Each extracted service must own its write-path; the monolith keeps a read-only cached copy via events or CDC (change-data-capture). During migration, dual-write to both old code + new service, with the old code as source of truth. When new service is proven stable (shadows real traffic, canaries pass), flip the flag: new service becomes source of truth, monolith's writes stop. You can retire code later; don't rush it.

**Data ownership is the hard part.** Each service owns its tables. Consistency is now eventually-consistent: the monolith and new service may disagree briefly. Synchronous invariants (e.g., "debit account, then credit") become sagas: multi-step flows coordinated by events. This is slower and messier than monolith transactions, so pick your first extraction carefully—something with few cross-service invariants.

**Keep it shippable by never requiring cutover.** Shadow traffic (send real requests to new service, discard the response) finds bugs before cutover matters. Canary (1% → 10% → 100%) catches production issues. If new service breaks, disable it and roll back—the monolith is still handling traffic. No "migration day" where everything breaks.

**The gotchas:** ordering is strict (extract backends, then services that consume them, then UI). Dual-write is fragile—if writes diverge, data rots—so keep old code as truth-maker until new service has 100% test coverage and production hours. Eventual consistency breaks things like "user deleted, all their data gone instantly"; you need new patterns (tombstones, reconciliation sweeps). Most teams underestimate the time here—this is a 2–3 year project for a large monolith, not a quarter.
~~~

### chisle-C: 632 answer tokens, 954 billed, 26 lines

~~~
**Core pattern: Strangler + gradual extraction.** New requests route to the service; old code paths still work. Database stays shared initially, splits later. Feature flags gate traffic. You ship every step.

**Ordering:**
- Extract by dependency graph (bottom-up): services with no dependencies first, so the monolith doesn't depend on them yet.
- Or extract by business value: highest ROI first if the team wants early wins and learning.
- Pick the smallest seam with clear boundaries and existing cohesion. If the code is tangled, you're extracting the wrong thing or the monolith isn't ready.

**Data ownership:**
- Start with shared database. New service reads/writes to shared schema. Monolith does too. No coordination cost yet.
- When you're ready to split: read replica strategy. Service gets its own read replica, writes still go through monolith. Publish change events from monolith → service (Kafka, webhooks, polling).
- Dual writes phase: monolith writes to both old and new tables. Service consumes from new table. This reveals consistency gaps (missed updates, timing). Run this for days/weeks; when it's solid, flip reads.
- Migrate historical data offline. No cutover of the whole dataset; backfill in background.
- Last: monolith stops writing to old tables. Service owns them.

**Staying shippable:**
- Every PR ships a feature flag. Service is running in prod (dark), handling traffic, but clients don't see it yet. Logs, metrics, errors all visible. Catch bugs before cutover.
- Route traffic to new service gradually: 1% → 5% → 25% → 100%. Watch SLOs. If latency/errors spike, roll back instantly.
- Run both paths in parallel during the transition. Monolith calls service, verifies response matches what monolith would have done. Log divergences. This is your safety net.
- Canary deployment for the monolith changes (dual writes, new routing). Most teams get this wrong—test the actual cutover path, not just the code.

**Key gotchas:**
- If you can't name clean seams in the monolith, extract a wrapper service first. It doesn't own logic—just adapts the old code. Gives you async boundaries and lets you practice.
- Transactions across service boundary kill shippability. Avoid until you have to; prefer eventual consistency and compensating transactions.
- Monitoring is non-negotiable. Can't cut over without understanding what you're changing. Instrument early, make the service visible before traffic hits.

**Timeline:** 3–6 months per service if you're doing this right (not rushing). Smaller services extract faster; larger ones teach you what not to do next.
~~~

> Implement email-and-password signup and login with password hashing, session issuing, and rate limiting on failed attempts. Show the code.

### vanilla: 2165 answer tokens, 2450 billed, 242 lines

~~~
I'll provide a complete implementation using Python/Flask with SQLite. This includes password hashing, JWT sessions, and rate limiting.

```python
# app.py
from flask import Flask, request, jsonify
from werkzeug.security import generate_password_hash, check_password_hash
import jwt
import sqlite3
from datetime import datetime, timedelta
from functools import wraps
import os
from collections import defaultdict

app = Flask(__name__)
app.config['SECRET_KEY'] = os.getenv('SECRET_KEY', 'your-secret-key-change-in-production')

# Rate limiting: track failed attempts per email
failed_attempts = defaultdict(list)
RATE_LIMIT_ATTEMPTS = 5
RATE_LIMIT_WINDOW = 15 * 60  # 15 minutes in seconds

def init_db():
    conn = sqlite3.connect('auth.db')
    c = conn.cursor()
    c.execute('''
        CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS users (
            id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
            email TEXT UNIQUE NOT NULL,
            password_hash TEXT NOT NULL,
            created_at TIMESTAMP DEFAULT CURRENT_TIMESTAMP
        )
    ''')
    conn.commit()
    conn.close()

def get_db():
    conn = sqlite3.connect('auth.db')
    conn.row_factory = sqlite3.Row
    return conn

def check_rate_limit(email):
    """Check if email has exceeded rate limit for failed attempts."""
    now = datetime.now()
    # Remove old attempts outside the window
    failed_attempts[email] = [
        timestamp for timestamp in failed_attempts[email]
        if (now - timestamp).total_seconds() < RATE_LIMIT_WINDOW
    ]
    
    if len(failed_attempts[email]) >= RATE_LIMIT_ATTEMPTS:
        return False, f"Too many failed attempts. Try again in {RATE_LIMIT_WINDOW // 60} minutes."
    return True, None

def record_failed_attempt(email):
    """Record a failed login attempt."""
    failed_attempts[email].append(datetime.now())

def clear_failed_attempts(email):
    """Clear failed attempts on successful login."""
    if email in failed_attempts:
        del failed_attempts[email]

def issue_token(user_id, email, expires_in=86400):
    """Issue JWT token (expires in 24 hours by default)."""
    payload = {
        'user_id': user_id,
        'email': email,
        'exp': datetime.utcnow() + timedelta(seconds=expires_in),
        'iat': datetime.utcnow()
    }
    token = jwt.encode(payload, app.config['SECRET_KEY'], algorithm='HS256')
    return token

def token_required(f):
    """Decorator to protect routes requiring valid token."""
    @wraps(f)
    def decorated(*args, **kwargs):
        token = None
        if 'Authorization' in request.headers:
            auth_header = request.headers['Authorization']
            try:
                token = auth_header.split(' ')[1]
            except IndexError:
                return jsonify({'error': 'Invalid token format'}), 401
        
        if not token:
            return jsonify({'error': 'Token is missing'}), 401
        
        try:
            data = jwt.decode(token, app.config['SECRET_KEY'], algorithms=['HS256'])
            request.user_id = data['user_id']
            request.email = data['email']
        except jwt.ExpiredSignatureError:
            return jsonify({'error': 'Token has expired'}), 401
        except jwt.InvalidTokenError:
            return jsonify({'error': 'Invalid token'}), 401
        
        return f(*args, **kwargs)
    return decorated

@app.route('/signup', methods=['POST'])
def signup():
    """User registration endpoint."""
    data = request.get_json()
    
    if not data:
        return jsonify({'error': 'No data provided'}), 400
    
    email = data.get('email', '').strip()
    password = data.get('password', '')
    
    # Validation
    if not email or '@' not in email:
        return jsonify({'error': 'Invalid email format'}), 400
    if not password or len(password) < 8:
        return jsonify({'error': 'Password must be at least 8 characters'}), 400
    
    conn = get_db()
    c = conn.cursor()
    
    try:
        password_hash = generate_password_hash(password, method='pbkdf2:sha256')
        c.execute('INSERT INTO users (email, password_hash) VALUES (?, ?)', (email, password_hash))
        conn.commit()
        user_id = c.lastrowid
        
        token = issue_token(user_id, email)
        return jsonify({
            'message': 'User created successfully',
            'token': token,
            'user_id': user_id,
            'email': email
        }), 201
        
    except sqlite3.IntegrityError:
        return jsonify({'error': 'Email already registered'}), 409
    finally:
        conn.close()

@app.route('/login', methods=['POST'])
def login():
    """User login endpoint."""
    data = request.get_json()
    
    if not data:
        return jsonify({'error': 'No data provided'}), 400
    
    email = data.get('email', '').strip()
    password = data.get('password', '')
    
    if not email or not password:
        return jsonify({'error': 'Email and password required'}), 400
    
    # Check rate limit
    allowed, error_msg = check_rate_limit(email)
    if not allowed:
        return jsonify({'error': error_msg}), 429
    
    conn = get_db()
    c = conn.cursor()
    c.execute('SELECT id, password_hash FROM users WHERE email = ?', (email,))
    user = c.fetchone()
    conn.close()
    
    if not user or not check_password_hash(user['password_hash'], password):
        record_failed_attempt(email)
        return jsonify({'error': 'Invalid email or password'}), 401
    
    # Successful login
    clear_failed_attempts(email)
    token = issue_token(user['id'], email)
    
    return jsonify({
        'message': 'Login successful',
        'token': token,
        'user_id': user['id'],
        'email': email
    }), 200

@app.route('/protected', methods=['GET'])
@token_required
def protected_route():
    """Example protected route requiring valid token."""
    return jsonify({
        'message': 'Access granted',
        'user_id': request.user_id,
        'email': request.email
    }), 200

@app.route('/logout', methods=['POST'])
@token_required
def logout():
    """Logout endpoint (token invalidation done client-side by discarding token)."""
    return jsonify({'message': 'Logged out successfully'}), 200

if __name__ == '__main__':
    init_db()
    app.run(debug=True)
```

**Usage examples:**

```bash
# Install dependencies
pip install flask pyjwt werkzeug

# Run the app
python app.py

# Signup
curl -X POST http://localhost:5000/signup \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d '{"email":"user@example.com","password":"SecurePass123"}'

# Login
curl -X POST http://localhost:5000/login \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d '{"email":"user@example.com","password":"SecurePass123"}'

# Access protected route with token
curl -X GET http://localhost:5000/protected \
  -H "Authorization: Bearer <token_from_login>"
```

**Key features:**

1. **Password hashing**: Uses PBKDF2-SHA256 via werkzeug
2. **JWT sessions**: 24-hour expiring tokens with user metadata
3. **Rate limiting**: Tracks failed attempts per email, blocks after 5 attempts for 15 minutes
4. **Validation**: Email format and 8+ character password minimum
5. **Error handling**: Appropriate HTTP status codes (401, 409, 429)
6. **Token protection**: `@token_required` decorator for guarding routes

**Production considerations:**
- Use environment variables for the secret key
- Replace SQLite with PostgreSQL/MySQL
- Use Redis for distributed rate limiting
- Add CORS headers
- Implement token refresh endpoints
- Add email verification for signups
- Use HTTPS only
- Implement CSRF protection if using session cookies

# Email-Password Auth with Hashing, Sessions, Rate Limiting

**Node.js + Express** implementation:

```javascript
// auth-server.js
const express = require('express');
const bcrypt = require('bcrypt');
const jwt = require('jsonwebtoken');
const rateLimit = require('express-rate-limit');

const app = express();
app.use(express.json());

// In-memory user store (use DB in production)
const users = new Map();

// Rate limiting: track failed login attempts per email
const loginAttempts = new Map();
const MAX_ATTEMPTS = 5;
const LOCKOUT_DURATION = 15 * 60 * 1000; // 15 min
const SECRET_KEY = process.env.JWT_SECRET || 'dev-secret-key';

// Utility: check if account locked
function isLocked(email) {
  const record = loginAttempts.get(email);
  if (!record) return false;
  if (Date.now() - record.lastAttempt > LOCKOUT_DURATION) {
    loginAttempts.delete(email);
    return false;
  }
  return record.count >= MAX_ATTEMPTS;
}

// Utility: record failed attempt
function recordFailedAttempt(email) {
  const record = loginAttempts.get(email) || { count: 0, lastAttempt: Date.now() };
  record.count++;
  record.lastAttempt = Date.now();
  loginAttempts.set(email, record);
}

// Utility: clear attempts on success
function clearAttempts(email) {
  loginAttempts.delete(email);
}

// Utility: validate email format
function isValidEmail(email) {
  return /^[^\s@]+@[^\s@]+\.[^\s@]+$/.test(email);
}

// SIGNUP
app.post('/signup', async (req, res) => {
  try {
    const { email, password } = req.body;

    if (!email || !password) {
      return res.status(400).json({ error: 'Email and password required' });
    }

    if (!isValidEmail(email)) {
      return res.status(400).json({ error: 'Invalid email format' });
    }

    if (password.length < 8) {
      return res.status(400).json({ error: 'Password must be 8+ chars' });
    }

    if (users.has(email)) {
      return res.status(409).json({ error: 'Email already registered' });
    }

    // Hash password with bcrypt (salt rounds = 10)
    const hashedPassword = await bcrypt.hash(password, 10);

    users.set(email, {
      email,
      hashedPassword,
      createdAt: new Date()
    });

    res.status(201).json({ message: 'Signup successful', email });
  } catch (err) {
    res.status(500).json({ error: 'Internal server error' });
  }
});

// LOGIN
app.post('/login', async (req, res) => {
  try {
    const { email, password } = req.body;

    if (!email || !password) {
      return res.status(400).json({ error: 'Email and password required' });
    }

    // Check rate limit
    if (isLocked(email)) {
      return res.status(429).json({ 
        error: 'Too many failed attempts. Try again later.' 
      });
    }

    const user = users.get(email);
    if (!user) {
      recordFailedAttempt(email);
      return res.status(401).json({ error: 'Invalid credentials' });
    }

    // Compare password with hashed password
    const passwordMatch = await bcrypt.compare(password, user.hashedPassword);
    if (!passwordMatch) {
      recordFailedAttempt(email);
      return res.status(401).json({ error: 'Invalid credentials' });
    }

    // Clear rate limit on success
    clearAttempts(email);

    // Issue JWT session token (1 hour expiry)
    const token = jwt.sign(
      { email, userId: email },
      SECRET_KEY,
      { expiresIn: '1h' }
    );

    res.json({ 
      message: 'Login successful',
      token,
      expiresIn: 3600
    });
  } catch (err) {
    res.status(500).json({ error: 'Internal server error' });
  }
});

// Middleware: verify JWT token
function verifyToken(req, res, next) {
  const authHeader = req.headers.authorization;
  if (!authHeader || !authHeader.startsWith('Bearer ')) {
    return res.status(401).json({ error: 'Missing or invalid token' });
  }

  const token = authHeader.slice(7);
  try {
    const decoded = jwt.verify(token, SECRET_KEY);
    req.user = decoded;
    next();
  } catch (err) {
    res.status(401).json({ error: 'Invalid or expired token' });
  }
}

// Protected route example
app.get('/profile', verifyToken, (req, res) => {
  res.json({ 
    message: 'Profile accessed',
    user: req.user 
  });
});

app.listen(3000, () => console.log('Auth server on :3000'));
```

**Setup:**
```bash
npm install express bcrypt jsonwebtoken
node auth-server.js
```

**Usage:**

Signup:
```bash
curl -X POST http://localhost:3000/signup \
  -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  -d '{"email":"user@example.com","password":"securepass123"}'
```

Login:
```bash
curl -X POST http://localhost:3000/login \
  -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  -d '{"email":"user@example.com","password":"securepass123"}'
# Returns: {"token":"eyJ...","expiresIn":3600}
```

Access protected route:
```bash
curl http://localhost:3000/profile \
  -H 'Authorization: Bearer eyJ...'
```

**Key features:**
- **bcrypt**: Password hashed with salt rounds=10. Never store plaintext.
- **JWT**: Stateless session tokens, 1h expiry. Client stores, sends in `Authorization: Bearer` header.
- **Rate limiting**: 5 failed attempts → 15 min lockout per email. Clears on successful login.
- **Validation**: Email format, password length ≥8 chars, duplicate signup check.
- **Error handling**: Vague "Invalid credentials" (no email enumeration).

Switch to PostgreSQL, Redis for production (rate limit in-memory not suitable at scale).

# Email-Password Auth with Hashing, Sessions & Rate Limiting

```python
from fastapi import FastAPI, HTTPException, Depends
from pydantic import BaseModel, EmailStr
from sqlalchemy import create_engine, Column, String, Integer, DateTime
from sqlalchemy.orm import sessionmaker, Session
from sqlalchemy.ext.declarative import declarative_base
from passlib.context import CryptContext
from datetime import datetime, timedelta
import jwt
from collections import defaultdict

# Setup
DATABASE_URL = "sqlite:///./auth.db"
engine = create_engine(DATABASE_URL)
SessionLocal = sessionmaker(bind=engine)
Base = declarative_base()

pwd_context = CryptContext(schemes=["argon2"], deprecated="auto")
SECRET_KEY = "change-this-to-random-secret"
ALGORITHM = "HS256"

# Rate limiting: {email: [timestamps of failed attempts]}
failed_login_attempts = defaultdict(list)
MAX_ATTEMPTS = 5
WINDOW_SECONDS = 300

class User(Base):
    __tablename__ = "users"
    id = Column(Integer, primary_key=True)
    email = Column(String, unique=True, index=True)
    password_hash = Column(String)
    created_at = Column(DateTime, default=datetime.utcnow)

Base.metadata.create_all(bind=engine)

class SignupReq(BaseModel):
    email: EmailStr
    password: str

class LoginReq(BaseModel):
    email: EmailStr
    password: str

class Token(BaseModel):
    access_token: str
    token_type: str = "bearer"

app = FastAPI()

def get_db():
    db = SessionLocal()
    try:
        yield db
    finally:
        db.close()

def hash_pwd(pwd: str) -> str:
    return pwd_context.hash(pwd)

def verify_pwd(plain: str, hashed: str) -> bool:
    return pwd_context.verify(plain, hashed)

def create_token(email: str, hours: int = 24) -> str:
    exp = datetime.utcnow() + timedelta(hours=hours)
    payload = {"sub": email, "exp": exp}
    return jwt.encode(payload, SECRET_KEY, algorithm=ALGORITHM)

def is_rate_limited(email: str) -> bool:
    now = datetime.utcnow().timestamp()
    cutoff = now - WINDOW_SECONDS
    
    # Prune old attempts
    failed_login_attempts[email] = [
        ts for ts in failed_login_attempts[email] if ts > cutoff
    ]
    
    return len(failed_login_attempts[email]) >= MAX_ATTEMPTS

def record_failed_attempt(email: str):
    failed_login_attempts[email].append(datetime.utcnow().timestamp())

@app.post("/signup", response_model=Token)
def signup(req: SignupReq, db: Session = Depends(get_db)):
    if db.query(User).filter(User.email == req.email).first():
        raise HTTPException(400, detail="Email already registered")
    
    if len(req.password) < 8:
        raise HTTPException(400, detail="Password must be 8+ chars")
    
    user = User(email=req.email, password_hash=hash_pwd(req.password))
    db.add(user)
    db.commit()
    
    return {"access_token": create_token(req.email)}

@app.post("/login", response_model=Token)
def login(req: LoginReq, db: Session = Depends(get_db)):
    if is_rate_limited(req.email):
        raise HTTPException(429, detail="Too many failed attempts, try later")
    
    user = db.query(User).filter(User.email == req.email).first()
    if not user or not verify_pwd(req.password, user.password_hash):
        record_failed_attempt(req.email)
        raise HTTPException(401, detail="Invalid email or password")
    
    return {"access_token": create_token(req.email)}

# Test
if __name__ == "__main__":
    import uvicorn
    uvicorn.run(app, host="127.0.0.1", port=8000)
```

**To use:**
```bash
pip install fastapi uvicorn sqlalchemy passlib python-multipart pyjwt argon2-cffi email-validator

# Start server
python app.py

# Signup
curl -X POST http://localhost:8000/signup \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d '{"email":"user@example.com","password":"securepass123"}'

# Login
curl -X POST http://localhost:8000/login \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d '{"email":"user@example.com","password":"securepass123"}'
```

**What's included:**
- **Argon2 hashing** via passlib (industry standard, strong against GPU attacks)
- **JWT tokens** issued on signup/login (stateless, 24hr expiry)
- **Rate limiting** on login: 5 failed attempts per 5-minute window per email
- **Email validation** at input boundary
- **Duplicate email check** at signup

**Skipped:** refresh tokens (add with `/refresh` endpoint), logout revocation (needs token blacklist or short expiry), 2FA, password reset flow. Add when needed.

```javascript
const express = require('express');

const app = express();

const SECRET = 'your-secret-key-change-in-prod';
const users = new Map(); // id -> {email, passwordHash}
const rateLimits = new Map(); // email -> {attempts, resetTime}
const RATE_LIMIT = { maxAttempts: 5, windowMs: 15 * 60 * 1000 };

// Rate limit check
function checkRateLimit(email) {
  const now = Date.now();
  const record = rateLimits.get(email);
  
  if (!record || now > record.resetTime) {
    rateLimits.set(email, { attempts: 0, resetTime: now + RATE_LIMIT.windowMs });
    return true;
  }
  
  if (record.attempts >= RATE_LIMIT.maxAttempts) {
    return false;
  }
  
  record.attempts++;
  return true;
}

function resetRateLimit(email) {
  rateLimits.delete(email);
}

// Verify JWT token
function verifyToken(req, res, next) {
  const token = req.headers.authorization?.split(' ')[1];
  if (!token) return res.status(401).json({ error: 'No token' });
  
  try {
    req.user = jwt.verify(token, SECRET);
    next();
  } catch {
    res.status(401).json({ error: 'Invalid token' });
  }
}

// Signup
app.post('/signup', async (req, res) => {
  const { email, password } = req.body;
  
  if (!email || !password) {
    return res.status(400).json({ error: 'Email and password required' });
  }
  
  if (Array.from(users.values()).some(u => u.email === email)) {
    return res.status(409).json({ error: 'Email already exists' });
  }
  
  const passwordHash = await bcrypt.hash(password, 10);
  const userId = `user_${Date.now()}`;
  users.set(userId, { email, passwordHash });
  
  const token = jwt.sign({ userId, email }, SECRET, { expiresIn: '7d' });
  res.json({ userId, token });
});

// Login
app.post('/login', async (req, res) => {
  const { email, password } = req.body;
  
  if (!checkRateLimit(email)) {
    return res.status(429).json({ error: 'Too many attempts, try later' });
  }
  
  const user = Array.from(users.entries()).find(([_, u]) => u.email === email);
  
  if (!user) {
    return res.status(401).json({ error: 'Invalid credentials' });
  }
  
  const [userId, userData] = user;
  const passwordMatch = await bcrypt.compare(password, userData.passwordHash);
  
  if (!passwordMatch) {
    return res.status(401).json({ error: 'Invalid credentials' });
  }
  
  resetRateLimit(email);
  const token = jwt.sign({ userId, email }, SECRET, { expiresIn: '7d' });
  res.json({ userId, token });
});

// Protected route example
app.get('/profile', verifyToken, (req, res) => {
  res.json({ user: req.user });
});

app.listen(3000, () => console.log('Running on :3000'));
```

**To use:**
- `npm install express bcrypt jsonwebtoken`
- Signup: `POST /signup` → `{email, password}` → returns `{userId, token}`
- Login: `POST /login` → `{email, password}` → returns token (rate-limited after 5 fails per 15min)
- Protected routes: add header `Authorization: Bearer {token}`

**Production gaps:** replace in-memory storage with database, use Redis for rate limiting, rotate SECRET, enforce password strength, add HTTPS, use secure session cookies instead of tokens in localStorage.

```javascript
// app.js
const express = require('express');
const bcrypt = require('bcrypt');
const jwt = require('jsonwebtoken');
const rateLimit = require('express-rate-limit');

const app = express();
app.use(express.json());

const SECRET = process.env.JWT_SECRET || 'dev-secret-change-in-prod';
const users = {}; // production: use database
const failedAttempts = {}; // production: use Redis

// Rate limiter: 5 failed attempts per email, 15-minute window
const loginLimiter = rateLimit({
  windowMs: 15 * 60 * 1000,
  max: 5,
  keyGenerator: (req) => req.body?.email || req.ip,
  handler: (req, res) => {
    res.status(429).json({ error: 'Too many failed attempts. Try again in 15 minutes.' });
  },
  skip: (req) => !req.body?.email,
});

// Signup
app.post('/signup', async (req, res) => {
  const { email, password } = req.body;

  if (!email || !password || password.length < 8) {
    return res.status(400).json({ error: 'Email and 8+ char password required' });
  }

  if (users[email]) {
    return res.status(409).json({ error: 'User exists' });
  }

  users[email] = { passwordHash: await bcrypt.hash(password, 10) };
  res.status(201).json({ message: 'Signup successful' });
});

// Login with rate limiting
app.post('/login', loginLimiter, async (req, res) => {
  const { email, password } = req.body;

  if (!email || !password) {
    return res.status(400).json({ error: 'Email and password required' });
  }

  const user = users[email];
  if (!user || !(await bcrypt.compare(password, user.passwordHash))) {
    return res.status(401).json({ error: 'Invalid credentials' });
  }

  const token = jwt.sign({ email }, SECRET, { expiresIn: '1h' });
  res.json({ token });
});

// Verify token middleware
const authMiddleware = (req, res, next) => {
  const token = req.headers.authorization?.split(' ')[1];
  try {
    req.user = jwt.verify(token, SECRET);
    next();
  } catch {
    res.status(401).json({ error: 'Unauthorized' });
  }
};

// Protected route
app.get('/profile', authMiddleware, (req, res) => {
  res.json({ email: req.user.email });
});

app.listen(3000, () => console.log('Running on :3000'));
```

**Install:** `npm install express bcrypt jsonwebtoken express-rate-limit`

**What it does:**
- **Signup**: validates input, hashes password with bcrypt (10 salt rounds)
- **Login**: compares hashed password, issues JWT (1h expiry)
- **Rate limit**: 5 failed attempts per email/15 min via `express-rate-limit`
- **Protected route**: verifies JWT before access

**For production:** replace in-memory stores with database (PostgreSQL, MongoDB), use Redis for rate limiting, store JWT_SECRET in env, add HTTPS, CSRF protection, password reset flow.

## large-s3 / csvpipeline

> Write a script that reads a large CSV of transactions, validates each row, aggregates totals per customer, and writes a report, handling malformed rows without dying. Show the code.

### vanilla: 1409 answer tokens, 1688 billed, 129 lines

~~~
Here's a production-ready Python script that handles large CSV transaction files robustly:

```python
#!/usr/bin/env python3
import csv
import sys
from pathlib import Path
from collections import defaultdict
from decimal import Decimal, InvalidOperation
from datetime import datetime

def validate_transaction(row, row_num):
    """Validate a transaction row. Returns (is_valid, cleaned_row, error_message)."""
    required_fields = ['customer_id', 'amount', 'date']
    
    # Check required fields exist
    for field in required_fields:
        if field not in row or row[field].strip() == '':
            return False, None, f"Missing or empty field: {field}"
    
    customer_id = row['customer_id'].strip()
    
    # Validate customer_id is non-empty
    if not customer_id:
        return False, None, "customer_id is empty"
    
    # Validate amount is a valid decimal
    try:
        amount = Decimal(row['amount'].strip())
        if amount < 0:
            return False, None, f"Negative amount: {amount}"
    except InvalidOperation:
        return False, None, f"Invalid amount: {row['amount']}"
    
    # Validate date format (optional, adjust as needed)
    try:
        datetime.fromisoformat(row['date'].strip())
    except ValueError:
        return False, None, f"Invalid date format: {row['date']}"
    
    return True, {'customer_id': customer_id, 'amount': amount}, None

def process_transactions(csv_path, output_path, error_log_path):
    """Read CSV, validate rows, aggregate by customer, write report."""
    
    totals = defaultdict(Decimal)
    transaction_counts = defaultdict(int)
    errors = []
    valid_count = 0
    
    try:
        with open(csv_path, 'r', encoding='utf-8') as infile:
            reader = csv.DictReader(infile)
            
            # Validate fieldnames exist
            if reader.fieldnames is None or 'customer_id' not in reader.fieldnames:
                print("Error: CSV must have 'customer_id' column", file=sys.stderr)
                return False
            
            for row_num, row in enumerate(reader, start=2):  # start=2 (after header)
                is_valid, cleaned, error = validate_transaction(row, row_num)
                
                if is_valid:
                    customer_id = cleaned['customer_id']
                    amount = cleaned['amount']
                    totals[customer_id] += amount
                    transaction_counts[customer_id] += 1
                    valid_count += 1
                else:
                    errors.append(f"Row {row_num}: {error}")
        
        # Write error log
        if errors:
            with open(error_log_path, 'w') as f:
                f.write(f"Found {len(errors)} malformed rows:\n\n")
                for error in errors:
                    f.write(error + '\n')
        
        # Write report
        with open(output_path, 'w') as f:
            f.write("Customer ID,Total Amount,Transaction Count\n")
            for customer_id in sorted(totals.keys()):
                f.write(f"{customer_id},{totals[customer_id]},{transaction_counts[customer_id]}\n")
        
        # Print summary
        print(f"✓ Processed: {valid_count} valid transactions")
        print(f"✗ Skipped: {len(errors)} malformed rows")
        print(f"✓ Report written to: {output_path}")
        if errors:
            print(f"✓ Error log written to: {error_log_path}")
        
        return True
    
    except FileNotFoundError:
        print(f"Error: File not found: {csv_path}", file=sys.stderr)
        return False
    except Exception as e:
        print(f"Error: {e}", file=sys.stderr)
        return False

if __name__ == '__main__':
    if len(sys.argv) < 2:
        print("Usage: script.py <input.csv> [output.csv] [error_log.txt]")
        sys.exit(1)
    
    input_csv = sys.argv[1]
    output_csv = sys.argv[2] if len(sys.argv) > 2 else 'report.csv'
    error_log = sys.argv[3] if len(sys.argv) > 3 else 'errors.log'
    
    success = process_transactions(input_csv, output_csv, error_log)
    sys.exit(0 if success else 1)
```

**Key features:**

- **Validation**: Checks required fields, validates amounts as decimals (catches non-numeric), validates dates
- **Graceful error handling**: Malformed rows are logged but don't crash; processing continues
- **Aggregation**: Uses `defaultdict` to sum amounts and count transactions per customer
- **Memory efficient**: Streams CSV reader instead of loading entire file
- **Decimal arithmetic**: Uses `Decimal` for precise currency calculations (avoids float rounding issues)
- **Separate error reporting**: Skipped rows logged to `errors.log` for review
- **Summary output**: Prints counts of valid/skipped rows

**Usage:**
```bash
./script.py transactions.csv report.csv errors.log
```

The script reads the input CSV, skips any malformed rows (logging them), sums totals per customer, and writes a clean report. Adjust the `validate_transaction()` function's validation rules for your specific data format.

```python
#!/usr/bin/env python3
import csv
import sys
from collections import defaultdict
from pathlib import Path

def validate_row(row, row_num):
    """Validate transaction row. Return (is_valid, error_msg, parsed_row)."""
    required = ['customer_id', 'amount', 'date']
    
    # Check required fields exist
    if not all(k in row for k in required):
        return False, f"Missing fields: {required}", None
    
    cust_id = row['customer_id'].strip()
    amount_str = row['amount'].strip()
    date_str = row['date'].strip()
    
    # Validate customer ID not empty
    if not cust_id:
        return False, "Empty customer_id", None
    
    # Validate amount is numeric
    try:
        amount = float(amount_str)
    except ValueError:
        return False, f"Invalid amount: {amount_str}", None
    
    # Validate amount not negative
    if amount < 0:
        return False, f"Negative amount: {amount}", None
    
    # Basic date format check (YYYY-MM-DD)
    if not date_str or len(date_str) != 10 or date_str.count('-') != 2:
        return False, f"Invalid date format: {date_str}", None
    
    return True, None, {'customer_id': cust_id, 'amount': amount, 'date': date_str}

def process_transactions(input_csv, output_report):
    """Read CSV, validate rows, aggregate by customer, write report."""
    totals = defaultdict(float)
    tx_count = defaultdict(int)
    errors = []
    valid_count = 0
    
    try:
        with open(input_csv, 'r', encoding='utf-8') as infile:
            reader = csv.DictReader(infile)
            
            if not reader.fieldnames:
                print("Error: Empty CSV file", file=sys.stderr)
                return
            
            for row_num, row in enumerate(reader, start=2):  # Start at 2 (after header)
                is_valid, error, parsed = validate_row(row, row_num)
                
                if is_valid:
                    cust_id = parsed['customer_id']
                    totals[cust_id] += parsed['amount']
                    tx_count[cust_id] += 1
                    valid_count += 1
                else:
                    errors.append((row_num, error, dict(row)))
    
    except IOError as e:
        print(f"Error reading file: {e}", file=sys.stderr)
        return
    except Exception as e:
        print(f"Unexpected error: {e}", file=sys.stderr)
        return
    
    # Write report
    try:
        with open(output_report, 'w', encoding='utf-8') as outfile:
            outfile.write("=== TRANSACTION SUMMARY REPORT ===\n\n")
            outfile.write(f"Valid rows processed: {valid_count}\n")
            outfile.write(f"Malformed rows skipped: {len(errors)}\n")
            outfile.write(f"Unique customers: {len(totals)}\n\n")
            
            outfile.write("CUSTOMER TOTALS\n")
            outfile.write("-" * 50 + "\n")
            outfile.write(f"{'Customer ID':<20} {'Count':<10} {'Total':<15}\n")
            outfile.write("-" * 50 + "\n")
            
            for cust_id in sorted(totals.keys()):
                total = totals[cust_id]
                count = tx_count[cust_id]
                outfile.write(f"{cust_id:<20} {count:<10} ${total:>13.2f}\n")
            
            outfile.write("\n" + "=" * 50 + "\n")
            outfile.write("ERRORS AND MALFORMED ROWS\n")
            outfile.write("=" * 50 + "\n\n")
            
            if errors:
                for row_num, error, row_data in errors:
                    outfile.write(f"Row {row_num}: {error}\n")
                    outfile.write(f"  Data: {row_data}\n\n")
            else:
                outfile.write("No errors.\n")
    
    except IOError as e:
        print(f"Error writing report: {e}", file=sys.stderr)
        return
    
    # Print summary to stdout
    print(f"✓ Processed {valid_count} valid transactions")
    print(f"⚠ Skipped {len(errors)} malformed rows")
    print(f"✓ Report written to: {output_report}")

if __name__ == '__main__':
    if len(sys.argv) < 3:
        print("Usage: python script.py <input.csv> <output_report.txt>")
        sys.exit(1)
    
    input_csv = sys.argv[1]
    output_report = sys.argv[2]
    
    if not Path(input_csv).exists():
        print(f"Error: Input file not found: {input_csv}", file=sys.stderr)
        sys.exit(1)
    
    process_transactions(input_csv, output_report)
```

**Usage:**
```bash
python script.py transactions.csv report.txt
```

**Features:**
- **Validation**: Checks required fields, numeric amounts, positive values, date format
- **Error handling**: Logs malformed rows with line number + error reason, keeps processing
- **Aggregation**: Groups by customer ID, sums amounts, counts transactions
- **Large file**: Processes streaming (no loading whole file into memory)
- **Report**: Customer totals table + error log
- **Exit safe**: Handles missing files, encoding issues, missing headers

**Example CSV:**

```
customer_id,amount,date,description
CUST001,150.00,2026-11-20,Purchase

CUST001,200.00,2026-11-22,Purchase
```

```python
import csv
import sys
from collections import defaultdict

def process_transactions(input_file, output_file):
    """Read CSV, validate rows, aggregate by customer, write report."""
    
    customers = defaultdict(float)
    skipped = 0
    processed = 0
    
    with open(input_file, 'r') as infile:
        reader = csv.DictReader(infile)
        
        for row_num, row in enumerate(reader, start=2):  # start=2 for header
            try:
                # Validate required fields exist
                if not row.get('customer_id') or not row.get('amount'):
                    print(f"Row {row_num}: Missing customer_id or amount, skipping", file=sys.stderr)
                    skipped += 1
                    continue
                
                customer_id = row['customer_id'].strip()
                amount = float(row['amount'])
                
                if not customer_id:
                    print(f"Row {row_num}: Empty customer_id, skipping", file=sys.stderr)
                    skipped += 1
                    continue
                
                customers[customer_id] += amount
                processed += 1
                
            except ValueError as e:
                print(f"Row {row_num}: Invalid amount '{row.get('amount')}', skipping", file=sys.stderr)
                skipped += 1
            except Exception as e:
                print(f"Row {row_num}: {e}, skipping", file=sys.stderr)
                skipped += 1
    
    # Write report sorted by customer
    with open(output_file, 'w', newline='') as outfile:
        writer = csv.writer(outfile)
        writer.writerow(['Customer ID', 'Total Amount'])
        
        for customer_id in sorted(customers.keys()):
            writer.writerow([customer_id, f"{customers[customer_id]:.2f}"])
    
    print(f"Processed: {processed} rows, Skipped: {skipped} rows")
    print(f"Report written to {output_file}")

if __name__ == '__main__':
    if len(sys.argv) != 3:
        print("Usage: python script.py <input.csv> <output.csv>")
        sys.exit(1)
    
    process_transactions(sys.argv[1], sys.argv[2])
```

```python
import csv
import sys
from collections import defaultdict

def parse_transaction(row, row_num):
    """Parse and validate a transaction row. Returns (customer_id, amount) or None."""
    try:
        if len(row) < 3:
            raise ValueError("Missing fields")
        customer_id = row[0].strip()
        amount_str = row[2].strip()
        
        if not customer_id:
            raise ValueError("Empty customer_id")
        
        amount = float(amount_str)
        if amount < 0:
            raise ValueError("Negative amount")
        
        return customer_id, amount
    except (ValueError, IndexError) as e:
        print(f"Skipped row {row_num}: {e} — {row}", file=sys.stderr)
        return None

def process_transactions(input_file, output_file):
    """Read CSV, validate, aggregate by customer, write report."""
    totals = defaultdict(float)
    skipped = 0
    processed = 0
    
    with open(input_file, 'r') as infile:
        reader = csv.reader(infile)
        next(reader, None)  # Skip header
        
        for row_num, row in enumerate(reader, start=2):
            result = parse_transaction(row, row_num)
            if result:
                customer_id, amount = result
                totals[customer_id] += amount
                processed += 1
            else:
                skipped += 1
    
    # Write report
    with open(output_file, 'w') as outfile:
        outfile.write("customer_id,total\n")
        for customer_id in sorted(totals.keys()):
            outfile.write(f"{customer_id},{totals[customer_id]:.2f}\n")
    
    print(f"Processed: {processed}, Skipped: {skipped}", file=sys.stderr)

if __name__ == '__main__':
    if len(sys.argv) != 3:
        print("Usage: python script.py <input.csv> <output.csv>", file=sys.stderr)
        sys.exit(1)
    
    process_transactions(sys.argv[1], sys.argv[2])
```

**Usage:** `python script.py transactions.csv report.csv`

**Handles:** Missing/empty fields, non-numeric amounts, negative values → logged to stderr, skipped, process continues. Streams rows (no memory spike on huge files). Aggregates into dict keyed by customer. Output sorted by customer ID.

```python
def validate_row(row, row_num):

        return False, None, None, f"Row {row_num}: {str(e)}"

def process_transactions(csv_path, report_path, error_log_path=None):

                if is_valid:

                    valid_count += 1
                else:

        # Write report
        with open(report_path, 'w', encoding='utf-8') as out:
            out.write("customer_id,total_amount\n")
            for cust_id in sorted(totals.keys()):
                out.write(f"{cust_id},{totals[cust_id]:.2f}\n")
        
        # Write error log if requested
        if error_log_path and errors:
            with open(error_log_path, 'w', encoding='utf-8') as err:
                for error in errors:
                    err.write(error + "\n")
        
        # Summary
        print(f"Processed {row_count} rows, {valid_count} valid, {len(errors)} errors")
        print(f"Report written to {report_path}")
        if error_log_path and errors:
            print(f"Errors logged to {error_log_path}")
        
        return True
    
    except FileNotFoundError:
        print(f"ERROR: File not found: {csv_path}", file=sys.stderr)
        return False
    except Exception as e:
        print(f"ERROR: {str(e)}", file=sys.stderr)
        return False

if __name__ == '__main__':

    error_log = sys.argv[3] if len(sys.argv) > 3 else None
```

## large-s3 / migration

> Write a database migration script that moves a users table from a single full_name column to first_name/last_name, backfills existing rows, and is safely re-runnable. Show the code.

### vanilla: 1047 answer tokens, 1394 billed, 110 lines

~~~
# Database Migration: full_name → first_name/last_name

Here's a safely re-runnable migration for PostgreSQL:

```sql
-- Migration: Split full_name into first_name and last_name
-- Safe to re-run: uses conditional logic and idempotent operations

BEGIN;

-- Step 1: Add new columns if they don't exist
DO $$
BEGIN
  IF NOT EXISTS (
    SELECT 1 FROM information_schema.columns 
    WHERE table_name = 'users' AND column_name = 'first_name'
  ) THEN
    ALTER TABLE users ADD COLUMN first_name VARCHAR(255);
  END IF;
  
  IF NOT EXISTS (
    SELECT 1 FROM information_schema.columns 
    WHERE table_name = 'users' AND column_name = 'last_name'
  ) THEN
    ALTER TABLE users ADD COLUMN last_name VARCHAR(255);
  END IF;
END $$;

-- Step 2: Backfill existing data only where new columns are still NULL
UPDATE users
SET 
  first_name = CASE 
    WHEN position(' ' in full_name) > 0 
    THEN substring(full_name, 1, position(' ' in full_name) - 1)
    ELSE full_name
  END,
  last_name = CASE 
    WHEN position(' ' in full_name) > 0 
    THEN substring(full_name, position(' ' in full_name) + 1)
    ELSE ''
  END
WHERE first_name IS NULL AND last_name IS NULL;

-- Step 3: Add NOT NULL constraints only if data is populated
-- (Do this after backfill to ensure no data loss on re-runs)
DO $$
BEGIN
  IF EXISTS (
    SELECT 1 FROM users WHERE full_name IS NOT NULL AND first_name IS NULL
  ) THEN
    RAISE EXCEPTION 'Cannot add NOT NULL constraint: backfill incomplete';
  END IF;
END $$;

ALTER TABLE users ALTER COLUMN first_name SET NOT NULL;
ALTER TABLE users ALTER COLUMN last_name SET NOT NULL;

-- Step 4: Drop old column (optional—comment out to keep for rollback safety)
-- ALTER TABLE users DROP COLUMN full_name;

COMMIT;
```

## For MySQL:

```sql
-- Check if columns exist and add them
SET @col_exists = (
  SELECT COUNT(*) 
  FROM INFORMATION_SCHEMA.COLUMNS 
  WHERE TABLE_NAME = 'users' AND COLUMN_NAME = 'first_name'
);

IF @col_exists = 0 THEN
  ALTER TABLE users ADD COLUMN first_name VARCHAR(255);
  ALTER TABLE users ADD COLUMN last_name VARCHAR(255);
END IF;

-- Backfill only NULL values
UPDATE users
SET 
  first_name = IF(
    LOCATE(' ', full_name) > 0,
    SUBSTRING(full_name, 1, LOCATE(' ', full_name) - 1),
    full_name
  ),
  last_name = IF(
    LOCATE(' ', full_name) > 0,
    SUBSTRING(full_name, LOCATE(' ', full_name) + 1),
    ''
  )
WHERE first_name IS NULL AND last_name IS NULL;

-- Add constraints after data is populated
ALTER TABLE users MODIFY first_name VARCHAR(255) NOT NULL;
ALTER TABLE users MODIFY last_name VARCHAR(255) NOT NULL;
```

## Key safety features:

| Feature | Why |
|---------|-----|
| **Conditional column adds** | Re-running won't fail on "column already exists" |
| **NULL checks in UPDATE** | Only backfills rows that need it; safe on retries |
| **Space-based splitting** | Simple heuristic: "John Doe" → "John" / "Doe" |
| **Constraints last** | Added after data is populated, so no conflicts |
| **Transaction wrapper** | All-or-nothing: either fully runs or rolls back |
| **Column drop commented** | Keep old column initially for safety; remove in separate deployment |

The splitting logic handles single names by putting the whole name in `first_name` and an empty string in `last_name`. Adjust as needed for your data (e.g., handle name suffixes like "Jr.", multiple middle names, etc.).

## Python/Alembic (Flask/Django Migration)

```python
"""Split users.full_name into first_name and last_name"""
from alembic import op
import sqlalchemy as sa

revision = '001_split_full_name'
down_revision = None
branch_labels = None
depends_on = None

def upgrade():
    # Add columns if not exist
    with op.batch_operations.Operations.context(op.get_context()) as batch_op:
        try:
            batch_op.add_column(sa.Column('first_name', sa.String(255), nullable=True))
        except sa.exc.OperationalError:
            pass
        
        try:
            batch_op.add_column(sa.Column('last_name', sa.String(255), nullable=True))
        except sa.exc.OperationalError:
            pass
    
    # Backfill
    connection = op.get_bind()
    connection.execute(sa.text("""
        UPDATE users 
        SET 
          first_name = TRIM(SUBSTRING_INDEX(full_name, ' ', 1)),
          last_name = TRIM(SUBSTRING_INDEX(full_name, ' ', -1))
        WHERE full_name IS NOT NULL 
          AND (first_name IS NULL OR last_name IS NULL)
    """))
    
    # Add constraints
    with op.batch_operations.Operations.context(op.get_context()) as batch_op:
        try:
            batch_op.alter_column('first_name', nullable=False)
        except sa.exc.OperationalError:
            pass
        
        try:
            batch_op.alter_column('last_name', nullable=False)
        except sa.exc.OperationalError:
            pass

def downgrade():
    with op.batch_operations.Operations.context(op.get_context()) as batch_op:
        # Reconstruct full_name before dropping columns
        connection = op.get_bind()
        connection.execute(sa.text("""
            UPDATE users 
            SET full_name = CONCAT(first_name, ' ', last_name)
            WHERE first_name IS NOT NULL AND last_name IS NOT NULL
        """))
        
        batch_op.drop_column('first_name')
        batch_op.drop_column('last_name')
```

---

## Key Safety Features

**Idempotency** — Checks columns exist before creating. `UPDATE` only backfills where target is NULL. Re-run safely.

**Data preservation** — Old `full_name` stays until manual `DROP` comment uncommented. Test backfill first.

**Transaction safety** — Wrap in explicit transaction in production:
```sql
BEGIN TRANSACTION;
  -- migration steps
COMMIT;
```

**Backfill logic** — Splits on first space (common). Adjust for edge cases (no space, trailing spaces, multiple spaces).

For multi-word names, use `TRIM()` + store remainder in `last_name`. Or prompt UX to fix ambiguous records post-migration.

```sql
-- Migration: Split full_name into first_name and last_name
-- Idempotent: safe to run multiple times

BEGIN;

-- Add new columns if they don't exist
ALTER TABLE users ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS first_name VARCHAR(255);
ALTER TABLE users ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS last_name VARCHAR(255);

-- Backfill new columns from full_name where they're still NULL
-- Splits on the first space: "John Doe" → first_name="John", last_name="Doe"
UPDATE users
SET 
  first_name = COALESCE(first_name, TRIM(SPLIT_PART(full_name, ' ', 1))),
  last_name = COALESCE(last_name, NULLIF(TRIM(SPLIT_PART(full_name, ' ', 2)), ''))
WHERE full_name IS NOT NULL 
  AND (first_name IS NULL OR last_name IS NULL);

-- Optional: drop old column after verifying new columns are populated correctly
-- ALTER TABLE users DROP COLUMN full_name;

COMMIT;
```

**Re-runnable because:**
- `IF NOT EXISTS` skips column adds on subsequent runs
- `COALESCE` skips rows that already have first/last names populated
- `WHERE ... IS NULL` stops the update if backfill already ran
- Transaction wraps everything atomically

**For MySQL**, swap `SPLIT_PART` → `SUBSTRING_INDEX(full_name, ' ', 1)` and `SUBSTRING_INDEX(full_name, ' ', -1)` for the last part.

**Deployment:** Test on a copy first. Keep the `DROP COLUMN` commented until you've verified the split looks right for edge cases (single names, suffixes like "Jr.", etc.).

```sql
-- Create new columns if they don't exist (idempotent)

WHERE first_name IS NULL AND full_name IS NOT NULL;

WHERE first_name IS NULL;
```

**Re-runnable:** `ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS` skips on repeat. `UPDATE` conditions (`WHERE first_name IS NULL`) mean second run is a no-op.

**Split logic:** "John Q Public" → first: "John Q", last: "Public" (last space wins). Single names → first only, last empty.

```sql
-- migration_001_split_names.sql
-- Idempotent: safe to run multiple times

BEGIN;

-- Track applied migrations (create if needed)
CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS schema_migrations (
  id SERIAL PRIMARY KEY,
  name VARCHAR(255) NOT NULL UNIQUE,
  applied_at TIMESTAMPTZ DEFAULT NOW()
);

-- Only run if not already applied
DO $$
BEGIN
  IF NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM schema_migrations WHERE name = 'split_names_001') THEN
    
    -- Add new columns if they don't exist
    ALTER TABLE users ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS first_name VARCHAR(255);
    ALTER TABLE users ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS last_name VARCHAR(255);
    
    -- Backfill from full_name
    UPDATE users 
    SET 
      first_name = COALESCE(NULLIF(TRIM(SPLIT_PART(full_name, ' ', 1)), ''), 'Unknown'),
      last_name = COALESCE(
        NULLIF(TRIM(SUBSTR(full_name, POSITION(' ' IN full_name) + 1)), ''),
        ''
      )
    WHERE full_name IS NOT NULL 
      AND (first_name IS NULL OR last_name IS NULL);
    
    -- Handle any remaining NULLs
    UPDATE users 
    SET 
      first_name = COALESCE(first_name, 'Unknown'),
      last_name = COALESCE(last_name, '')
    WHERE first_name IS NULL OR last_name IS NULL;
    
    -- Make columns NOT NULL
    ALTER TABLE users 
      ALTER COLUMN first_name SET NOT NULL,
      ALTER COLUMN last_name SET NOT NULL;
    
    -- Optional: drop old column only after verified
    -- ALTER TABLE users DROP COLUMN full_name;
    
    -- Mark migration as applied
    INSERT INTO schema_migrations (name) VALUES ('split_names_001');
    
  END IF;
END $$;

COMMIT;
```

**Key safety features:**

1. **Idempotent via `DO` block** — checks if already applied before running
2. **`IF NOT EXISTS`** — columns created only if missing
3. **`COALESCE` + `NULLIF`** — handles empty/NULL gracefully; defaults to 'Unknown' for first name
4. **Splits on first space** — "John Q Public" → "John" / "Q Public"
5. **Tracks state** — `schema_migrations` table prevents re-runs
6. **Two-phase backfill** — first pass updates based on full_name, second pass fixes any remaining NULLs
7. **Keep full_name initially** — commented-out DROP lets you verify before removal

**To use:**
```bash
psql -U user -d dbname -f migration_001_split_names.sql
```

Re-running does nothing (migration already marked applied). Safe to include in deployment scripts.

**Gotchas:** Name parsing is lossy—no perfect general case. This handles most Western names; adjust the split logic if needed (e.g., handle suffixes "Jr.", "PhD").
