### vanilla: 1695 answer tokens, 1852 billed, 137 lines

~~~
# Incident Postmortem: Connection Pool Exhaustion Due to Slow Downstream Dependency

## Executive Summary

On November 20, 2026, a four-hour outage (14:32–18:47 UTC) affected user-facing APIs caused by database connection pool exhaustion. A downstream payment service began responding slowly (~8 seconds per request vs. normal <100ms), causing API workers to hold connections open longer. Workers unable to get connections from the depleted pool queued indefinitely, triggering cascading timeouts and request pile-up.

**Impact:** 100% API error rate for 4 hours; 847 failed transactions; ~15,000 affected users  
**Root Cause:** Lack of timeout enforcement on downstream service calls, combined with absence of connection pool monitoring  
**Resolution:** Deployed timeout limits and connection pool circuit breaker

---

## Timeline

| Time (UTC) | Event |
|-----------|-------|
| 14:30 | Payment service (vendor-operated) experienced upstream database issue; response times degraded from 80ms to 8s |
| 14:32 | First API calls to payment service hit 8-second timeouts; workers retained open DB connections waiting for responses |
| 14:34 | Connection pool capacity (200 conns) exhausted; API workers queueing for available connections |
| 14:36 | Incoming requests begin timing out; error rate reaches 15% |
| 14:38 | On-call engineer paged; error rate reaches 95% |
| 14:42 | Root cause identified: payment service latency + no timeout on pool.getConnection() |
| 14:45 | Escalation to payment vendor support; they confirm DB issue under investigation |
| 15:12 | Decision made to circuit-break payment service calls to restore partial functionality |
| 15:18 | Circuit breaker patch deployed; API error rate drops to <5% (payment operations fail fast, non-payment operations resume) |
| 17:30 | Payment vendor resolves root database issue; service latency returns to normal |
| 17:45 | Circuit breaker disabled; full functionality restored and monitored |
| 18:47 | Declared resolved; error rate stable at <0.1% |

---

## Root Cause Analysis

**Primary Cause:** Downstream dependency (payment service) response time degradation (80ms → 8s) exposed missing timeout controls on database connection acquisition.

**Contributing Factors:**
1. **No timeout on connection pool acquisition** — Code called `pool.getConnection()` with implicit infinite timeout
2. **Lack of circuit breaker or bulkhead** — No isolation between payment service calls and core API traffic
3. **Insufficient connection pool monitoring** — Alerts did not exist for pool exhaustion or queue depth
4. **Assumption of fast downstream** — Code was written assuming sub-100ms payment service responses; no defensive checks in place

**Why It Cascaded:**
- Each slow payment request held a DB connection for 8+ seconds instead of <100ms
- Pool drained from 200 → 0 available connections within ~2 minutes
- Queued requests waiting for connections also acquired by timeout, holding connections longer
- No backpressure mechanism: API continued accepting requests it couldn't serve

---

## Impact Assessment

- **Duration:** 4 hours (14:32–18:47 UTC)
- **Affected Users:** ~15,000 (all users attempting to use APIs during window)
- **Failed Transactions:** 847 payment-related; ~12,000 read operations
- **Revenue Impact:** ~$8,400 (estimated failed orders)
- **Reputational:** 7 customer support escalations, 2 social media mentions

---

## Remediations

### Immediate Actions (Deployed 15:18 UTC)
1. **Circuit breaker on payment service** — Fail fast with error after 3 consecutive timeouts; allow 60-second recovery window
2. **Connection timeout enforcement** — Added 500ms timeout on `pool.getConnection()`; request fails rather than queuing indefinitely
3. **Request queue depth monitoring** — Added real-time dashboard; alert at 50+ queued requests

### Short-Term (Next Sprint)
1. **Add timeout to all external service calls**
   - Payment: 5 seconds (after circuit breaker)
   - Inventory: 2 seconds
   - Analytics: 1 second
   - Implement via standardized HTTP client wrapper

2. **Connection pool auto-tuning**
   - Increase max pool size from 200 → 400 (capacity buffer)
   - Add min pool size of 50 to maintain warm connections
   - Add connection validation on checkout to detect stale connections

3. **Enhanced alerting**
   - Alert when pool utilization exceeds 70%
   - Alert when connection wait time exceeds 100ms (p95)
   - Alert when downstream service response time exceeds 2s

### Long-Term (Q1 2027)
1. **Bulkhead pattern implementation** — Isolate payment service calls to dedicated thread pool (50 threads, separate from general API pool of 150)
2. **Observability expansion** — Add distributed tracing to map request flow and identify where connections held longest
3. **Load testing** — Quarterly chaos testing simulating slow/unavailable downstream services
4. **Dependency fallback strategy** — Evaluate async processing for non-critical payment operations; allow graceful degradation

---

## Action Items

| Task | Owner | Due Date | Priority |
|------|-------|----------|----------|
| Deploy connection timeout wrapper across all services | Backend | 2026-11-25 | P0 |
| Add pool exhaustion alerting to production monitoring | DevOps | 2026-11-24 | P0 |
| Increase connection pool capacity to 400 | Backend | 2026-11-26 | P1 |
| Design and implement bulkhead for payment service | Backend | 2026-12-05 | P1 |
| Conduct vendor SLA review with payment provider | Partnerships | 2026-11-29 | P2 |
| Add load test scenario: downstream latency degradation | QA | 2026-12-10 | P2 |

---

## Lessons Learned

**What Went Well:**
- On-call response was quick (4 min to page receipt)
- Vendor communication was responsive once escalated
- Partial recovery via circuit breaker allowed non-payment transactions to resume

**What Could Improve:**
- Pool exhaustion should have triggered alerts at 50% capacity, not discovered during outage
- Timeouts on external calls should be defensive standard, not retrofit after incidents
- Runbook for circuit breaker deployment was manual; should be automated/templated

---

## Appendix: Configuration Changes

**Connection Pool (after):**
```
maxPoolSize: 400
minPoolSize: 50
connectionTimeout: 500ms
idleTimeout: 15m
validationQuery: "SELECT 1"
```

**Payment Service Call (after):**
```
httpTimeout: 5s
circuitBreaker:
  failureThreshold: 3
  recoveryWindow: 60s
  halfOpenRequests: 10
```
~~~

# Incident Postmortem: Connection Pool Exhaustion Outage

**Incident ID:** INC-2026-1123-001  
**Date:** 2026-11-23  
**Duration:** 4 hours (14:30–18:30 UTC)  
**Severity:** SEV-1 (Complete service unavailability)  
**Author:** [On-call engineer]

---

## Summary

Service became unavailable for 4 hours due to database connection pool exhaustion. Root cause: downstream payment API began responding with 30+ second latencies, causing application threads to hold connections while waiting for responses. Pool depleted within 15 minutes; all subsequent requests rejected.

---

## Timeline

| Time | Event |
|------|-------|
| 14:30 | Payment API operator deployed infrastructure change. No notification sent to dependent services. |
| 14:33 | First customer complaints. Payment processing latency spike detected in logs (5s → 35s). |
| 14:38 | On-call notified via escalation. Initial hypothesis: database slow query. |
| 14:45 | Database CPU normal. Team pivots to investigating application behavior. |
| 14:52 | Error logs show "connection pool exhausted" (50/50 connections in use). |
| 15:00 | Root cause identified: payment API timeout. Application has no request timeout; threads block indefinitely. |
| 15:15 | Mitigation: deployed configuration rollout setting 10s timeout on payment API calls. |
| 15:30 | Service recovered. Pool connections returned to normal. |
| 18:30 | Full validation complete. No data loss. |

---

## Root Cause

Application connection pool (50 connections) used for all downstream calls, including to payment API. Payment API latency spike (5s → 30+s) caused:

1. HTTP client requests blocked on remote I/O with no timeout
2. Threads held connections for 30+ seconds each
3. Pool depleted within 15 minutes
4. New requests rejected immediately ("pool exhausted")
5. Service unavailable to all customers

**Why not caught earlier:** No timeout set on payment API client. No monitoring alert on connection pool utilization. Tight coupling between pool and external dependency.

---

## Impact

- **Users:** 100% of platform unavailable for 4 hours
- **Transactions:** ~2,400 payment requests dropped/failed
- **Revenue:** ~$180k estimated impact
- **Data loss:** None (requests failed before reaching database)

---

## Remediations

### Immediate (Completed)
- ✅ Deployed 10s timeout on all external HTTP calls
- ✅ Added alert on connection pool utilization >80%
- ✅ Rotated on-call; added incident channel notification to ops

### Short-term (1–2 weeks)
- [ ] Separate connection pools by dependency (payment API, user service, analytics)
- [ ] Implement circuit breaker for payment API (fail fast after 3 consecutive timeouts)
- [ ] Add request-level timeout enforcement in HTTP middleware
- [ ] Document timeout strategy for all external dependencies

### Long-term (1–2 months)
- [ ] Migrate to async HTTP client (reduce thread-per-request model)
- [ ] Build service mesh observability (latency dashboards by endpoint)
- [ ] Add chaos testing: inject artificial latency into payment API, verify graceful degradation
- [ ] Establish SLA agreements with payment API operator; require outage notifications

---

## Action Items

| Owner | Task | Due Date |
|-------|------|----------|
| Eng Lead | Approve separate pool architecture | 2026-11-30 |
| Backend Team | Implement circuit breaker | 2026-12-07 |
| Platform Team | Deploy chaos test for latency injection | 2026-12-21 |
| Ops | Setup escalation to payment API team | 2026-11-25 |

---

## Lessons Learned

1. **Timeouts are non-negotiable.** Every external call needs an explicit timeout. Defaults are not safe.
2. **Pool isolation matters.** Shared resource pools amplify blast radius of single bad dependency.
3. **Alerting on resource exhaustion > alerting on symptoms.** We caught "errors" late; we should have alerted on "pool utilization" first.
4. **Synchronous I/O is fragile.** Thread-per-request model leaves us vulnerable to slow downstreams.
~~~

### ponytail: 861 answer tokens, 1097 billed, 63 lines

~~~
# Incident Postmortem: Connection Pool Exhaustion Outage

**Date:** 2026-11-20  
**Duration:** 4h 12m (14:35–18:47 UTC)  
**Impact:** API responses timing out for all users; ~2.3M requests failed  
**Severity:** P1

---

## Timeline

| Time | Event |
|------|-------|
| 14:35 | Downstream payment service (`payments-svc`) begins responding slowly (~8s latency, normally 200ms). Our monitoring doesn't alert on it. |
| 14:47 | First error spike in logs: "connection pool exhausted" on the main API service. |
| 14:51 | Oncall page fires for P1 latency. |
| 15:02 | Team assumes database under load, restarts DB connections. No effect. |
| 15:18 | Grep reveals all hung connections are waiting on `payments-svc` calls. |
| 15:22 | Cut `payments-svc` traffic by routing payment requests to a stub handler. API recovers, latency drops to normal. |
| 16:10 | Payments team fixes their downstream issue (quota exhaustion on Stripe API). Reconnect payments service. |
| 18:47 | Full recovery verified, monitoring confirms stable. |

---

## Root Cause

The connection pool in our HTTP client was sized for normal downstream latency (~200ms). When `payments-svc` began responding in 8s, each request held a connection 40x longer. By 14:47, the pool of 50 connections was completely saturated—new requests queued indefinitely, causing cascading timeouts. The service had no timeout or circuit breaker on outbound calls, so hung requests never released their connection.

**Trigger:** Payments team hit a Stripe rate limit at 14:35. They didn't surface this upstream; we learned it only after drilling into logs.

---

## Remediations

### Immediate (Done)
1. **HTTP call timeout** (`30s → 5s`) on payments-svc calls. Prevents connections from being held indefinitely.
2. **Circuit breaker** on payments-svc. After 5 consecutive failures, fail fast for 30s instead of piling onto the pool.

### Short-term (This week)
3. **Downstream latency alert:** Page if any single dependency's p99 latency exceeds 2s for 2min.
4. **Connection pool instrumentation:** Log pool usage (active/waiting/total) every 10s. Alert if >80% full.

### Long-term (Next sprint)
5. **Dependency isolation:** Move payments calls to a separate worker pool with its own connection budget (10 conns), so a slow payment service never starves the main request pool.
6. **Explicit SLO:** Define max acceptable latency for each dependency call; fail fast if exceeded. Currently we inherit whatever latency happens.

---

## What Went Well
- Team correctly identified the connection pool bottleneck within 50 minutes (not chasing the database).
- Stub routing unblocked users immediately while payments team fixed their issue.

---

## What We'll Do Differently
- **Alert on dependency latency, not just our latency.** We didn't see the payments-svc slowdown until it had already cascaded to us.
- **Timeout every blocking call.** The 8-second hangs were the attack surface; a 5-second timeout would have failed fast and preserved connections.

---

**Owner:** Platform team  
**Review:** 2026-11-27  
**Jira Link:** `INC-4521`

# Incident Postmortem: Connection Pool Exhaustion

**Date:** 2026-11-22  
**Duration:** 4h 15m (14:30–18:45 UTC)  
**Severity:** SEV-1 (complete service degradation)  
**Responders:** Backend on-call, Platform team

---

## Timeline

| Time | Event |
|------|-------|
| 14:30 | Alerts fire: API p99 latency 45s, error rate climbing. Requests timing out. |
| 14:35 | On-call pages Platform. Logs show `connection pool exhausted` from database layer. |
| 14:40 | Initial hypothesis: database overload. DB metrics normal—CPU 12%, connections 85/100. Problem downstream. |
| 15:00 | Traced to calls to `auth-service` (internal dependency). Auth-service responding but slow: 8–12s per request vs. normal 50ms. |
| 15:15 | Auth-service logs show its own pool exhausted waiting for Redis. Redis at capacity, slow eviction. |
| 15:30 | Cascading failure identified: auth-service pools full → main service pools full → user requests hang. |
| 16:00 | Mitigation: reduced main service pool size to 20 (from 50) to fail fast instead of queue. Added circuit breaker on auth-service calls. Rolled out. |
| 16:15 | Error rate drops to 2%, users see fast failures instead of timeouts. Services recover. |
| 17:00 | Restarted auth-service to clear hung connections. Confirmed Redis was misconfigured: eviction policy set to `no-eviction` instead of `allkeys-lru`. |
| 17:30 | Fixed Redis eviction policy. Load test auth-service. Confirmed it handles burst traffic without saturating pools. |
| 18:45 | Full recovery. All services green. No data loss. |

---

## Root Cause

**Primary:** Auth-service was CPU-bound on requests to Redis (misconfigured to never evict). Requests queued in Redis connection pool instead of failing fast. Auth-service worker threads blocked indefinitely. Pool exhausted.

**Secondary:** Main service's connection pool was sized for normal auth-service latency (50ms). When auth-service latencies jumped to 8–12s, pools filled instantly. No timeout or circuit breaker to shed load.

**Tertiary:** Redis `no-eviction` policy allowed hot keys to consume all memory. Subsequent requests waited for memory to free, causing latency spike. Monitoring did not alert on Redis memory or eviction policy drift.

---

## Impact

- **Users:** 100% error rate for 4h 15m. Fallback auth (if any) not deployed.
- **Internal:** 12 customers escalated. 3 on-call incidents for downstream systems.
- **Data:** None lost. Requests dropped cleanly, not silently stored.

---

## Remediations

### Immediate (Completed)

1. **Circuit breaker on auth-service calls** — fail fast after 3 consecutive failures or 500ms timeout. Deployed 16:00.
2. **Connection pool right-sizing** — main service pool: 20 → 50 (more lenient). Auth-service: add timeout and queue depth limits. Deployed 16:30.
3. **Redis eviction policy** — fixed `no-eviction` → `allkeys-lru`. Deployed 17:00.

### Short-term (Next Sprint)

1. **Monitoring gaps**
   - Alert on Redis memory usage >80%.
   - Alert on Redis eviction rate >100/min.
   - Alert on connection pool utilization >75%.

2. **Bulkhead isolation** — auth-service gets its own replica of frequently accessed keys in local cache (5m TTL). Reduces Redis dependency.

3. **Load testing** — baseline auth-service with realistic TPS and connection pool depth. Run monthly.

### Long-term (Q1)

1. **Service mesh observability** — trace end-to-end latency and timeouts per dependency. Surface pool starvation before user impact.
2. **Graceful degradation** — if auth-service slow, serve requests with cached auth tokens (stale but working) instead of failing.
3. **Configuration drift detection** — config audit on critical services (Redis, pools, timeouts) every 24h. Alert on change.

---

## Action Items

| Task | Owner | Due |
|------|-------|-----|
| Deploy circuit breaker v2 (retries, backoff) | Backend | 2026-11-28 |
| Add Redis/pool alerts | Platform | 2026-11-25 |
| Auth-service load test & right-size pools | Auth team | 2026-11-30 |
| Design local cache layer for auth | Arch review | 2026-12-05 |
| Config drift detection design doc | Platform | 2026-12-05 |

---

## Lessons

1. **Connection pools are circuit breakers.** Right-size for the latency you expect downstream, not the latency you hope for.
2. **Configuration changes (Redis eviction) are incidents waiting to happen.** Version, review, and audit critical config.
3. **Cascading failure is the default.** Add timeouts and circuit breakers at every service boundary.
~~~

# Incident Postmortem: Connection Pool Exhaustion — 4h Outage

**Date:** 2026-11-22  
**Duration:** 14:35–18:47 UTC  
**Severity:** SEV-2 (service degradation)  
**Services:** API backend, checkout flow  
**Authors:** [On-call], [Platform team]

---

## Impact

- API latency increased from 150ms median to >30s (p99)
- Checkout success rate dropped from 99.2% to 76% over 4 hours
- ~18k failed transactions; ~$340k estimated revenue impact
- Downstream payment processor received ~8k duplicate/retry requests

---

## Timeline

| Time | Event |
|------|-------|
| 14:35 | Payment processor begins returning 500 errors; latency degrades from 180ms to 2.5s |
| 14:52 | Alert fires: API error rate >5%. On-call investigates; assumes payment-side issue. Opens ticket with vendor. |
| 15:04 | Vendor responds: "No incident on our end; check your side." |
| 15:18 | Metrics reviewed: connection pool at 100% utilization; requests queuing indefinitely. Database connections healthy. |
| 15:31 | Root cause identified: payment processor SDK has open connection leak in error path; connections not released on timeout. |
| 15:47 | **Mitigation 1:** Restart API fleet. Pool recovers; latency drops to 400ms but doesn't recover to baseline. |
| 16:12 | Requests still timing out. Traced: payment processor still slow (now 3–8s), pool refilling. Status page updated. |
| 16:28 | Restarted again; connections leak faster this time. Added circuit breaker (10 consecutive timeouts = 60s open). |
| 17:01 | Circuit breaker engaged at 16:58; API latency normalized to 180ms baseline. Failed requests now rejected cleanly with 503. |
| 17:30 | Payment processor recovers to 200ms latency. Circuit breaker still protecting; no pool pressure. |
| 17:52 | Automated circuit breaker half-open state; 1% traffic allowed. Payment processor healthy. Full traffic restored. |
| 18:47 | Error rate <0.1%; all alerts cleared. |

---

## Root Cause

The payment processor SDK's error handling path didn't release connection pool resources when requests timed out. When the downstream payment API began experiencing latency (triggered by their own database query timeout, unrelated), SDK errors accumulated, connections queued indefinitely without being returned, and the pool hit its 100-connection limit.

**Contributing factors:**
- Payment SDK version 2.1.4 had the leak; we were on that version
- No explicit timeout or circuit breaker between API and payment processor
- Pool instrumentation (metrics) reported utilization but not leak rate
- Alerting didn't trigger on sustained pool exhaustion

---

## Remediations

**Immediate (completed):**
1. ✅ Upgraded payment SDK to 2.3.1 (leak fixed in 2.3.0)
2. ✅ Deployed circuit breaker (10-strike, 60s open window) on payment processor calls
3. ✅ Reduced connection pool timeout from 30s to 5s to fail fast
4. ✅ Restarted API fleet; validated pool behavior

**Short-term (1–2 weeks):**
- [ ] Add alert: connection pool utilization >80% for >5 min
- [ ] Add metric: connections-released vs. connections-acquired per downstream service
- [ ] Load-test payment processor SDK upgrades before deploy
- [ ] Document SDK update cadence (currently ad-hoc)

**Long-term (1–2 months):**
- [ ] Implement per-service pool isolation (payment processor gets dedicated pool, isolated from checkout)
- [ ] Add adaptive timeout logic: if p95 latency >2s, shorten timeout or fail fast
- [ ] Canary payment SDK upgrades in staging env for 2 weeks before prod
- [ ] Automate SDK dependency scanning for CVEs and known leak issues

---

## What Went Well

- Vendor response time (17 min) was clear and correct
- Circuit breaker deployment was fast (20 min from decision to prod)
- Metrics correctly identified the pool as the chokepoint; avoided false debugging paths

---

## What Didn't

- No baseline for connection leak detection; we waited for 100% exhaustion to notice
- SDK upgrade path was manual; payment processor was low-priority dependency in our tracking
- No timeout or bulkhead between services; a downstream slow dep could take down the whole API layer

---

## Owners

- **SDK upgrades & circuit breaker:** Platform team by 2026-12-01
- **Pool isolation / timeout logic:** API team by 2026-12-15
- **Alerting / metrics:** Observability team by 2026-11-30

---

**Next review:** 2026-12-20 (verify long-term fixes and canary results)

### vanilla: 1136 answer tokens, 1922 billed, 120 lines

~~~
I'll show you a clean state machine implementation with enforced legal transitions:

```python
class InvalidTransitionError(Exception):
    """Raised when an invalid state transition is attempted."""
    pass


class OrderStateMachine:
    """Order state machine with enforced legal transitions."""
    
    # Define valid transitions: from state → list of allowed next states
    VALID_TRANSITIONS = {
        'created': {'paid', 'cancelled'},
        'paid': {'shipped', 'refunded'},
        'shipped': {'delivered', 'refunded'},
        'delivered': {'refunded'},
        'cancelled': set(),
        'refunded': set()
    }
    
    ALL_STATES = {'created', 'paid', 'shipped', 'delivered', 'cancelled', 'refunded'}
    
    def __init__(self):
        self.state = 'created'
        self.history = ['created']
    
    def can_transition_to(self, next_state):
        """Check if a transition is valid without changing state."""
        if next_state not in self.ALL_STATES:
            return False
        return next_state in self.VALID_TRANSITIONS[self.state]
    
    def transition(self, next_state):
        """Attempt to transition to the next state."""
        if not self.can_transition_to(next_state):
            raise InvalidTransitionError(
                f"Cannot transition from '{self.state}' to '{next_state}'. "
                f"Valid transitions: {self.VALID_TRANSITIONS[self.state]}"
            )
        self.state = next_state
        self.history.append(next_state)
        return self.state
    
    def get_current_state(self):
        """Get the current state."""
        return self.state
    
    def get_valid_transitions(self):
        """Get all valid next states from current state."""
        return self.VALID_TRANSITIONS[self.state]
    
    def is_terminal(self):
        """Check if current state is terminal (no further transitions possible)."""
        return len(self.VALID_TRANSITIONS[self.state]) == 0


# Example usage
if __name__ == '__main__':
    order = OrderStateMachine()
    
    print(f"Initial state: {order.get_current_state()}")
    print(f"Valid transitions: {order.get_valid_transitions()}\n")
    
    # Valid transitions
    order.transition('paid')
    print(f"After payment: {order.get_current_state()}")
    print(f"Valid transitions: {order.get_valid_transitions()}\n")
    
    order.transition('shipped')
    print(f"After shipment: {order.get_current_state()}")
    print(f"Valid transitions: {order.get_valid_transitions()}\n")
    
    order.transition('delivered')
    print(f"After delivery: {order.get_current_state()}")
    print(f"Valid transitions: {order.get_valid_transitions()}\n")
    
    # Invalid transition attempt
    try:
        order.transition('paid')  # Can't go back to paid from delivered
    except InvalidTransitionError as e:
        print(f"Error: {e}\n")
    
    # Terminal state
    order.transition('refunded')
    print(f"After refund: {order.get_current_state()}")
    print(f"Is terminal: {order.is_terminal()}")
    print(f"State history: {order.history}")
```

**Output:**
```
Initial state: created
Valid transitions: {'paid', 'cancelled'}

After payment: paid
Valid transitions: {'shipped', 'refunded'}

After shipment: shipped
Valid transitions: {'delivered', 'refunded'}

After delivery: delivered
Valid transitions: {'refunded'}

Error: Cannot transition from 'delivered' to 'paid'. Valid transitions: {'refunded'}

After refund: refunded
Is terminal: True
State history: ['created', 'paid', 'shipped', 'delivered', 'refunded']
```

**Key features:**

1. **Explicit transitions map** — only listed transitions are allowed
2. **Exception on invalid transitions** — raises `InvalidTransitionError` with clear error message
3. **Query methods** — `can_transition_to()` and `get_valid_transitions()` let you check state before acting
4. **History tracking** — maintains the path through states
5. **Terminal state detection** — `is_terminal()` identifies states with no outgoing transitions (cancelled, refunded)

The transition logic is straightforward: any state not in the VALID_TRANSITIONS entry for the current state is rejected.
~~~

Order state machine with legal transitions enforced:

```python
from enum import Enum

class OrderState(Enum):
    CREATED = "created"
    PAID = "paid"
    SHIPPED = "shipped"
    DELIVERED = "delivered"
    CANCELLED = "cancelled"
    REFUNDED = "refunded"

class OrderStateMachine:
    # Legal transitions: state → set of allowed next states
    TRANSITIONS = {
        OrderState.CREATED: {OrderState.PAID, OrderState.CANCELLED},
        OrderState.PAID: {OrderState.SHIPPED, OrderState.REFUNDED, OrderState.CANCELLED},
        OrderState.SHIPPED: {OrderState.DELIVERED},
        OrderState.DELIVERED: {OrderState.REFUNDED},
        OrderState.CANCELLED: set(),
        OrderState.REFUNDED: set(),
    }
    
    def __init__(self):
        self.state = OrderState.CREATED
    
    def transition(self, new_state: OrderState) -> None:
        if new_state not in self.TRANSITIONS[self.state]:
            raise ValueError(
                f"Invalid transition: {self.state.value} → {new_state.value}"
            )
        self.state = new_state
    
    def can_transition(self, new_state: OrderState) -> bool:
        return new_state in self.TRANSITIONS[self.state]
    
    def __repr__(self):
        return f"Order({self.state.value})"

# Examples
order = OrderStateMachine()
print(order)  # Order(created)

order.transition(OrderState.PAID)
print(order)  # Order(paid)

order.transition(OrderState.SHIPPED)
print(order)  # Order(shipped)

order.transition(OrderState.DELIVERED)
print(order)  # Order(delivered)

order.transition(OrderState.REFUNDED)
print(order)  # Order(refunded)

# Invalid transition
try:
    order.transition(OrderState.PAID)
except ValueError as e:
    print(f"Error: {e}")  # Error: Invalid transition: refunded → paid
```

**Transitions enforced:**
- `created` → `paid`, `cancelled`
- `paid` → `shipped`, `refunded`, `cancelled`
- `shipped` → `delivered`
- `delivered` → `refunded`
- `cancelled`, `refunded` → (terminal, no transitions)

Invalid transitions raise `ValueError`. Check `can_transition()` before attempting, or catch exception.

```python
from enum import Enum
from typing import Set


class OrderState(Enum):
    CREATED = "created"
    PAID = "paid"
    SHIPPED = "shipped"
    DELIVERED = "delivered"
    CANCELLED = "cancelled"
    REFUNDED = "refunded"


class OrderStateMachine:
    # Define legal transitions: state -> set of valid next states
    TRANSITIONS = {
        OrderState.CREATED: {OrderState.PAID, OrderState.CANCELLED},
        OrderState.PAID: {OrderState.SHIPPED, OrderState.REFUNDED, OrderState.CANCELLED},
        OrderState.SHIPPED: {OrderState.DELIVERED, OrderState.REFUNDED},
        OrderState.DELIVERED: {OrderState.REFUNDED},
        OrderState.CANCELLED: set(),
        OrderState.REFUNDED: set(),
    }

    def __init__(self):
        self.state = OrderState.CREATED

    def transition(self, next_state: OrderState) -> None:
        """Move to next state if transition is legal, raise ValueError otherwise."""
        if next_state not in self.TRANSITIONS[self.state]:
            raise ValueError(
                f"Invalid transition from {self.state.value} to {next_state.value}. "
                f"Valid transitions: {', '.join(s.value for s in self.TRANSITIONS[self.state]) or 'none (terminal state)'}"
            )
        self.state = next_state

    def can_transition_to(self, next_state: OrderState) -> bool:
        """Check if transition is legal without changing state."""
        return next_state in self.TRANSITIONS[self.state]


# Example usage and tests
if __name__ == "__main__":
    order = OrderStateMachine()
    print(f"Initial state: {order.state.value}")

    # Happy path: created → paid → shipped → delivered
    order.transition(OrderState.PAID)
    print(f"After payment: {order.state.value}")

    order.transition(OrderState.SHIPPED)
    print(f"After shipment: {order.state.value}")

    order.transition(OrderState.DELIVERED)
    print(f"After delivery: {order.state.value}")

    order.transition(OrderState.REFUNDED)
    print(f"After refund: {order.state.value}")

    # Test invalid transition
    print("\nTesting invalid transition:")
    try:
        order.transition(OrderState.PAID)
    except ValueError as e:
        print(f"Caught: {e}")

    # Test cancelled path
    print("\nCancelled order flow:")
    order2 = OrderStateMachine()
    order2.transition(OrderState.CANCELLED)
    print(f"Cancelled at: {order2.state.value}")

    try:
        order2.transition(OrderState.PAID)
    except ValueError as e:
        print(f"Cannot transition from terminal state: {e}")

    # Test partial flow with refund
    print("\nPartial flow with refund:")
    order3 = OrderStateMachine()
    order3.transition(OrderState.PAID)
    order3.transition(OrderState.REFUNDED)
    print(f"Refunded at: {order3.state.value}")

    # Verify check
    print(f"\nCan go from SHIPPED to CANCELLED? {OrderStateMachine().TRANSITIONS[OrderState.SHIPPED]}")
```

**Legal transitions:**
- `created` → `paid`, `cancelled`
- `paid` → `shipped`, `refunded`, `cancelled`
- `shipped` → `delivered`, `refunded`
- `delivered` → `refunded`
- `cancelled` → (terminal, no transitions)
- `refunded` → (terminal, no transitions)

Invalid transitions (like `paid` → `delivered` or `cancelled` → `shipped`) raise `ValueError` with the valid options. Use `.can_transition_to()` to check before acting, or catch the exception if you want fail-fast behavior.

```python
class OrderState:
    CREATED = "created"
    PAID = "paid"
    SHIPPED = "shipped"
    DELIVERED = "delivered"
    CANCELLED = "cancelled"
    REFUNDED = "refunded"

class Order:
    VALID_TRANSITIONS = {
        OrderState.CREATED: {OrderState.PAID, OrderState.CANCELLED},
        OrderState.PAID: {OrderState.SHIPPED, OrderState.REFUNDED},
        OrderState.SHIPPED: {OrderState.DELIVERED, OrderState.CANCELLED},
        OrderState.DELIVERED: set(),
        OrderState.CANCELLED: set(),
        OrderState.REFUNDED: set(),
    }
    
    def __init__(self, order_id):
        self.order_id = order_id
        self.state = OrderState.CREATED
    
    def transition(self, new_state):
        if new_state not in self.VALID_TRANSITIONS[self.state]:
            raise ValueError(
                f"Cannot transition order {self.order_id} from {self.state} to {new_state}"
            )
        self.state = new_state
    
    def __repr__(self):
        return f"Order({self.order_id}, state={self.state})"


# Valid flows
order = Order("ORD-001")
order.transition(OrderState.PAID)       # created → paid ✓
order.transition(OrderState.SHIPPED)    # paid → shipped ✓
order.transition(OrderState.DELIVERED)  # shipped → delivered ✓

# Invalid
order.transition(OrderState.CANCELLED)  # delivered → cancelled ✗ → ValueError
```

State machine: each state maps to legal next states. `transition()` checks membership, raises on invalid path. Terminal states (delivered, cancelled, refunded) have empty transition sets.

### chisle-C: 747 answer tokens, 1307 billed, 83 lines

~~~
```python
class OrderStateMachine:
    TRANSITIONS = {
        'created': {'paid', 'cancelled'},
        'paid': {'shipped', 'refunded'},
        'shipped': {'delivered'},
        'delivered': set(),
        'cancelled': set(),
        'refunded': set(),
    }
    
    def __init__(self):
        self.state = 'created'
    
    def transition(self, new_state):
        if new_state not in self.TRANSITIONS[self.state]:
            raise ValueError(f"Cannot transition from '{self.state}' to '{new_state}'")
        self.state = new_state
        return self.state
    
    def can_transition(self, new_state):
        return new_state in self.TRANSITIONS[self.state]


# Usage
order = OrderStateMachine()

order.transition('paid')           # ✓ created → paid
order.transition('shipped')        # ✓ paid → shipped
order.transition('delivered')      # ✓ shipped → delivered

try:
    order.transition('refunded')   # ✗ delivered → refunded (illegal)
except ValueError as e:
    print(e)  # "Cannot transition from 'delivered' to 'refunded'"

# Check before transitioning
order2 = OrderStateMachine()
order2.can_transition('shipped')   # False
order2.can_transition('paid')      # True
```

**TypeScript version:**

```typescript
type OrderState = 'created' | 'paid' | 'shipped' | 'delivered' | 'cancelled' | 'refunded';

class OrderStateMachine {
  private static readonly TRANSITIONS: Record<OrderState, Set<OrderState>> = {
    'created': new Set(['paid', 'cancelled']),
    'paid': new Set(['shipped', 'refunded']),
    'shipped': new Set(['delivered']),
    'delivered': new Set(),
    'cancelled': new Set(),
    'refunded': new Set(),
  };
  
  private state: OrderState = 'created';
  
  transition(newState: OrderState): OrderState {
    if (!OrderStateMachine.TRANSITIONS[this.state].has(newState)) {
      throw new Error(`Cannot transition from '${this.state}' to '${newState}'`);
    }
    this.state = newState;
    return this.state;
  }
  
  canTransition(newState: OrderState): boolean {
    return OrderStateMachine.TRANSITIONS[this.state].has(newState);
  }
  
  getState(): OrderState {
    return this.state;
  }
}
```

**Legal paths:**
- `created` → `paid` → `shipped` → `delivered` (happy path)
- `created` → `paid` → `refunded` (refund after payment)
- `created` → `cancelled` (early cancellation)

**Illegal transitions:** Terminal states block all transitions; `created` can't reach `shipped` directly; `delivered` can't be refunded.
~~~
